coordinator11:15 PM

You are researching source material for a dense, fact-packed song (think Hamilton-style lyrical density, or "I'm Upping My P(doom)" with its rapid-fire in-jokes) about infant science. The song's spine is the Netflix documentary series Babies (2020, Nutopia, 12 episodes in two parts) and the scientific literature it draws from. Your deliverable is a fact sheet, not lyrics.

Write the fact sheet to /Users/jalex/projects/clawdpop/songs/baby-facts/FACTS.md (the directory exists; use Bash heredocs or the Write tool).

What to gather, using WebSearch and WebFetch:

  1. Episode-by-episode: for each of the 12 episodes (Part 1: Love, First Food, Crawling, First Words, Sleep, First Steps; Part 2: What Babies Know, Movement, Senses, Relationships, Nature and Nurture, Toddlers), list the scientists featured (name, institution), the specific study shown, and the headline finding with a concrete number where one exists. Known leads to verify and expand: Ruth Feldman (oxytocin in fathers / same-sex dads), Ed Tronick (still-face), Michelle Lampl at Emory (growth spurts, babies grow in bursts), Eric Walle at UC Merced (walkers have bigger vocabularies than crawlers), Rebecca Spencer at UMass (naps consolidate memory), Libby Cowgill at Missouri (bone shape and locomotion). Likely also featured somewhere: Karen Adolph (NYU, infant locomotion), Patricia Kuhl (Washington, phoneme narrowing), Kiley Hamlin (UBC, helper/hinderer morality), Rebecca Saxe (MIT, awake infant fMRI), Maria Gloria Dominguez-Bello (microbiome), Katie Hinde (milk composition), Michael Georgieff (iron), Charles Nelson (Bucharest Early Intervention), Mark Blumberg (Iowa, sleep twitches), Elizabeth Spelke, Susan Hespos, Fei Xu, Michael Tomasello, Felix Warneken, Dan Fessler, Celeste Kidd. Confirm who actually appears and who does not. Good sources: university press releases from Feb and June 2020 ("featured in Netflix Babies"), the Nutopia site, the Netflix episode pages, reviews on calmfamily.org and the Notre Dame student blog (sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke), IMDb full credits for the series.
  2. The underlying literature: for the 25 to 40 most striking findings, give the primary paper (authors, year, journal) and the specific numbers lyricists love: e.g. Adolph's toddlers take about 2,368 steps and fall 17 times per hour; Kuhl's 6-to-12-month window for losing non-native phoneme discrimination; Hamlin's 6-month-olds preferring the helper; Lampl's growth spurts of up to 1 cm in 24 hours after up to 63 days of no growth; Tronick's still-face timeline; Spencer's nap-and-memory effect sizes; infant sleep architecture (50 percent REM in newborns vs about 20 percent in adults, 16 to 18 hours of sleep a day); the number of neurons/synapses formed per second in infancy; the newborn's visual acuity (about 20/400) and focal distance (8 to 12 inches); the Moro, rooting, stepping, grasp reflexes; the 300-odd bones at birth fusing to 206; babies' taste buds and the amniotic-fluid flavor exposure studies (Mennella); the microbiome seeding by delivery mode; colostrum and the sex-specific milk composition in rhesus macaques (Hinde); infant-directed speech / "parentese" effects; the word gap and its criticisms; crying peaks around 6 weeks (the PURPLE crying curve) and the 2 to 3 hours a day of normal crying; the Wonder Weeks claims and their lack of replication; sleep regressions at 4, 8, 12 months and the evidence for them; the 4-month sleep architecture change; separation anxiety onset around 8 months; object permanence; the mirror test around 18 months; pointing at 12 months; first words around 12 months and the vocabulary spurt around 18 months; statistical learning (Saffran 1996, 8-month-olds segmenting words after 2 minutes); the Goldilocks effect (Kidd); numerosity in infants (Xu and Spelke, 6-month-olds discriminating 8 vs 16); babies' preference for faces and for their mother's voice/smell; the "babies are born with expectations" physics studies (Baillargeon).
  3. Flag reliability. Mark each fact as solid, contested, or pop-science-only. Where a well-known claim did not replicate or is overstated (Wonder Weeks, the 30-million-word gap, "babies learn language from Baby Einstein"), say so, since the song can joke about that.

Format: Markdown. Section per episode, then a "Literature greatest hits" section organized by theme (sleep, crying, feeding, movement, language, cognition, social, senses, growth), then a final "Hooks" section: the 30 most quotable, numeric, surprising facts in one line each, each with a source. Keep each fact to one or two lines with the number and the citation inline. Include URLs. Do not write lyrics. Do not pad. Aim for a dense 400 to 700 line file.

Report back with a 15-line summary of the most lyrically useful findings and anything you could not verify.

prompt

You are researching primary scientific literature for a fact sheet that will feed a dense, fact-packed song about infant science (think Hamilton-style lyrical density). Deliverable: return your findings as your final message in Markdown (do NOT write any files). Use WebSearch and WebFetch (load them via ToolSearch "select:WebSearch,WebFetch" first).

Themes you own: SLEEP, CRYING, FEEDING, GROWTH, SENSES. For each fact, give: one or two lines, the specific number(s), the primary paper (first author, year, journal) or best authoritative source, a URL, and a reliability flag: [solid] / [contested] / [pop-science-only]. Prefer primary papers and university press releases over blogs. Verify numbers; do not invent them. If you cannot verify a number, say so explicitly.

Items to verify and quantify (add others you find striking):

SLEEP

  • Newborn sleep: hours per day (16-18?), percent REM/active sleep in newborns (~50%) vs adults (~20-25%), when REM proportion drops; Roffwarg, Muzio & Dement 1966 Science ontogenetic hypothesis.
  • Rebecca Spencer (UMass Amherst): naps consolidate memory in preschoolers (Kurdziel, Duclos & Spencer 2013 PNAS) - effect sizes, % forgetting without nap; her later infant work; she appeared in Netflix "Babies" Sleep episode - what did she say / what study was filmed (press release UMass Feb 2020).
  • Mark Blumberg (Iowa): sleep twitches in infants (REM twitches as sensorimotor training) - key paper and finding; was he in the Netflix series?
  • 4-month sleep architecture change (emergence of adult-like sleep stages, circadian consolidation); "sleep regressions" at 4/8/12 months - what does the evidence actually say? (Is there peer-reviewed evidence for discrete regressions? Hysing, Mindell, Galland reference ranges.)
  • Infant sleep variability: Galland et al. 2012 Sleep Medicine Reviews normative values (e.g., 0-2 months total sleep ~14.6 h, range); Paavonen/ Hysing infant night wakings % at 6/12 months; Pennestri 2018 Pediatrics "sleeping through the night" (27-57% of 6-12-month-olds don't).
  • Sleep and synaptic pruning/ brain development claims.

CRYING

  • Normal crying curve: peaks ~6 weeks at ~2-3 h/day, declines by 3-4 months (Brazelton 1962 Pediatrics; Barr's "Period of PURPLE Crying"); St James-Roberts; Wolke et al. 2017 J Pediatrics meta-analysis of fuss/cry durations by country (e.g., Denmark/Germany/Japan lowest, UK/Canada/Italy highest; numbers).
  • Colic definition (Wessel rule of 3s: 3 h/day, 3 days/week, 3 weeks) and prevalence.
  • Cry acoustics: newborn cry melody shaped by native language prosody (Mampe, Friederici, Christophe & Wermke 2009 Current Biology: French vs German newborns), 30-60 cries.
  • Shaken baby / crying as trigger - a sentence only.
  • Do babies cry with tears? (tear production onset ~1-3 months.)

FEEDING

  • Colostrum composition/amount (teaspoons in first days), stomach size of newborn (the "cherry/walnut/egg" claim - how solid is it? check Bergman 2013 and criticism).
  • Katie Hinde (Arizona State): rhesus macaque milk differs by infant sex (Hinde 2009 Am J Hum Biol / Hinde 2013-14 cortisol in milk; sons get richer milk, daughters more volume/calcium) - numbers; was she in Netflix "Babies"?
  • Human milk composition changes across the day (melatonin/cortisol), during a feed (foremilk/hindmilk), and over lactation; immune cells spike during infant illness (Hassiotou 2013; Riskin 2012).
  • Mennella: amniotic fluid and breast milk carry flavors (Mennella, Jagnow & Beauchamp 2001 Pediatrics: carrot juice study, mothers drank 300 ml carrot juice; babies showed fewer negative faces); garlic 1991/1993.
  • Newborn taste: sweet preference at birth, salt taste emerges ~4 months, bitter rejection; Steiner facial expression studies.
  • Microbiome seeding by delivery mode: Dominguez-Bello 2010 PNAS (vaginal vs C-section babies' microbiota resemble mother's vaginal vs skin), 2016 Nature Medicine vaginal seeding; 2019 Nature Shao et al. (Baby Biome Study, 596 babies, C-section lacked Bacteroides, more hospital pathogens); was Dominguez-Bello in Netflix Babies "First Food"?
  • Michael Georgieff (Minnesota): iron and brain development - was he in Netflix Babies? Key finding.
  • Introduction of solids / allergen timing (LEAP study 2015 NEJM: early peanut introduction reduced allergy ~80% relative risk reduction).
  • Breastfeeding and IQ evidence (PROBIT, Victora 2015 Lancet Global Health Brazil cohort +3.76 IQ points; sibling studies) - flag as contested.

GROWTH

  • Michelle Lampl (Emory): saltatory growth - Lampl, Veldhuis & Johnson 1992 Science: infants grew 0.5-2.5 cm in bursts separated by 2-63 days of no growth; daily measurement of 31 infants. Netflix Babies "Crawling" episode featured Lampl - what was shown (Emory press release Feb 2020)? Also Lampl's sleep-growth link (Lampl & Johnson 2011 Sleep: growth spurts follow increased sleep).
  • Weight doubling by ~5 months, tripling by 1 year; length +50% by 1 year; head circumference growth; brain reaches ~80% of adult volume by age 2 (Knickmeyer 2008 J Neurosci: brain volume +101% in year 1, +15% in year 2).
  • 270-300 bones at birth fusing to 206 - how solid? Fontanelle closure timing (anterior ~18 months, posterior ~2 months).
  • Synapse formation: "up to 1 million new neural connections per second" (Harvard Center on the Developing Child claim - trace the source and how solid it is; Huttenlocher synaptic density peaks).
  • Birth weight; proportion of body that is head at birth (~1/4 of body length).
  • Teething: first tooth ~6 months; no, teething does not cause fever (Massignan 2016 Pediatrics meta-analysis).

SENSES

  • Newborn visual acuity (~20/400 or 20/600; Snellen equivalents; reaches 20/20 by ~3-5 years), focal distance 8-12 inches, color vision emergence (~2-3 months; red first), newborn preference for faces (Johnson & Morton 1991; Goren 1975), preference for mother's face within days (Bushnell 1989), newborns prefer high-contrast.
  • Hearing: fetus hears from ~24-27 weeks; DeCasper & Fifer 1980 Science (newborns suck to hear mother's voice; "Cat in the Hat" DeCasper & Spence 1986); newborns prefer native language (Moon, Cooper & Fifer 1993); Moon 2013 Acta Paediatrica vowel learning in utero.
  • Smell: newborns prefer mother's amniotic fluid/breast odor (Varendi, Porter & Winberg 1994 Lancet; Marlier); newborns crawl toward unwashed breast.
  • Touch/pain: newborns feel pain (Goksan 2015 eLife fMRI 18 of 20 adult pain regions active); skin-to-skin effects.
  • Synesthesia-like cross-modal perception in infants; infants' pupil / eye-contact findings.
  • Infant visual system: newborns see mostly in shades of gray? (Verify.)
  • Babies' preference for sweet; taste buds count claim (10,000?).

Return a dense, well-organized Markdown list grouped under SLEEP / CRYING / FEEDING / GROWTH / SENSES, ~120-180 lines, each fact with number + citation + URL + reliability flag. Note explicitly which Netflix "Babies" scientists you confirmed via press releases (quote the episode name if found).

prompt

You are researching primary scientific literature for a fact sheet that will feed a dense, fact-packed song about infant science (think Hamilton-style lyrical density). Deliverable: return your findings as your final message in Markdown (do NOT write any files). Use WebSearch and WebFetch (load them via ToolSearch "select:WebSearch,WebFetch" first).

Themes you own: MOVEMENT, LANGUAGE, COGNITION. For each fact, give: one or two lines, the specific number(s), the primary paper (first author, year, journal) or best authoritative source, a URL, and a reliability flag: [solid] / [contested] / [pop-science-only]. Prefer primary papers and university press releases over blogs. Verify numbers; do not invent them. If you cannot verify a number, say so explicitly.

Items to verify and quantify (add others you find striking):

MOVEMENT

  • Karen Adolph (NYU): Adolph et al. 2012 Psychological Science "How do you learn to walk? Thousands of steps and dozens of falls per day" - 12-19-month-olds average 2,368 steps/hour, 17 falls/hour, ~14,000 steps/day, distance of ~46 football fields/day (check exact figures: 701 m/hour?); also Adolph's visual cliff / slopes work (infants' knowledge doesn't transfer from crawling to walking: Adolph 1997 Monographs; Kretch & Adolph 2013 "Cliff or step?"); Adolph's "learning to crawl" and crawling styles (belly, hands-knees, bear crawl, bum shuffle, 25 variants); Adolph & Robinson; Netflix Babies episode "First Steps"/"Movement" - was Adolph featured? Check NYU press / her lab news page (adolphlab).
  • Eric Walle (UC Merced): Walle & Campos 2014 Developmental Psychology "Infant language development is related to the acquisition of walking" - walkers had bigger receptive and productive vocabularies than same-age crawlers; UC Merced press release Feb 2020 about Netflix "Babies" (which episode? "First Steps"?). Also Walle's follow-up in Chinese infants (He, Walle & Campos 2015 Infancy) replicating.
  • Libby Cowgill (University of Missouri): bone shape / femur cross-sectional geometry and locomotion (Cowgill et al. 2010 AJPA "Waddling and toddling: the biomechanical effects of an immature gait"), her Netflix Babies appearance (Missouri press release, which episode: "Crawling"? "Movement"?). What was measured?
  • Reflexes: Moro, rooting, sucking, stepping (disappears ~2 months; Thelen's leg-weight explanation: Thelen, Fisher & Ridley-Johnson 1984), palmar grasp (can support own weight? - a 1891 Robinson claim; flag reliability), Babinski, tonic neck; when each disappears.
  • Motor milestones: WHO Multicentre Growth Reference Study 2006 windows (sitting without support median 6.0 mo, 3.8-9.2; hands-and-knees crawling 8.5 mo, 5.2-13.5; walking alone 12.1 mo, 8.2-17.6); ~ what % of babies skip crawling.
  • Thelen's treadmill stepping; "motor development is not maturation alone" (dynamic systems).
  • Back to Sleep campaign delayed motor milestones slightly (Davis 1998 Arch Pediatr Adolesc Med) and reduced SIDS by ~50%.
  • Culture: Kipsigis in Kenya walk earlier (Super 1976); swaddling/ cradleboard (Hopi, Dennis 1940) - walking unaffected.
  • Sleep twitches and motor map (Blumberg).
  • Fine motor: pincer grasp ~9-10 months.

LANGUAGE

  • Patricia Kuhl (U Washington): perceptual narrowing - 6-8-month-olds discriminate non-native phonemes, 10-12-month-olds don't (Werker & Tees 1984 Infant Behav Dev - Hindi/Thompson Salish contrasts); Kuhl 2003 PNAS: 9-month-olds learned Mandarin phonetics from live tutors after 12 sessions (~5 hours) but NOT from video/audio; Kuhl's "social gating"; Kuhl 1992 Science "perceptual magnet" at 6 months. Was Kuhl in Netflix Babies "First Words"?
  • Statistical learning: Saffran, Aslin & Newport 1996 Science - 8-month-olds segment words from 2 minutes of continuous syllable stream (24 infants).
  • Infant-directed speech / parentese: Fernald; Ramírez-Esparza, García-Sierra & Kuhl 2014 Developmental Science (parentese in one-on-one context predicts vocabulary: infants hearing most had 433 words at 24 mo vs 169 for least? verify); Ferjan Ramírez 2020 PNAS parentese coaching RCT.
  • Word gap: Hart & Risley 1995 (30 million word gap by age 3; 42 families) and its criticisms (Sperry, Sperry & Miller 2019 Child Development failed to replicate; Gilkerson 2017 LENA estimate ~4 million words gap); flag contested.
  • Baby Einstein / DVDs: Zimmerman, Christakis & Meltzoff 2007 J Pediatrics - each hour/day of baby DVDs associated with 6-8 fewer vocabulary words in 8-16-month-olds; Disney refund 2009; DeLoache 2010 Psychological Science (no learning from video). Flag.
  • Milestones: babbling ~6 months (canonical babbling), first words ~12 months (range), 50-word mark ~18 months, vocabulary spurt ~18 months (is it real? Ganger & Brent 2004 contested), 200-300 words at 24 mo; words understood vs produced (comprehend ~50 words at 12 mo).
  • Pointing: emerges ~12 months (Tomasello, Carpenter & Liszkowski 2007; Liszkowski declarative pointing); pointing predicts vocabulary (Colonnesi 2010 meta-analysis).
  • Newborn cry melody & language (Mampe 2009) - only if not covered elsewhere; skip detail.
  • Bilingual infants: Kovács & Mehler 2009 PNAS 7-month-olds; Byers-Heinlich 2010 Psychological Science newborns of bilingual mothers.
  • Gesture / sign claims (baby sign language doesn't boost language: Kirk et al. 2013 Child Development) - flag.
  • Eric Walle's walking-vocabulary link (brief cross-ref).
  • Fast mapping (Carey & Bartlett 1978 "chromium").
  • Fernald, Marchman & Weisleder 2013 Developmental Science: SES differences in processing speed by 18 months, 6-month gap by 24 months.

COGNITION

  • Object permanence: Piaget ~8 months (A-not-B error 8-12 months); Baillargeon 1985/1987 Cognition "drawbridge" study: 3.5-4.5-month-olds look longer at impossible events; violation-of-expectation; Baillargeon's "core knowledge" of physics (support, containment).
  • Elizabeth Spelke: core knowledge; Spelke 1992 Psychological Review; Xu & Spelke 2000 Cognition: 6-month-olds discriminate 8 vs 16 dots but not 8 vs 12 (1:2 ratio; 9-month-olds do 2:3); Wynn 1992 Nature "Addition and subtraction by human infants" (5-month-olds, 1+1=2 vs 1+1=1, Mickey Mouse dolls); Izard 2009 PNAS newborns match number across senses (4 vs 12).
  • Fei Xu: Xu & Garcia 2008 PNAS - 8-month-olds infer population from sample (ping-pong balls, red/white); Gweon, Tenenbaum & Schulz 2010 PNAS 15-month-olds; was Xu in Netflix Babies "What Babies Know"?
  • Celeste Kidd: Goldilocks effect - Kidd, Piantadosi & Aslin 2012 PLoS ONE: 7-8-month-olds look away from events that are too predictable or too surprising (U-shaped); 2014 follow-up with sounds.
  • Rebecca Saxe (MIT): awake infant fMRI - Deen et al. 2017 Nature Communications: 4-6-month-olds' visual cortex already shows face- and scene-preferring regions (9 infants scanned, out of 17?); Saxe scanned her own son (Smithsonian 2015); was Saxe in Netflix Babies?
  • Susan Hespos (Northwestern): infants' knowledge of liquids vs solids (Hespos, Ferry & Rips 2009 Psychological Science: 5-month-olds); Hespos & Baillargeon containment; was Hespos in Netflix Babies?
  • Memory: infant memory (Rovee-Collier mobile conjugate reinforcement: 3-month-olds remember 1 week, 6-month-olds 2 weeks); infantile amnesia (earliest memories ~3.5 years; Bauer); deferred imitation (Meltzoff 1988: 9-month-olds remember 24h; 14-month-olds remember 1 week).
  • Mirror self-recognition at ~18-24 months (Amsterdam 1972; Lewis & Brooks-Gunn): rouge test, ~65% at 20-24 months; cross-cultural variation (Kenyan children fail at 6 years: Broesch 2011).
  • Habituation and looking-time method basics (Fantz 1961/1963).
  • Neonatal imitation (Meltzoff & Moore 1977 Science tongue protrusion) and the failed replication (Oostenbroek 2016 Current Biology, 106 infants) - flag contested.
  • Executive function / delayed gratification in toddlers (Mischel marshmallow at 4 yrs; Watts 2018 replication halved effect) - brief.
  • "Babies are born scientists" - Gopnik's framing; Stahl & Feigenson 2015 Science: 11-month-olds explore and learn more about objects that violated expectations (e.g., a ball passing through a wall).
  • Categorization and causal learning in infants (Gopnik/ Schulz blicket detector, 2-4-year-olds).

Return a dense, well-organized Markdown list grouped under MOVEMENT / LANGUAGE / COGNITION, ~150-200 lines, each fact with number + citation + URL + reliability flag. Note explicitly which Netflix "Babies" scientists you confirmed via press releases or interviews (quote the episode name if found).

prompt

You are researching primary scientific literature for a fact sheet that will feed a dense, fact-packed song about infant science (think Hamilton-style lyrical density). Deliverable: return your findings as your final message in Markdown (do NOT write any files). Use WebSearch and WebFetch (load them via ToolSearch "select:WebSearch,WebFetch" first).

Themes you own: SOCIAL/BONDING, MORALITY, NATURE-NURTURE/TEMPERAMENT, and a DEBUNKED/OVERSTATED CLAIMS section. For each fact, give: one or two lines, the specific number(s), the primary paper (first author, year, journal) or best authoritative source, a URL, and a reliability flag: [solid] / [contested] / [pop-science-only]. Prefer primary papers and university press releases over blogs. Verify numbers; do not invent them. If you cannot verify a number, say so explicitly.

Items to verify and quantify (add others you find striking):

SOCIAL / BONDING

  • Ruth Feldman (Reichman University / Bar-Ilan / Yale): oxytocin rises in mothers AND fathers across the first months (Gordon, Zagoory-Sharon, Leckman & Feldman 2010 Biological Psychiatry: 80 couples, fathers' oxytocin equal to mothers'); Abraham et al. 2014 PNAS: primary-caregiving gay fathers show amygdala activation like mothers plus superior temporal sulcus activation like fathers (48 couples; 89 parents); "bio-behavioral synchrony"; Feldman in Netflix Babies "Love" episode - confirm and describe what was filmed.
  • Ed Tronick (UMass Boston): still-face paradigm (Tronick, Als, Adamson, Wise & Brazelton 1978 J Am Acad Child Psychiatry): timeline (2 min play, 2 min still face, 2 min reunion); infants' response within seconds; "good enough" parenting - matched/mismatched states ~30% of the time (Tronick & Gianino 1986 - verify the "70% miscoordinated" / "repair" claim); Tronick in Netflix Babies "Love"? confirm.
  • Harlow's monkeys (cloth vs wire mother, 1958) - one line with number (hours per day on cloth mother ~17-18 h vs <1 h wire).
  • Attachment: Bowlby; Ainsworth Strange Situation 1978 (Baltimore; ~65% secure, 20% avoidant, 15% resistant in US samples; van IJzendoorn 1988 cross-cultural meta-analysis); separation anxiety onset ~8 months, stranger anxiety 6-12 months; disorganized attachment ~15%.
  • Charles Nelson / Nathan Fox / Charles Zeanah: Bucharest Early Intervention Project (136 children, randomized to foster care vs institution; children placed before ~24 months had IQ gains ~ 8-10 points; EEG; Nelson 2007 Science; Nelson, Fox & Zeanah "Romania's Abandoned Children" 2014). Was Nelson in Netflix "Babies" ("Relationships" or "Nature and Nurture")? confirm.
  • Skin-to-skin / kangaroo care: Charpak 2017 Pediatrics 20-year follow-up (Colombia), WHO 2021 immediate KMC trial (NEJM 2021: 25% lower mortality in low-birth-weight infants).
  • Social smile ~6-8 weeks; laughter ~3-4 months; social referencing ~10-12 months (Sorce, Emde, Campos & Klinnert 1985 visual cliff: 74% crossed when mother smiled, 0 when she looked fearful).
  • Joint attention / gaze following ~9-12 months; Tomasello's "shared intentionality"; Meltzoff's "like me".
  • Gaze / eye contact: newborns prefer direct gaze (Farroni 2002 PNAS); newborn and mother gaze synchrony.
  • Contagious crying in newborns (Sagi & Hoffman 1976; Simner 1971; Martin & Clark 1982 - newborns cry more to another newborn's cry than to their own).
  • Fathers: Feldman and others on paternal brain; Lamb; fathers' testosterone drops after birth (Gettler 2011 PNAS, Philippines, 624 men: testosterone fell ~26% morning/ 34% evening among new fathers).
  • Maternal brain changes: Hoekzema 2017 Nature Neuroscience gray matter reductions lasting 2 years.
  • Michael Tomasello & Felix Warneken: toddlers help spontaneously - Warneken & Tomasello 2006 Science (18-month-olds helped in 22 of 24? out-of-reach objects; 84% helped at least once; chimpanzees too); Warneken 2008: rewards undermine helping. Were they in Netflix Babies "Relationships" / "Toddlers"?
  • Dan Fessler (UCLA): anthropology - what infant work would he have featured in Netflix Babies? (Perhaps in "Nature and Nurture" or "Senses" on disgust / crying?) Confirm.
  • Peer play: parallel play ~2 years; toddlers share with peers.

MORALITY

  • Kiley Hamlin (UBC): Hamlin, Wynn & Bloom 2007 Nature "Social evaluation by preverbal infants" - 6- and 10-month-olds prefer the helper over the hinderer (puppet show; 14 of 16 10-month-olds and 12 of 12 6-month-olds? verify exact numbers); Hamlin 2011 PNAS 3-month-olds; Hamlin 2013 Psychological Science 8-month-olds prefer those who punish the hinderer; replication debate (Salvadori 2015; ManyBabies 4 2024 failed to replicate the helper preference - verify, give numbers). Was Hamlin in Netflix Babies "What Babies Know"? confirm.
  • Fairness: Schmidt & Sommerville 2011 PLoS ONE 15-month-olds expect equal sharing; Sloane, Baillargeon & Premack 2012 Psychological Science 19-month-olds.
  • Ingroup bias: Mahajan & Wynn 2012 (graham crackers vs green beans).
  • Paul Bloom "Just Babies" framing.

NATURE-NURTURE / TEMPERAMENT

  • Jerome Kagan: behavioral inhibition - 20% of 4-month-olds highly reactive; Kagan & Snidman; longitudinal outcomes (reactive infants more likely anxious adults).
  • Thomas & Chess New York Longitudinal Study (easy 40%, difficult 10%, slow-to-warm 15%).
  • Twin studies of infant temperament heritability (~20-60%).
  • Epigenetics / Meaney rat licking-and-grooming (Weaver 2004 Nature Neuroscience) - one line, flag extrapolation to humans.
  • Dunedin Study: Caspi/Moffitt - childhood self-control at 3-11 predicts adult health, wealth, crime (Moffitt 2011 PNAS, 1,000 children).
  • Culture and parenting: Lancy; Meehan; Hadza/Aka fathers (Aka fathers hold infants 22% of time, Hewlett); co-sleeping worldwide (~most societies); Gusii mothers rarely talk to infants (LeVine).
  • Birth order / parenting effects - flag.
  • Gender: parents' differential treatment; Mondschein, Adolph & Tamis-LeMonda 2000 "Gender bias in mothers' expectations about infant crawling" - mothers underestimated daughters and overestimated sons' crawling ability; actual performance did not differ.
  • Twin/ sibling: identical twins' babble synchrony - skip unless found.

DEBUNKED / OVERSTATED

  • Wonder Weeks (van de Rijt & Plooij; 10 "leaps"): the PhD student Carolina de Weerth failed to replicate the regression periods (de Weerth & van Geert 1998 Infant Behavior and Development; Plooij's conflict with Groningen, Plooij lost his job in 1998) - give specifics and flag.
  • Mozart effect (Rauscher 1993 Nature in college students, not babies; Pietschnig 2010 meta-analysis null; Georgia governor Zell Miller's CDs 1998).
  • Baby Einstein / video deficit (Zimmerman 2007; Disney refunds 2009) - brief if the other researcher covers; include the refund detail.
  • 30-million-word gap (Hart & Risley 1995; Sperry 2019 critique) - brief cross-reference.
  • Neonatal imitation failed replication (Oostenbroek 2016) - brief.
  • Vaccines-autism (Wakefield 1998 retracted) - one line.
  • "Babies don't feel pain" (surgery without anesthesia until 1980s; Anand 1987) - one line with number.
  • Sleep training harms? (Gradisar 2016 Pediatrics: no harm at 12 months; Price 2012 Pediatrics 5-year follow-up no harm) - flag solid/contested.
  • Teething causes fever (Massignan 2016) - brief if not covered.
  • Walkers/jumpers delay walking (Siegel & Burton 1999; Canada banned baby walkers 2004).
  • "Blank slate" - Pinker; babies born with core knowledge.
  • "Babies see upside down"? flag as myth with explanation.
  • Full-moon birth rates myth; "ten fingers ten toes" skip.
  • Left-brain/right-brain learning styles - skip.
  • Breast milk IQ - cross-ref only.
  • "Sleep regression" discrete stages - cross-ref: little peer-reviewed support.
  • Tummy-time evidence (Hewitt 2020 Pediatrics systematic review).
  • Pacifier/SIDS (De-Kun Li 2006 BMJ: ~90% reduction) and SIDS Back to Sleep (~50% drop since 1994).

Return a dense, well-organized Markdown list grouped under SOCIAL/BONDING / MORALITY / NATURE-NURTURE / DEBUNKED, ~150-200 lines, each fact with number + citation + URL + reliability flag. Note explicitly which Netflix "Babies" (2020) scientists you confirmed via press releases, interviews, or university news (quote the episode name if found).

# Netflix's Babies: A Documentary Review (Blog by eclarke2)

**Blog title:** Netflix's Babies: A documentary review
**Blog URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/
**Structure:** Six episode posts, each with a summary and a review. Posts are dated December 6, 2019, and updated April 28, 2020. The page also links to "Critical Review" and "Source Citations" sections, which are not included in this content.

---

## Episode 1: "Love"
**URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-10/

**Summary**
- **Ruth Feldman** (Centre for Developmental Social Neuroscience, IDC Herzliya, Israel): Studies oxytocin in parent-infant bonding. Oxytocin rises in mothers and infants with lots of physical contact, and fathers' levels were reported as identical to mothers'.
- **Feldman lab, amygdala findings:** The maternal oxytocin surge activated mothers' amygdalae more than fathers'. In a study of gay couples, men who were primary caregivers showed amygdala activation similar to mothers, even without pregnancy.
- **Ed Tronick** (Harvard): Uses the still-face experiment. Infants try to re-engage unresponsive mothers. Infants with better relationships showed lower cortisol and less stress.
- **Anne Rifkin-Graboi** (NIE, NTU Singapore): Rated mothers' responsiveness to six-month-olds, then compared MRI scans with scans from soon after birth. Infants with less responsive parents had slightly larger hippocampi, which the post links to self-managed stress.
- Filming locations included London, England, and Connecticut.

**Review**
- The author argues the three experts show the importance of early bonding, with added focus on fathers.
- Cites Weisman et al., "Oxytocin administration, salivary testosterone, and father–infant social behavior" (pp. 48, 51). Fathers with lower basal testosterone had better relationships with children and greater T increases after oxytocin.
- The author concludes oxytocin supports father-infant bonding, possibly more effectively in men who are already more involved in childcare. The post links this to the course text *Paleo Parenting*.

---

## Episode 2: "First Food"
**URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-9/

**Summary**
- **Katie Hinde** (Arizona State University, anthropologist): Studied monkey breast milk. Found richer, more energetically dense milk for sons and higher calcium-phosphorus ratios for daughters. Analyzed data from over one million dairy cows, finding cows produce significantly more milk after bearing daughters, for unknown reasons.
- Hinde says the best option after breastmilk is formula designed with human-milk science, not cow's milk. She describes milk as individualized, with daily experiences leaving "signatures," and says mothers can raise antibodies when they sense illness. Hormones pass through milk, enabling a "conversation."
- Mothers interviewed: a British mother who enjoys breastfeeding, and a Connecticut mother of twins who stopped due to insufficient supply.
- **Michael Georgieff** (University of Minnesota Institute of Child Development): Found many babies deficient in iron, zinc, and copper. Babies are born with iron lasting about four months, but some mothers transfer less in the womb. EEG studies showed iron-sufficient babies better distinguished their mother's voice from a stranger's, suggesting a more efficient hippocampus. He claims eliminating metal deficiencies worldwide would "shift the world's IQ positively by ten points."
- **Susan Lynch** (UCSF, Department of Medicine): Argues infants need early microbe exposure. Gut microbiomes can predict health. Early dog and cat exposure protected against immune disease. Household dust from homes without pets had less bacterial exposure. Stool samples from one-month-olds showed three colonization patterns, two linked to lower allergy and asthma risk.
- Hinde concludes that food is central to human culture and that eating solids joins babies to the family.

**Review**
- The author opens by questioning the "breast is best" phrase, saying the evidence is less widespread than the slogan suggests.
- Cites Oddy (2001), "Breastfeeding protects against illness and infection in infants and children: a review of the evidence." The author quotes Oddy: "it is now clear that human milk is precisely engineered for the human infant" (p. 11).
- The author uses Hinde's antibody findings to argue breastmilk is individualized and mothers are "the best source of food" for children.

---

## Episode 3: "Crawling"
**URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-8/

**Summary**
- Opens with a London mother whose husband stays home while she works. Her son shows little interest in crawling.
- **Marianne Barbu-Roth** (Motor Development Group, University of Paris): Newborns can move toward the breast within an hour and swim in water. Her black-dot motion illusion made babies "swim in the air," which she calls a "primitive link" between vision and motor systems. Her "Crawlerskate" let two-day-old babies move across a mattress. She argues humans are quadrupeds, not bipeds, so crawling is not transitional.
- **Michelle Lampl** (Emory Center for the Study of Human Health, Atlanta): Measured 30 babies daily. Growth came in spurts of 0.5 to 1.65 cm per day, separated by 2 to 28 days of no growth. Spurts disrupted sleep and increased hunger. She describes hostile questioning from a leading figure in the field.
- **Karen Adolph** (NYU, psychology): Used an adjustable slope apparatus that increased in six-degree steps. Novice crawlers approached a 90-degree drop as they did shallow slopes. Intermediate crawlers stopped at edges. Advanced crawlers (about 20 weeks of experience) used alternative strategies, such as descending backward. Crawling is not a steady progression, and some babies skip it. Adolph concludes "crawling isn't mandatory."

**Review**
- The author argues crawling is not critical to development, citing the Ache people of Paraguay, whose babies are carried until walking, with no crawling stage (Kuther, 2020).
- Cites Jacalyn Duffin's *Clio in the Clinic* (p. 155). Duffin argues that back-sleeping to prevent SIDS may have altered infant motor development. The author quotes a study from England starting in 1990: "increasing numbers never crawled."
- The author concludes babies don't need to crawl to walk, and public health measures can change how infants move.

---

## Episode 4: "First Words"
**URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-7/

**Summary**
- **Kathy Hirsh-Pasek** (Infant Language Lab, Temple University, Philadelphia): Tested whether speech melodies help infants segment language. Babies looked longer at speakers playing speech with natural pauses than unnatural ones. She suggests babies may hear language patterns in the womb and recommends commenting on what infants notice and "pretending" to converse.
- **Jenny Saffran** (UW-Madison, psychology): Studies statistical learning using an invented language of nonsense words in random streams. Babies listened longer to non-words once familiar words were learned. She says babies may understand 10 to 50 words by age one.
- A bilingual London family (father speaks French, mother English). Saffran says babies sort languages by "musical properties."
- **Erich Jarvis** (Lab of Neurogenetics of Language, Rockefeller University): Only three bird and five mammal species are vocal learners. He argues human speech learning resembles songbird learning. Notes motor-vocal connections and the motor theory of vocal learning. Compares speech to "learning how to coordinate your body." Humans have an extra copy of the SRGAP2 gene, which he links to more brain connections.
- The episode closes with families reading to babies at bedtime.

**Review**
- The author argues the best approach is talking with infants as much as possible to build "conversation," which strengthens caregiver closeness.
- Cites Golinkoff et al. (p. 341) on infant-directed speech. The author quotes: "language learning is therefore powered by the shared social relationship between infants and caregivers."
- The author concludes that babbling, though unintelligible, builds the foundation for future speech, consistent with Jarvis's view that speech requires practice.

---

## Episode 5: "Sleep"
**URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-6/

**Summary**
- Opens with a London family whose one-month-old has disrupted sleep.
- **Andre Fenton** (NYU, Neurobiology of Cognition Lab) with **Pascal Wallisch**: Used data from parent-logged sleep apps, with millions of minute-accurate data points. Found an initial eating-sleeping cycle starting around four months, with consistent night sleep within a year.
- **Mark Blumberg** (University of Iowa, psychology and brain sciences): Explains the circadian rhythm, linked to the hypothalamus and brainstem. Connections between these regions are absent early on and develop later. Shows twitching in sleeping animals and infants, which he describes as possibly "byproducts of dreams." EEG recordings suggest infant twitches help the brain "map the body."
- **Rebecca Spencer** (UMass Amherst, psychological and brain sciences): Toy-play experiments found babies who stayed awake remembered less and played with toys differently than babies who napped. Sleep spindles move memories from the hippocampus to cortical long-term storage. Babies need multiple daily sleeps to consolidate learning faster than adults.

**Review**
- Cites Lubowski and Milojevich (2013), "Sleeping like a baby: Examining relations between habitual infant sleep, recall memory, and generalization across cues at 10 months" (p. 374).
- Findings: babies remembered actions better after a two-hour delay. Napping time correlated positively with recall. Percent of sleep at night correlated negatively with memory performance, which the author calls "contrary to popular opinion."
- The author concludes napping matters more than night sleep for memory at about 10 months.

---

## Episode 6: "First Steps"
**URL:** https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-5/

**Summary**
- Opens with eleven-month-old twins at different walking stages.
- **Nadia Dominici** (Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam, Department of Human Movement Sciences): Studied "newborn stepping," a reflex when infants are supported upright. Compared muscle activity (EMG) and movement sensors between newborns on a treadmill and independent toddlers. Toddlers showed the same movement patterns, suggesting innate walking building blocks. She recommends encouraging movement before walking.
- **Libby Cowgill** (University of Missouri, biological anthropology): Examined bone cross-sections from over 600 infant remains, tens to thousands of years old. Young leg bones are oval rather than teardrop-shaped like adults', suggesting bones adapt to loading.
- **Cara Ocobock** (University of Notre Dame, anthropology), with Cowgill: Used force plate data (ground reaction force). Infants sway side to side while walking, so bone builds up on the sides, producing different bone shapes than adults.
- **Eric Walle** (UC Merced, developmental psychology): Found walking infants had larger vocabularies. Crawling and walking infants heard about the same number of words daily and vocalized about equally, but parents' responses differed. Walking infants' parents' responses supported higher word learning rates.
- The episode ends with a montage of all featured babies walking, including the slower twin.

**Review**
- The author summarizes Dominici's question: whether walking is innate or newborn stepping is forgotten.
- Cites Adolph and Robinson, "The Road to Walking" (p. 4): infants' legs gain mass faster than strength, so stepping is lost, then regained as strength catches up.
- The author concludes walking may be innate and passed down through ancestors who became bipedal, and infants would keep stepping if legs weren't so heavy. This is the author's own inference, not a finding stated in the cited sources.
WebFetch{"url":"https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-10/","prompt":"Give the full content of
input
{
  "url": "https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-10/",
  "prompt": "Give the full content of this post about Episode 1 'Love': every scientist named with institution, each study described, findings, numbers, quotes, and the writer's critique. Also list any links to other episode posts (titles and URLs)."
}
result
**Episode 1: "Love" (Netflix's *Babies*: A documentary review)**

**Scientists featured in the episode summary**

- **Dr. Ruth Feldman**, Centre for Developmental Social Neuroscience, IDC Herzliya, Israel:
  - Studies oxytocin in parent-infant bonding. Oxytocin rises in mothers and infants who have a lot of physical contact, which increases the urge to bond and feels rewarding to mothers.
  - Measured oxytocin in new fathers and found levels identical to mothers' levels. She argues that fathers' oxytocin rises with more time spent caring for the child.
  - Found that the maternal oxytocin surge activated mothers' amygdalae much more than fathers'.
  - Studied gay couples. Men who were primary caregivers showed amygdala activation similar to mothers', even without pregnancy or childbirth. She concludes that becoming a parent to an infant is "a choice."
- **Professor Ed Tronick**, Harvard University:
  - Used the still-face experiment. When mothers stopped responding, babies smiled and cried to re-engage them, showing that infants are born ready for social relationships.
  - Measured cortisol to test whether a good mother-baby relationship helps infants cope with stress. Infants with better relationships had lower cortisol and less stress.
- **Dr. Anne Rifkin-Graboi**, head of Infancy and Early Childhood Research at NIE, NTU, Singapore:
  - Rated mothers' responsiveness while they played with their six-month-old babies, then compared the babies' MRI brain scans with scans taken soon after birth.
  - Found that hippocampus development varied with parenting style. Infants with less responsive parents had slightly larger hippocampi, which the researchers read as the babies managing their own stress.
  - Stresses that babies need to feel the world is safe in order to explore.

**Study cited in the writer's review**

- **"Oxytocin administration, salivary testosterone, and father–infant social behavior"** (Weisman et al., pp. 48, 51):
  - Fathers with lower basal testosterone had better relationships with their children and did more childcare.
  - Fathers were given oxytocin and their interactions with their children were observed. Those with lower basal testosterone showed a larger testosterone increase after oxytocin and "greater social–behavioral reciprocity between father and infant, indicating greater investment in the parenting context."

**Writer's critique**

The writer says the episode shows that early bonding and support are essential to healthy development, and gives unusual attention to father-infant bonding. Connecting the episode to *Paleo Parenting*, the writer argues that oxytocin supports father-infant bonding much as it does mother-infant bonding, and may work even more effectively in men who are already more involved in childcare.

**Links to other episode posts**

**Babies (Netflix, 2020) and Babies (2010 film): Quotes and Episode Details**

**Series overview:** 12 episodes in 2 parts, produced by Nutopia, set in multiple countries including the USA, UK, Israel, and Singapore. The page lists six numbered episodes.

**Episode 1: Love**
- Narrator, opening: "Babies are born with the answer to what it means to be human." The page presents this as the series' opening narration.
- Dr. Ruth Feldman: "Fatherhood is biological. The more you do with your child, the more oxytocin is produced." Her research found primary caregiving fathers show hormonal surges like mothers.
- Dr. Feldman: "It is a choice to be a parent to that infant." She discusses a study of gay couples who were primary caregivers, arguing the brain adapts to the parenting role.
- Professor Ed Tronick: "Babies are born with the ability to be in social relationships." He discusses the Still Face Experiment, in which infants try to re-engage a non-responsive parent.
- Dr. Anne Rifkin-Graboi: "The hippocampus varied with parenting styles... infants with less responsive parents had slightly larger hippocampi." She links parenting to how infant brains manage stress.
- Narrator: "A baby's cry is a biological siren that no human brain can ignore." This explains the evolutionary role of distress signals.
- Narrator: "They are masters of the universe in the making." This contrasts newborns' helplessness with their cognitive potential.
- Dr. Feldman: "When we fall in love with our babies, our brains change forever." She describes lasting changes in the amygdala and oxytocin pathways.

**Episode 2: What Babies Know**
- Narrator: "They are not just a blank slate; they are born with expectations about everyday life." This challenges the historical "tabula rasa" theory.

**Episode 3: Sleep**
- Narrator: "Sleep is not just a time for rest; it is a time for the brain to work." Infants process motor skills and memories while asleep.

**Episode 4: First Steps**
- Narrator: "Every crawl, every step, is a massive victory of physics and biology." This covers the effort of moving from crawling to walking.

**Episode 5: First Words**
- Dr. Anne Fernald: "Language begins long before the first word is ever spoken." Babies track sound statistics in their environment to map language.

**Episode 6: Toddlers**
- Narrator: "The brain is literally building itself through every interaction." The page describes this as concluding the first season, covering the first 365 days.

**Unassigned or series-wide narration**
- "The first year of life is the most significant period of our existence." (Recurring theme; neural and physical growth from birth to twelve months.)
- "They are the world's greatest learners." (Summary of "Nature vs. Nurture" segment.)
- Tronick: "The bond is the safety net that allows them to explore the world." Discusses low cortisol and secure attachment during stress.
- Narrator, series finale: "It's not just about nature or nurture; it's about how they dance together."
- Narrator, closing: "Every baby is a different story, but the language of love is universal."

**Article**
- **Title:** "This 'Babies' Clip Shows The Power Of Dad Spending Time With Baby — EXCLUSIVE" (Romper)
- **Author:** Janet Manley
- **Date:** Feb. 21, 2020
- **Show:** *Babies*, a Netflix docuseries filmed over three years, following families through their babies' first year
- **Episode:** "Love" (episode 1), which explores parent-child connection
- **Release:** Streaming on Netflix Feb. 21 (2020)

**Scientists and Experts**
- **Ruth Feldman, Ph.D.**, neuroscientist, Simms-Mann Professor of Developmental Social Neuroscience at the Interdisciplinary Center (IDC) Herzliya, Israel, with a joint appointment at Yale Child Study Center

**Numbers and Findings**
- **80 fathers** had their chemical changes studied over the first few months of their babies' lives
- Oxytocin, the "love hormone," was measured in fathers' saliva
- Oxytocin bonding in fathers mirrored that of mothers
- Father's chemical response correlated with the amount of time spent caring for the baby
- Oxytocin's role in mothers has been documented for about a century

**Studies Cited**
1. **Kim, P., Rigo, P., Swain, J. (2014)**, "Neural Plasticity in Fathers of Human Infants," *Social Neuroscience*. Yale researchers found increased gray matter in brain areas tied to "parental motivation."
2. **Brown, G., Mangelsdorf, S., Neff, C. (2012)**, "Father Involvement, Paternal Sensitivity, and Father-Child Attachment Security in the First Three Years," *Journal of Family Psychology*. Found a link between father involvement and sensitivity at 3 months and child attachment at 3 years.

**Quotes**
- Feldman: "Fatherhood is biological," (on fatherhood)
- Feldman: "It's as deep as motherhood." (comparing fatherhood to motherhood)
- Feldman: "The more you do with baby — really lift your sleeves and take care of the child" (on caregiving and oxytocin)
- Brown et al. (2012) concluded that the data supported "reciprocal relations between fathering behavior and father-child attachment security."

**Other Details**
- The clip shows fathers bathing, snuggling, and holding babies overhead.
- The article links to an external Independent article for the 80-father study.

- **Kuhl's research:** A University of Washington study, with Kuhl as lead author, found that motor-planning regions of seven-month-old infants' brains activated when they heard vowel sounds. The researchers expected activity only in the auditory regions, which babies use to listen. Kuhl's interpretation is that infants want to answer back: when babies hear us talk, the main thing they want to do is talk back and take turns.
- **Parentese:** Kuhl's work supports parentese, a form of talking to babies with a higher pitch, slower pace and exaggerated facial expressions. Other coverage says babies spoken to in parentese developed larger vocabularies throughout the first three years of life.

A 2017 Nature Communications study led by Ben Deen found face and scene responses in infant brains. A 2022 study led by Heather Kosakowski in Current Biology found usable data from more than 50 infants two to nine months old, with face, body, and scene regions in the visual cortex.

The search turned up a clear match. Felix Warneken's work appears in the "Toddlers" episode of Netflix's *Babies*, which is the second season. According to a University of Michigan news release, the episode showcases his work with children and chimpanzees as he seeks to understand the origins of human cooperation. The show's second batch of episodes premiered June 19, 2020, and the six episodes are: "What Babies Know," "Movement," "Senses," "Relationships," "Nature & Nurture," and "Toddlers."

The episode covers the helping experiment. In one study, Warneken clumsily knocks a cup off a table to the floor, in full view of a toddler, and the child walks to retrieve it for him.

The underlying research is Warneken and Michael Tomasello's 2006 study in *Science*. It tested 24 18-month-old toddlers, as well as three three-year-old to five-year-old chimps raised by people. The chimpanzee results were weaker: their altruistic capacity seemed limited only to certain scenarios, and the researchers note that the chimps' basic helping skills may be attributed to their upbringing among people.

WebFetch{"url":"https://ubyssey.ca/science/netflix-babies","prompt":"Extract everything about Kiley Hamlin's
input
{
  "url": "https://ubyssey.ca/science/netflix-babies",
  "prompt": "Extract everything about Kiley Hamlin's segment: episode, study filmed, findings, numbers (e.g., how many infants prefer helper), quotes, other scientists mentioned, and her 'personal journey'."
}
result
**Episode and series**
- **Series:** Netflix docu-series *Babies*, 12 episodes, following infants through their first year.
- **Episode:** Hamlin's research appears in the social relationships episode.
- **Hamlin's role:** Director of the UBC Centre for Infant Cognition and associate professor of psychology.

**Study filmed**
- Infants watch short shows in which one character needs help, one character helps, and a third character harms the one needing help. The article says researchers run many versions of this scenario.
- At the end, researchers ask which character the infant prefers to interact with.
- Participants range from three months old, with most research done within the first 24 months.

**Findings**
- Babies generally show greater preference for helpers over harmers.
- Infants may attribute positive and negative associations to social interactions and use them to form their own preferences.
- Hamlin says this suggests babies have a stronger or more sophisticated understanding of the social world than previously thought: "babies have a stronger or more sophisticated understanding of the social world than we previously thought."

**Numbers**
- The article gives no sample sizes, percentages, or counts of infants who preferred helpers.

**Quotes**
- On why helping and harming matter: "if babies can understand anything, they should probably understand actions"
- On the longitudinal follow-up: "very early and very basic knowledge about the social world could really impact the trajectory of one's social development"
- On infant science: "infant science is just this funny and great combination of important and rigorous science"

**Ongoing longitudinal project**
- Hamlin has started a project studying babies as young as hours after birth.

description Extract related Babies episode video IDs and English captions from YouTube pages; extract text from the educational PDF
timeout 180000
cd /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jalex-projects-clawdpop/ca2089be-6e29-4607-84a4-ba095fc3fca1/scratchpad && python3 -I - <<'EOF'
import re, json, html, sys, urllib.request, pathlib
out = pathlib.Path('yt'); 
for vid in ['KJCL9LR6rJI','p1otGt99Rec']:
    h = open(f'yt/{vid}.html', encoding='utf-8', errors='ignore').read()
    # related videos: videoId followed by title
    rel = {}
    for m in re.finditer(r'"videoId":"([A-Za-z0-9_-]{11})".{0,600}?"title":\{"(?:runs":\[\{"text"|simpleText)":"([^"]+)"', h):
        rel.setdefault(m.group(1), m.group(2))
    print(f"== {vid} related:")
    for k,v in rel.items():
        if 'abies' in v or 'Netflix' in v:
            print('  ', k, '|', v)
    # caption url en-US
    m = re.search(r'"captionTracks":(\[.*?\])', h)
    tracks = json.loads(m.group(1))
    en = [t for t in tracks if t.get('languageCode','').startswith('en')]
    print('  en tracks:', [(t['languageCode'], t.get('kind','')) for t in en])
    if en:
        url = en[0]['baseUrl']
        req = urllib.request.Request(url, headers={'User-Agent':'Mozilla/5.0'})
        data = urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=60).read().decode('utf-8','ignore')
        open(f'yt/{vid}.xml','w').write(data)
        # convert to plain text
        texts = re.findall(r'<text[^>]*>(.*?)</text>', data, flags=re.S)
        txt = ' '.join(html.unescape(re.sub(r'<[^>]+>','',t)).replace('\n',' ') for t in texts)
        open(f'yt/{vid}.txt','w').write(txt)
        print('  caption chars:', len(txt))
EOF
echo "---PDF---"
PYTHONPATH=pylib python3 -I -c "
import pypdf
r=pypdf.PdfReader('pdf/babies-edu.pdf')
t='\n'.join(p.extract_text() or '' for p in r.pages)
open('pdf/babies-edu.txt','w').write(t)
print(len(t)); print(t[:6000])

**Note:** The page content is truncated at the end, so this list covers only the visible text.

**Scientists and experts**
- **Prof. Ruth Feldman**, developmental social neuroscience, based in Tel Aviv, Israel (Series 1, Episode 1 "Love")
- **Prof. Susan Lynch**, University of California (Series 1, Episode 2)
- **Julie Mennella**, Monell Chemical Senses Center (food preferences)
- **Vasu Reddy**, University of Portsmouth (laughter and bonding theory)
- **Kang Lee**, University of Toronto (multiple languages)

**Family featured**
- **Willow**, a one-day-old baby, with parents **Adam** and **Rachel** and toddler sister **Lily** (Series 1, Episode 1). Their country is not stated.

**Studies and findings**
- **Oxytocin (Ep. 1):** Feldman says the oxytocin rise mothers experience can also occur in fathers who take on a caregiving role.
- **Amygdala (Ep. 1):** Feldman says the mother's amygdala stays activated long-term, while a father's response is about a quarter of a mother's.
- **Pets and immunity (Ep. 2):** A study found babies living with dogs, and to a lesser extent cats, are better protected from childhood asthma, likely due to greater microbe exposure.
- **Pregnancy diet (Ep. 2):** A mother's diet during pregnancy shapes her baby's food preferences, as taste and memory form before birth.
- **Laughter (Series 2):** Babies use teasing, laughter, and smiling to bond with parents and other adults. This is a theory put forward by Reddy.
- **Language (Series 2):** Babies can learn multiple languages at once. By around one year old, unused neural pathways are removed, leaving only the sounds of their native language.

**Series 1 episode topics (as stated)**
- Ep. 1: biology of bonding
- Ep. 2: a child's first food
- Ep. 3: crawling
- Ep. 4: how babies learn to speak
- Ep. 5: sleep
- Final episode: how walking changes a baby's world

**Series 2 topics (no episode numbers stated)**
- Socialisation, how children acquire normality, humour, how toddlers view their parents, and what makes humans human

**Numbers**
- 15 newborns followed from around the world
- 12 episodes in total
- 6 new episodes in Series 2
- About 3 years of filming
- Babies' brains lose unused language sounds by about 1 year old
- A father's amygdala response is about a quarter of a mother's

**Other locations**
- Series 2 was released on Netflix in June (the article was published 24 June 2020)
- Not stated: the countries of the other 14 newborns

cd /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jalex-projects-clawdpop/ca2089be-6e29-4607-84a4-ba095fc3fca1/scratchpad && echo "== FULL EPISODE mentions in HTML:" && for id in KJCL9LR6rJI p1otGt99Rec; do grep -o '.\{0,160\}FULL EPISODE.\{0,40\}' yt/$id.html | grep -o '"[^"]*FULL EPISODE[^"]*"' | sort -u; grep -o '"contentId":"[A-Za-z0-9_-]\{11\}"' yt/$id.html | sort -u | head -40; grep -o 'list=[A-Za-z0-9_-]\{10,40\}' yt/$id.html | sort -u | head; done; echo "== xml head:"; head -c 400 yt/KJCL9LR6rJI.xml; echo; echo "== yt-dlp subs:"; for id in KJCL9LR6rJI p1otGt99Rec; do python3 -I -c "import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'pylib'); from yt_dlp import main; main()" -- --skip-download --write-subs --write-auto-subs --sub-langs "en.*" --sub-format "vtt/srv3/best" -o "yt/%(id)s.%(ext)s" "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=$id" 2>&1 | tail -4; done; ls -la yt/; echo "== PDF:"; python3 -I -c "
import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'pylib')
import pypdf

2020   |   Docu-series   |   Netflix   |   TV-PG
      START THE CONVERSATION  
Each episode of Babies explores different developmental and emotional stages of babies and 
their families. As you watch one or all of the episodes, keep track of the questions and topics that 
arise to explore in class, with friends, and your family. 
Here are several questions to get your started:
What can babies teach us about ourselves, love, relationships and what it means to be 
human?
How has science and research contributed to and influenced our understanding of infant 
development and family relationships?
What research question would you want to investigate about babies?
Revisit some of the central questions from each episode as another option:
What’s it like to be a baby?
What does crawling teach us about the way all of us move?
Why do human babies learn to speak much later than they learn language? 
Why is it challenging for babies to sleep according to a pattern?
Why does sleep help us feel and think better?
Why is learning to walk such a game changer in a baby’s life? 
 
The docu-series follows 15 international and diverse families 
and 36 world renowned scientists (mostly from Europe, the U.S. 
and Israel) that research everything related to babies -  from 
newborn to toddler, and showcases groundbreaking science 
revealing the developmental leaps that babies go through in the 
first year of life.
 WATCH TRAILER

The discussion and engagement questions are organized by each episode. Some of the questions 
lean towards open discussion, while others may be discussed after learning more information 
shared in the series. 
If you are watching Babies as part of a virtual class experience, we invite you to research these 
questions in more depth beyond the episode. If the episodes are being watched more informally 
with your family and friends, the questions can be helpful in guiding your general discussions.
      DISCUSS & ENGAGE: FOR SCHOOLS AND IN THE HOME
LOVE: The biology of bonding unravels to 
reveal how caregiving, social interaction, and 
stress can physically change both parent and 
newborn.
What do we learn about the biology of 
bonding from the “Love” episode?
How do the parenting styles effect the 
development of an infant’s brain?
Where does it’s personality come from? 
How do they become who they are?
CRAWLING: From how they grow to how 
they go, three scientists find out that crawling 
is much more than a transitional phase.
Is early movement a reflex, or is it 
something that even newborn babies can 
control?
What does crawling teach us about the 
way we all move?
How does our new understanding of 
babies’ growth patterns change the 
way we think about crawling and other 
developmental stages?
Is there such a thing as “normal” with 
regard to how babies acquire movement? 
FIRST WORDS: Experts examine the 
rhythm and flow of language and explore how 
babies break down linguistic beats before 
they know how to speak.
How do babies learn language? 
What role do rhythms, such as pitch 
patterns or natural and unnatural pauses, 
play in learning language? How do babies 
figure out where words begin and end? 
Professor Erich Jarvis asks, “We humans 
are along a spectrum with other animals 
when it comes to spoken language 
abilities. But we are at the far end of 
the spectrum allowing us to have more 
complex language. I have asked the 
question, ‘what makes us so much further 
on the spectrum? What is different with 
our brains or genes?’” 
SLEEP: Researchers delve into sleep, a 
dynamic time for babies, with studies on 
twitching, apps, and naps.
According to Dr. Andre Fenton, “Getting 
babies to sleep through the night is a 
universal human problem.” Why is it 
difficult for babies to develop a normal 
sleep pattern?
What is happening in babies’ bodies and 
brains when they sleep?
How is sleep related to memory and 
learning?
 
FIRST STEPS: Scientists venture into the 
ways that walking changes a baby’s world and 
unveil findings on neonatal reflexes, skeletal 
development and talking.
Walking is a gamechanger for babies - it 
affects the baby’s life, the parents’ life and 
how they will forever interact. Why do 
you think walking is so transformational?
Professor Eric Walle at the University of 
California, Merced shares how important 
it is to see that development in babies 
is interconnected. Language, motor 
development, social interaction are all 
working together to create a person 
unlike anything else in our world. When 
you reflect on what you have learned 
about babies from these episodes, does 
one developmental milestone stand out 
more than another in regards to effecting 
a baby’s development?

   17896 pm/batch1.txt
1. Science. 1992 Oct 30;258(5083):801-3. doi: 10.1126/science.1439787.

Saltation and stasis: a model of human growth.

Lampl M(1), Veldhuis JD, Johnson ML.

Author information:
(1)Department of Anthropology, University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia 19104.

Comment in
    Science. 1995 Apr 21;268(5209):442-7. doi: 10.1126/science.7716552.

Human growth has been viewed as a continuous process characterized by changing 
velocity with age. Serial length measurements of normal infants were assessed 
weekly (n = 10), semiweekly (n = 18), and daily (n = 3) (19 females and 12 
males) during their first 21 months. Data show that growth in length occurs by 
discontinuous, aperiodic saltatory spurts. These bursts were 0.5 to 2.5 
centimeters in amplitude during intervals separated by no measurable growth (2 
to 63 days duration). These data suggest that 90 to 95 percent of normal 
development during infancy is growth-free and length accretion is a distinctly 
saltatory process of incremental bursts punctuating background stasis.

DOI: 10.1126/science.1439787
PMID: 1439787 [Indexed for MEDLINE]


2. Psychol Sci. 2012;23(11):1387-94. doi: 10.1177/0956797612446346. Epub 2012 Oct
 19.

How do you learn to walk? Thousands of steps and dozens of falls per day.

Adolph KE(1), Cole WG, Komati M, Garciaguirre JS, Badaly D, Lingeman JM, Chan 
GL, Sotsky RB.

Author information:
(1)New York University, USA.

A century of research on the development of walking has examined periodic gait 
over a straight, uniform path. The current study provides the first corpus of 
natural infant locomotion derived from spontaneous activity during free play. 
Locomotor experience was immense: Twelve- to 19-month-olds averaged 2,368 steps 
and 17 falls per hour. Novice walkers traveled farther faster than expert 
crawlers, but had comparable fall rates, which suggests that increased 
efficiency without increased cost motivates expert crawlers to transition to 
walking. After walking onset, natural locomotion improved dramatically: Infants 
took more steps, traveled farther distances, and fell less. Walking was 
distributed in short bouts with variable paths--frequently too short or 
irregular to qualify as periodic gait. Nonetheless, measures of periodic gait 
and of natural locomotion were correlated, which indicates that better walkers 
spontaneously walk more and fall less. Immense amounts of time-distributed, 
variable practice constitute the natural practice regimen for learning to walk.

DOI: 10.1177/0956797612446346
PMCID: PMC3591461
PMID: 23085640 [Indexed for MEDLINE]


3. Science. 2011 Nov 18;334(6058):997-9. doi: 10.1126/science.1210617.

Locomotor primitives in newborn babies and their development.

Dominici N(1), Ivanenko YP, Cappellini G, d'Avella A, Mondì V, Cicchese M, 
Fabiano A, Silei T, Di Paolo A, Giannini C, Poppele RE, Lacquaniti F.

Author information:
(1)Laboratory of Neuromotor Physiology, IRCCS Santa Lucia Foundation, Rome, 
Italy.

Comment in
    Science. 2011 Nov 18;334(6058):912-3. doi: 10.1126/science.1214778.

How rudimentary movements evolve into sophisticated ones during development 
remains unclear. It is often assumed that the primitive patterns of neural 
control are suppressed during development, replaced by entirely new patterns. 
Here we identified the basic patterns of lumbosacral motoneuron activity from 
multimuscle recordings in stepping neonates, toddlers, preschoolers, and adults. 
Surprisingly, we found that the two basic patterns of stepping neonates are 
retained through development, augmented by two new patterns first revealed in 
toddlers. Markedly similar patterns were observed also in the rat, cat, macaque, 
and guineafowl, consistent with the hypothesis that, despite substantial 
phylogenetic distances and morphological differences, locomotion in several 
animal species is built starting from common primitives, perhaps related to a 
common ancestral neural network.

DOI: 10.1126/science.1210617
PMID: 22096202 [Indexed for MEDLINE]


4. Biochem Genet. 2013 Aug;51(7-8):503-13. doi: 10.1007/s10528-013-9581-1. Epub 
2013 Mar 21.

The effect of certain conditions in the regulation of cystathionine γ-lyase by 
exogenous hydrogen sulfide in mammalian cells.

Wang M(1), Guo Z, Wang S.

Author information:
(1)College of Life Sciences and Technology, Tongji University, 1239 Siping Road, 
Shanghai 200092, China.

Cystathionine γ-lyase (CSE), one of three enzymes in the trans-sulfuration 
pathway, is responsible for the production of endogenous hydrogen sulfide (H2S) 
using L-cysteine or L-homocysteine as a substrate. The regulatory mechanism of 
CSE by exogenous H2S remains unknown. The transcription and expression of the 
CSE gene regulated by exogenous H2S at approximately physiologic concentrations 
was investigated using luciferase assay, Western blotting, and quantitative 
RT-PCR. The results revealed that exogenous H2S down-regulates the transcription 
and expression of CSE in mammalian cells at 10-80 μM. Exogenous H2S at 120 μM 
increases the transcription and expression of CSE significantly. At a 
concentration of exogenous H2S over 160 μM, the transcription and expression of 
CSE are inhibited completely. These findings suggest that CSE expression has not 
only a feedback to the enzyme itself at lower concentrations of exogenous H2S 
but also can be up-regulated at higher concentrations, and H2S may become toxic 
at higher levels.

DOI: 10.1007/s10528-013-9581-1
PMID: 23515848 [Indexed for MEDLINE]


5. J Dent. 2010 Jul;38(7):597-601. doi: 10.1016/j.jdent.2010.03.010. Epub 2010
Mar  30.

Variations in human DEJ scallop size with tooth type.

Brauer DS(1), Marshall GW, Marshall SJ.

Author information:
(1)Division of Biomaterials and Bioengineering, Department of Preventive and 
Restorative Dental Sciences, University of California San Francisco, Box 0758, 
San Francisco, CA 94143-0758, USA. d.brauer@qmul.ac.uk

OBJECTIVE: Recent literature suggests that the scalloped structure of the 
dentino-enamel junction (DEJ) is critical for DEJ stability. Aim of our study 
was to see if there are differences in scallop size and shape with tooth type.
METHODS: Enamel of extracted permanent human teeth was demineralised using EDTA. 
After fixation and dehydration the scallops of the DEJ were investigated in a 
scanning electron microscope. Scallop area and shape (circularity) were measured 
for molars, premolars, canines and incisors.
RESULTS: Scallop area showed main effects for tooth type and specimen, while, 
due to high variability in third molars, there was also an interaction effect 
(repeated measures two-way ANOVA, p<0.05). Differences between tooth types were 
statistically significant, suggesting that posterior teeth showed larger 
scallops compared to anterior teeth. Differences in shape (circularity) were not 
statistically significant.
CONCLUSION: Our results suggest that teeth which are subject to higher 
masticatory loads (posterior teeth) show larger and more pronounced scallops. 
These findings might be of interest for improving other interfaces joining 
dissimilar materials.

Neonatal gut microbiota associates with childhood multisensitized atopy and T 
cell differentiation.

Fujimura KE(1), Sitarik AR(2), Havstad S(2), Lin DL(1), Levan S(1), Fadrosh 
D(1), Panzer AR(1), LaMere B(1), Rackaityte E(1), Lukacs NW(3), Wegienka G(2), 
Boushey HA(4), Ownby DR(5), Zoratti EM(6), Levin AM(2), Johnson CC(2), Lynch 
SV(1).

Author information:
(1)Division of Gastroenterology, Department of Medicine, University of 
California, San Francisco, San Francisco, California, USA.
(2)Department of Public Health Sciences, Henry Ford Health System, Detroit, 
Michigan, USA.
(3)Department of Pathology, University of Michigan Medical School, Ann Arbor, 
Michigan, USA.
(4)Pulmonary, Critical Care, Allergy and Sleep Medicine, Department of Medicine, 
University of California, San Francisco, San Francisco, California, USA.
(5)Section of Allergy-Immunology, Augusta University, Augusta, Georgia, USA. .
(6)Department of Internal Medicine, Division of Allergy and Immunology, Henry 
Ford Health System, Detroit, Michigan, USA.

Comment in
    Hepatobiliary Surg Nutr. 2017 Jun;6(3):190-192. doi: 
10.21037/hbsn.2017.03.05.

Gut microbiota bacterial depletions and altered metabolic activity at 3 months 
are implicated in childhood atopy and asthma. We hypothesized that 
compositionally distinct human neonatal gut microbiota (NGM) exist, and are 
differentially related to relative risk (RR) of childhood atopy and asthma. 
Using stool samples (n = 298; aged 1-11 months) from a US birth cohort and 16S 
rRNA sequencing, neonates (median age, 35 d) were divisible into three 
microbiota composition states (NGM1-3). Each incurred a substantially different 
RR for multisensitized atopy at age 2 years and doctor-diagnosed asthma at age 4 
years. The highest risk group, labeled NGM3, showed lower relative abundance of 
certain bacteria (for example, Bifidobacterium, Akkermansia and 
Faecalibacterium), higher relative abundance of particular fungi (Candida and 
Rhodotorula) and a distinct fecal metabolome enriched for pro-inflammatory 
metabolites. Ex vivo culture of human adult peripheral T cells with sterile 
fecal water from NGM3 subjects increased the proportion of CD4+ cells producing 
interleukin (IL)-4 and reduced the relative abundance of CD4+CD25+FOXP3+ cells. 
12,13-DiHOME, enriched in NGM3 versus lower-risk NGM states, recapitulated the 
effect of NGM3 fecal water on relative CD4+CD25+FOXP3+ cell abundance. These 
findings suggest that neonatal gut microbiome dysbiosis might promote CD4+ T 
cell dysfunction associated with childhood atopy.

DOI: 10.1038/nm.4176
PMCID: PMC5053876
PMID: 27618652 [Indexed for MEDLINE]

interests.


7. PLoS One. 2014 Jan 30;9(1):e87966. doi: 10.1371/journal.pone.0087966. 
eCollection 2014.

GNAS mutations identify a set of right-sided, RAS mutant, villous colon cancers.

Fecteau RE(1), Lutterbaugh J(2), Markowitz SD(3), Willis J(4), Guda K(5).

Author information:
(1)Department of Pathology, Case Western Reserve University and Case Medical 
Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United States of America.
(2)Case Comprehensive Cancer Center, Case Western Reserve University and Case 
Medical Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United States of America.
(3)Case Comprehensive Cancer Center, Case Western Reserve University and Case 
Medical Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United States of America ; Department of 
Medicine, Case Western Reserve University and Case Medical Center, Cleveland, 
Ohio, United States of America ; Department of Genetics and Genome Sciences, 
Case Western Reserve University and Case Medical Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United 
States of America.
(4)Department of Pathology, Case Western Reserve University and Case Medical 
Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United States of America ; Case Comprehensive Cancer 
Center, Case Western Reserve University and Case Medical Center, Cleveland, 
Ohio, United States of America.
(5)Case Comprehensive Cancer Center, Case Western Reserve University and Case 
Medical Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United States of America ; Division of General 
Medical Sciences-Oncology, Case Western Reserve University and Case Medical 
Center, Cleveland, Ohio, United States of America.

The purpose of this study is to determine the genetic frequency of GNAS 
activating mutations in colorectal cancer and the corresponding pathology of 
GNAS mutant tumors. Oncogenic mutations in GNAS have been described in a number 
of neoplasms including those of the pituitary, kidney, pancreas, and, more 
recently, in colon cancer. To ascertain the frequency in colon cancer we 
employed a sensitive pyrosequencing platform for mutation detection of the R201C 
and R201H GNAS hotspots in tumor samples representing all clinical stages. We 
additionally assayed for KRAS and BRAF mutations as previous reports have shown 
that these often co-occur with activating GNAS mutations. Of the 428 colon 
tumors assayed, mutations in GNAS were present in 10 of the samples (2.3%), 
indicating this is a significant, albeit infrequent, mutation in colorectal 
tumors. Nine GNAS mutant tumors (90%) harbored concomitant activating mutations 
in either the KRAS or BRAF oncogene, which was significantly greater than the 
mutation frequency of these genes in the tumor population (56%, p<0.0305). All 
ten of the GNAS mutant tumors arose in the right (proximal) colon (p<0.007), and 
7 of 8 reviewed cases exhibited a marked villous morphology. Taken together, 
these data indicate that GNAS mutant colon tumors commonly have synchronous 
mutations in KRAS or BRAF, are right-sided in location, and are associated with 
a villous morphology.

8. Am J Hum Biol. 2009 May-Jun;21(3):326-36. doi: 10.1002/ajhb.20863.

Searching for the origin of Gagauzes: inferences from Y-chromosome analysis.

Varzari A(1), Kharkov V, Stephan W, Dergachev V, Puzyrev V, Weiss EH, Stepanov 
V.

Author information:
(1)Biocentre, Ludwig-Maximilians University Munich, Munich, Germany. 
a_varsahr@yahoo.com

The Gagauzes are a small Turkish-speaking ethnic group living mostly in southern 
Moldova and northeastern Bulgaria. The origin of the Gagauzes is obscure. They 
may be descendants of the Turkic nomadic tribes from the Eurasian steppes, as 
suggested by the "Steppe" hypothesis, or have a complex Anatolian-steppe origin, 
as postulated by the "Seljuk" or "Anatolian" hypothesis. To distinguish these 
hypotheses, a sample of 89 Y-chromosomes representing two Gagauz populations 
from the Republic of Moldova was analyzed for 28 binary and seven STR 
polymorphisms. In the gene pool of the Gagauzes a total of 15 Y-haplogroups were 
identified, the most common being I-P37 (20.2%), R-M17 (19.1%), G-M201 (13.5%), 
R-M269 (12.4%), and E-M78 (11.1%). The present Gagauz populations were compared 
with other Balkan, Anatolian, and Central Asian populations by means of genetic 
distances, nonmetric multidimentional scaling and analyses of molecular 
variance. The analyses showed that Gagauzes belong to the Balkan populations, 
suggesting that the Gagauz language represents a case of language replacement in 
southeastern Europe. Interestingly, the detailed study of microsatellite 
haplotypes revealed some sharing between the Gagauz and Turkish lineages, 
providing some support of the hypothesis of the "Seljuk origin" of the Gagauzes. 
The faster evolving microsatellite loci showed that the two Gagauz samples 
investigated do not represent a homogeneous group. This finding matches the 
cultural and linguistic heterogeneity of the Gagauzes well, suggesting a crucial 
role of social factors in shaping the Gagauz Y-chromosome pool and possibly also 
of effects of genetic drift.

(c) 2008 Wiley-Liss, Inc.

DOI: 10.1002/ajhb.20863
PMID: 19107901 [Indexed for MEDLINE]
