# Netflix *Babies* (2020) and the infant-science literature: fact sheet for a dense song

Compiled 2026-10-16. Reliability flags: **[solid]** = primary paper or primary source checked; **[contested]** = real evidence but disputed, under-replicated, or a secondary-source number; **[pop]** = pop-science-only, no primary support, or an on-air gloss that goes beyond the paper; **[unverified]** = could not confirm.
Part 1 episode content below is taken from the actual episode audio (Netflix posted five full episodes on YouTube: Love, Crawling, First Words, Sleep, First Steps). "First Food" and all of Part 2 are reconstructed from press releases, lab pages and reviews; gaps are marked.

---

## 0. Series facts

- Produced by Nutopia for Netflix. Executive producer Jane Root (Nutopia CEO); series producer / EP Sara Brailsford. 12 episodes of 46–54 min in two parts: Part 1 (Love, First Food, Crawling, First Words, Sleep, First Steps) released 21 Feb 2020; Part 2 (What Babies Know, Movement, Senses, Relationships, Nature and Nurture, Toddlers) released 19 Jun 2020. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babies_(American_TV_series) ; https://media.netflix.com/en/only-on-netflix/80117833 **[solid]**
- Opening title cards (verbatim): "15 BABIES / FILMED OVER ONE YEAR / 36 WORLD RENOWNED SCIENTISTS / REVEALING HOW WE ALL BEGIN." Nutopia says the whole project was "filmed over the course of three years." Netflix's educational guide: 36 scientists "mostly from Europe, the U.S. and Israel." https://digbi.net/wp-content/uploads/Babies-Educational-Resource-Netflix.pdf **[solid]**
- Part 1 reached Netflix's Top 10 for several weeks (Northwestern release; WSU says it "rose to number seven"). https://news.northwestern.edu/stories/2020/02/netflix-babies-docu-series-features-northwestern-psychology-professor-susan-hespos **[solid]**
- Opening narration (Ep 1, from the audio): Andre Fenton: "As a scientist and a new parent, I realize that babies are born totally helpless. But they're going to grow up to be masters of the universe. How does that happen?" Closing question of Ep 1: "One of the great puzzles is, what's it like to be a baby?" Another: "Isn't it amazing that the answer to what it means to be human lies in the smallest, youngest creatures?" **[solid, transcript]**
- Families followed (names as spoken on air): Willow (London; parents Rachel and Adam; big sister Lily); Pascoe (London; Amelia and Rich; Rich is the stay-at-home "primary carer"; Pascoe never crawled); Dakota (US; Destiny, a realtor, and Shawn); Eric (two dads, Isaac and Josh, via surrogate AJ in Canada, where paid surrogacy is illegal); Nelson (London; Morning-Star and Charlie); Mateo (Stacy); twins Kiki and Yaya (Belinda and Dayo); twins Eloisa and Augusto (Agustina; Spanish-speaking; Eloisa 2.7 kg at birth, Augusto under 2 kg and 10 days in an incubator); bilingual family Natasha (English) and Adrien (French) with Lola and baby Hugo; Penelope (Merced, CA; wears a LENA recorder to the zoo). **[solid, transcript]**
- Scientists on the brief's lead list who do NOT appear anywhere in accessible sources: Patricia Kuhl, Maria Gloria Dominguez-Bello, Charles Nelson, Elizabeth Spelke, Fei Xu, Michael Tomasello, Dan Fessler, Celeste Kidd. The microbiome scientist is Susan Lynch, not Dominguez-Bello; the language scientists are Hirsh-Pasek, Saffran and Jarvis, not Kuhl. **[solid negative, within the limits of available sources]**

---

## 1. Episode by episode

### Ep 1 "Love" (Part 1)
Netflix: "The biology of bonding unravels to reveal how caregiving, social interaction and stress can physically change both parent and newborn."

- **Ruth Feldman** (IDC Herzliya, now Reichman University, Israel; joint appointment Yale Child Study Center). Began oxytocin work in 2001 with "about 80 mothers" sampled through pregnancy and the first postpartum month: oxytocin rises in pregnancy and stays high; mother–infant touch raises it in both. **[solid]**
- Then "80 couples": fathers' oxytocin was "identical" to mothers'. Paper: Gordon, Zagoory-Sharon, Leckman and Feldman 2010, *Biological Psychiatry* 68:377 — 160 first-time parents at 6 weeks and 6 months; no mother–father difference; mothers' OT tracks affectionate touch, fathers' tracks stimulatory play. On air: "Fatherhood is biological. It's as deep as motherhood." https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2010/08/100820101207.htm **[solid]**
- On air: the oxytocin surge at birth "activates a very primitive structure, the amygdala... once the mother's amygdala is open, it stays like this forever"; dad's amygdala response is "about a quarter of what you see in mom's." **[pop: on-air gloss of the 2014 paper]**
- "In 2010 we recruited... 48 gay couples" raising surrogacy-born infants from day one: primary-caregiving fathers "had amygdala activation just like mothers." Paper: Abraham et al. 2014, *PNAS* 111:9792 — 89 parents (20 primary-care mothers, 21 secondary-care fathers, 48 primary-care gay fathers); gay fathers showed mother-like amygdala plus father-like superior temporal sulcus activation; amygdala–STS connectivity scaled with hours of childcare (r = 0.33). On air: "It's a choice to be a parent to that infant." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4103311/ **[solid]**
- **Ed Tronick** (UMass Boston; Harvard Medical School). Joined Harvard psychology in 1968; a professor put an arm round him: "This emotion stuff is not what you want to be doing." Still-face experiment: mothers of 3–5-month-olds asked to "stop reacting to the baby for about two minutes"; babies smile, bid, eventually cry, then self-comfort (hands to mouth). Paper: Tronick, Als, Adamson, Wise and Brazelton 1978, *J Am Acad Child Psychiatry* 17:1 (PMID 632477). New version measures salivary cortisol: infants with positive parental experience are less stressed by the still face. **[solid for paradigm; cortisol result is described, not cited]**
- **Anne Rifkin-Graboi** (National Institute of Education, NTU Singapore) with imaging colleague **Anqi Qiu**: GUSTO cohort (Growing Up in Singapore Towards Healthy Outcomes); babies scanned in the first weeks and again at 6 months; 20 mother–infant pairs rated for responsiveness with a card-sort. Less responsive caregiving went with "a bit bigger" hippocampi, read as babies managing their own stress. "To my knowledge, that was the first time a group had seen this." **[contested: n = 20, counterintuitive direction, primary paper not located]**
- Family beats: Willow's birth opens the series; Destiny pumping ("I'm gonna stop at 55"); Josh at Eric's vaccination: "I don't know who is going to suffer more. Him or me."

### Ep 2 "First Food"
Netflix: "...how breast milk, iron and microbes can enrich, nourish and protect offspring." (Not on YouTube; roster from Notre Dame episode summary, Netflix media page, UCSF Lynch Lab, goodto.com.)

- **Katie Hinde** (Arizona State University; comparative lactation). Rhesus macaques: mothers of sons make richer, more energy-dense milk; daughters get more volume and a higher calcium:phosphorus ratio. Hinde 2009, *Am J Hum Biol* 21:512 (114 mothers; total milk energy equal); Hinde et al. 2013, *Am J Phys Anthropol* "Daughter dearest." https://doi.org/10.1002/ajhb.20917 **[solid]**
- Dairy cows favor daughters: 2.39 million lactations from 1.49 million Holsteins; ~1.3% more milk (~445 kg over two lactations) after a heifer calf. Hinde, Carpenter, Clay and Bradford 2014, *PLoS ONE* 9:e86169. https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0086169 **[solid]**
- Hinde's on-air lines (per summaries): "an individual mom makes individualized milk for an individual baby"; daily experiences leave "signatures" in milk; mothers can raise antibodies when they sense illness; best substitute is formula built from human-milk science, not cow's milk. Supporting lit: milk leukocytes jump from 0–2% to as high as 94% during infant or maternal infection (Hassiotou et al. 2013, *Clin Transl Immunol* 2:e3). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25505951/ **[solid]**
- **Michael Georgieff** (University of Minnesota, Institute of Child Development; neonatologist). Babies are born with about four months of iron stores; some mothers transfer less. Iron-sufficient newborns' EEG/ERPs distinguish mother's voice from a stranger's, iron-deficient newborns' do not (hippocampal memory). Siddappa et al. 2004, *Pediatr Res* 55:1034 — 32 newborns of diabetic mothers; cord ferritin ≤34 µg/L = brain-iron deficient. **[solid]** On-air claim that fixing micronutrient deficiencies would "shift the world's IQ positively by ten points." **[pop]**
- **Susan Lynch** (UCSF). "It's really important for babies to ingest microbes early in life." Babies living with dogs (less so cats) are better protected from asthma and allergy; dust from dog-owning homes restructures the mouse gut microbiome (Lactobacillus johnsonii) and blunts allergic airway disease (Fujimura et al. 2014, *PNAS* 111:805). One-month-old stool microbiota fall into three states; the highest-risk state (NGM3: low Bifidobacterium/Akkermansia/Faecalibacterium, more Candida) predicts multisensitized atopy at 2 and asthma at 4 (Fujimura et al. 2016, *Nat Med* 22:1187; 298 samples, median age 35 days). https://lynchlab.ucsf.edu/node/701 **[solid]**
- Also in episode (goodto.com): a mother's pregnancy diet shapes the baby's food preferences — taste and memory form before birth (Mennella; see Senses). Mother Mag adds: mothers produce more milk for a second child. **[solid / secondary]**

### Ep 3 "Crawling"
Netflix: "From how they grow to how they go, three scientists find out that crawling is much more than a transitional phase."

- **Marianne Barbu-Roth** (CNRS / Université de Paris, Integrative Neuroscience and Cognition Center). Trained as a nuclear physicist; a newborn is "an astronaut coming back from the moon... stuck with the gravity." About an hour after birth, skin-to-skin, newborns crawl to the breast; in water they "literally crawl." **[solid]**
- Optic flow: moving black dots projected under a suspended 3-day-old make it "swim in the air." Barbu-Roth et al. 2009, *Child Development* 80:8 — 48 three-day-olds; more air-steps to forward-translating flow than to rotating or static patterns; "a rudimentary coupling between optical flow and stepping is present at birth." On air: "this crawling pattern that a lot of people believe was just a reflex is not a reflex." **[solid]**
- A newborn's head is "one third of the weight of the body" (on air). **[pop: usually stated as ~1/4 of body length or ~1/3 of... check; the length figure is the standard one]**
- The "Crawlerskate" (published as the Crawliskate): a mini-skateboard supporting the trunk; first baby tested 2016 after "one or two years" of building; two-day-olds "even crossed the mattress once or two or three times." Forma et al. 2019, *Child Development*. "We are born moving arms and legs as quadrupeds... We are still quadrupeds inside us." Her metaphor: "the engine is sleeping and waiting for the bodywork to be ready." **[solid finding; quadruped framing is interpretation]**
- **Michelle Lampl** (Emory, Center for the Study of Human Health; MD-PhD anthropologist). 1979: measured her neighbor's baby: 62.7 cm one day, 64.5 cm the next, then 64.5 for days — "I'm really terrible at this." Growth charts are "connect-the-dots" from 882 children measured at 1, 2, 4, 6, 8 weeks and then monthly. Set out to measure 30 babies; notebook for 23 Aug 1981: 12 children measured, first house 7:20 a.m., last left 10 p.m. **[solid, transcript]**
- 19 Feb 1986: a baby measured 59.8, 59.8, 59.8, then 60.8 cm — one centimetre in one day. On air: daily-measured babies grew "between one half of a centimeter and 1.65 centimeters in one 24-hour period," separated by "two to twenty-eight days of no growth." Paper: Lampl, Veldhuis and Johnson 1992, *Science* 258:801 — 31 infants (10 weekly, 18 semiweekly, 3 daily) over 21 months; saltations 0.5–2.5 cm; stasis 2–63 days; "90 to 95 percent of normal development during infancy is growth-free." https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1439787/ **[solid, but critics attribute part of it to measurement error; see Science 1995 exchange — contested]**
- Growth days coincided with "changes in their sleeping behavior, tantrums, and times of insatiable hunger." Follow-up: Lampl and Johnson 2011, *Sleep* 34:641 — 23 infants, 5,798 diary days; length spurts follow sleep bursts (odds up a median 43% per extra sleep episode, 20% per extra hour). Head circumference is also saltatory (Lampl et al. 2011, *Early Hum Dev* 87:361; 40 infants, 2,616 measurements). **[solid, small n]**
- The paper made the front page of the New York Times and Washington Post; at a conference "a leading figure in the field stood up and started by mocking the data." Filmed May 2019 at Emory and a family's home. https://news.emory.edu/stories/2020/02/emory-professor-featured-new-netflix-documentary-series-babies **[solid]**
- **Karen Adolph** (NYU Infant Action Lab). Studied fine art in college; a professor handed her a plant: "Draw this." Adjustable slope apparatus raised "six degrees at a time" to a 90-degree drop-off. Grant (10 months, crawling about a month) "approached the 90-degree drop-off the same way as... the shallower slopes" and went over the edge; Marlow stopped at the brink; Dax switched to a bear crawl. "It takes about 20 weeks of crawling experience before a baby is showing adult-like accuracy." **[solid, transcript; cf. Adolph 1997 Monographs SRCD 62(3)]**
- Diary study of her daughter Lily: 19 March combat crawl, never seen again; 28 March belly crawl, not again until 1 April. "Skills are sort of appearing and disappearing and reappearing." "The whole idea of motor milestones... it just really doesn't happen like that." "Crawling isn't mandatory... if your baby finds another solution for getting around, like bum-shuffling, great." Lit: Adolph, Vereijken and Denny 1998, *Child Dev* 69:1299 — 28 infants; 15 belly-crawled first, 13 skipped straight to hands-and-knees. **[solid]**
- Family beat: Pascoe hates tummy time, never crawls, goes straight to cruising; Grandma on Skype: "It's terribly important for them to crawl."

### Ep 4 "First Words"
Netflix: "Experts examine the rhythm and flow of language and explore how babies break down linguistic beats before they know how to speak."

- **Kathy Hirsh-Pasek** (Temple University Infant Language Lab; Brookings). Was "headed toward being a musician"; her aha moment was a 3-year-old at a pool complaining "with the sophisticated grammar... of an adult." Experiment: a Cinderella passage with 1-second pauses placed either at clause boundaries or mid-clause, played from left and right speakers; babies turned faster and looked longer toward the natural version. Paper: Hirsh-Pasek et al. 1987, *Cognition* 26:269, "Clauses are perceptual units for young infants" (7–10-month-olds). "40 years ago... there was nobody, literally no one in the world, who I could find who was doing music and language together." **[solid]**
- "At just two days of age... babies recognize classes of language. They know, for example, that English and some of the other Germanic languages kinda sound alike." (Rhythm-class discrimination in newborns: Nazzi, Bertoncini and Mehler 1998, *JEP:HPP* 24:756; Mehler et al. 1988, *Cognition* 29:143.) "At the end of the first year, these babies have kinda cracked the code of what the units of language are." **[solid]**
- **Jenny Saffran** (University of Wisconsin–Madison Infant Learning Lab). Origin story: her mother studied brain damage; a patient lost every noun except "shopping center" ("The shopping center went to the shopping center to buy the shopping center"). Made-up language with words like "pabeecoo" and "golatoo" in a synthesized stream; babies listen for 2 minutes and hear each word "like 45 times," then turn longer toward non-words (boredom with the learned words). Paper: Saffran, Aslin and Newport 1996, *Science* 274:1926 — 8-month-olds; transitional probability 1.0 within words vs 0.33 across. https://psych.wisc.edu/news/saffrans-infant-learning-lab-featured-on-netflix-docuseries-babies/ **[solid]**
- Word meaning by cross-situational elimination ("doggie" with a stick and a bone, later a ball and a shoe). "By the end of the first year of life they understand somewhere between 10 and 50 words on average... most of them are not saying any words yet." Bilingual babies sort "two soups" by the "musical properties" (rhythm, pitch) of each language (cf. Byers-Heinlein, Burns and Werker 2010, *Psychol Sci* 21:343). **[solid]**
- **Erich Jarvis** (Rockefeller University; HHMI). Trained as a dancer, "six hours a day"; chose science at 18 in 1988 at his senior concert. Vocal learners are "three birds and five mammals: the hummingbirds, the parrots, songbirds, elephants, bats, dolphins, seals, and human" (Jarvis 2019, *Science* 366:50). Began in 1989 imaging singing birds' brains; "eight years later, an accidental discovery": hopping and wing-flapping lit up regions right next to the song nuclei, giving the "motor theory of vocal learning origin" (Feenders et al. 2008, *PLoS ONE* 3:e1768). "Think of spoken language as learning how to coordinate your body... Just like learning how to walk, speech comes with a delay." **[solid]**
- 2012: humans carry an extra copy of SRGAP2 that "maintains extra connections in the entire human brain." Dennis et al. 2012, *Cell* 149:912 — SRGAP2 duplicated ~3.4, ~2.4 and ~1 million years ago; the SRGAP2C copy slows dendritic-spine maturation, leaving denser connections (Charrier et al. 2012, *Cell* 149:923). Jarvis's link to language is his proposal. **[solid gene story; language link is a hypothesis]**
- Family beats: Nelson goes from "non-existent" to counting to six and "Red means stop"; Willow says "Dada"; Pascoe says "no, no" "with a bit of sass"; Lola translates Papa's French for Mum.

### Ep 5 "Sleep"
Netflix: "Researchers delve into sleep, a dynamic time for babies, with studies on twitching, apps and naps."

- **Andre Fenton** (NYU Center for Neural Science). "Getting your baby to sleep through the night turns out to be a universal human challenge." "A newborn baby is spending 16 hours a day sleeping." Became a sleep researcher because he "couldn't figure out how to get my daughter to sleep through the night." Founded the NYU Baby Sleep Study with baby-tracking app developers; parents log to the minute, error "no more than five minutes." **[solid, transcript; the 16-hour figure is high: Galland 2012 meta-analysis gives 14.6 h at 0–2 months — see Sleep theme]**
- **Pascal Wallisch** (NYU): "about 1,000 babies... six million events... about 1.5 million sleep events, 2.5 million eating events, and 1.5 million diaper change events." At 1 month, eating and sleeping are random; "within about four months an initial cycle is emerging"; at 8 months "a hint of a pattern"; "within a year, this clear pattern emerges": sleep after the morning feed, again ~5 hours later, and through the night. "We are not comfortable to publish this yet." Fenton: the no-pattern-to-pattern transition "is something that almost all babies do. And it'll take about a year." **[unverified: unpublished at filming]**
- **Mark Blumberg** (University of Iowa). "I've always been a terrible sleeper... we study the things that we're bad at." Baby rats and humans cycle sleep–wake "hundreds of" times in 24 hours. Dye tracing: at birth the hypothalamic master clock has no wiring to brainstem sleep-wake centers; a week later (rats) it does. In humans, "prior to three to four months, there's very little you can do to help babies sleeping throughout the night." **[solid, transcript; Rivkees 2003 Pediatrics: circadian rhythms emerge after ~2 months]**
- Twitching: his dog Katie; the "hundreds of years, if not millennia" view that twitches are "a byproduct of dreams." EEG caps on infants asleep in car seats: "This baby's a champion twitcher." Submarine metaphor: a twitch is a sonar ping; "A single twitch can produce activity in hundreds, probably thousands, of neurons... you're mapping your body." Lit: Blumberg, Marques and Iida 2013, *Curr Biol* 23:R532; Sokoloff et al. 2021, *Curr Biol* (22 infants; twitches in quiet sleep from ~3 months, time-locked to sleep spindles). Lab page: "highlighted in Episode 5." https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/node/41 **[solid]**
- **Rebecca Spencer** (UMass Amherst SomneuroLab). "I'm a lark." Postdoc at UC Berkeley in the mid-2000s when sleep-and-memory work took off. Infant study: 9-month-olds shown eight toys, four with unusual actions; half nap, half kept awake; nappers reproduce more actions ("three target objects"). Sleep spindles fire "simultaneously across the brain," moving memories from hippocampus to cortex; "in an infant, their hippocampus is smaller. So babies need to sleep in multiple sleep bouts, two naps and that overnight sleep." Landmark paper: Kurdziel, Duclos and Spencer 2013, *PNAS* 110:17267 — 40 preschoolers, ~78-min classroom naps, ~10% more recall after napping, spindle density r = 0.65 with memory gain. Filmed March 2019. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3808582 **[solid; infant result was unpublished at filming]**
- Fenton's closer: "It's solving one of the hardest problems in the universe. We should let them sleep."
- Family beats: Adam bets Rachel £50 he can handle both kids alone (he loses); twins Kiki and Yaya "just refuse to sleep at night"; Pascoe later sleeps "till, like, 6:30–7:00" and drops to two naps.

### Ep 6 "First Steps"
Netflix: "...walking changes a baby's world and unveil findings on neonatal reflexes, skeletal development and talking."

- **Nadia Dominici** (Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam; Italian physicist turned neurophysiologist). Newborn stepping: "You support about 70% of the baby's weight and you put its feet on a flat surface... one leg slowly in front of the other." 2010 study at the Santa Lucia Foundation, Rome, with Sant'Eugenio Hospital's neonatal unit: EMG and motion sensors on 46 newborns (2–3 days old) stepping on a treadmill, compared with 10 toddlers. **[solid, transcript]**
- Finding: newborn stepping needs only two muscle-activation patterns (weight support and swing); toddlers keep both and add two (touchdown and push-off). Dominici et al. 2011, *Science* 334:997 — same primitives in rat, cat, macaque and guineafowl. "The baby is born with an innate walking skill." https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22096202/ **[solid]**
- **Libby Cowgill** (University of Missouri; biological anthropologist, powerlifter and CrossFit instructor; her grandfather did a gymnastics routine on top of the Empire State Building). In 2006 she visited "about 15 different museums" across Europe, Africa, the US and the Middle East carrying "about 130 pounds of scientific equipment on my back," collecting cross-sections of leg bones from over 600 infants and children, from the 20th century back to 250,000 years ago; she developed the X-ray films by hand. **[solid, transcript]**
- Adult femur cross-section is "an upside-down teardrop"; toddlers' is "a wide oval" that normalizes "by the time they're around five or six." Reviewer 2 on her first paper: "Children's bones don't respond to exercise." With **Cara Ocobock** (Notre Dame), motion capture plus force plates on living toddlers ("Mabel is our all-star walker"): toddlers' legs go straight down, a wide base, and more side-to-side ground reaction force, so bone is laid down on the sides. Cowgill, Warrener, Pontzer and Ocobock 2010, *Am J Phys Anthropol* 143:52. https://anthropology.missouri.edu/news/prof-libby-cowgill-featured-netflix-series-babies **[solid]**
- **Eric Walle** (UC Merced Interpersonal Development Lab). As a grad student he gave parents a language survey "primarily just to distract" them during an emotion experiment; the accidental result: "walking infants had significantly larger vocabulary sizes... regardless of their age." Walle and Campos 2014, *Dev Psychol* 50:336; replicated in Chinese infants (He, Walle and Campos 2015, *Infancy* 20:283). **[solid; all parent-report measures]**
- LENA recorders in ~20 homes: "at 10:50, the child heard 374 adult words" in a five-minute block. Walkers and crawlers hear the same number of words and vocalize equally; the difference is parents' responses ("Du..." "Duck." "Duck!"). "Walking is a complete game changer for the infant." https://idlab.ucmerced.edu/node/81 **[solid; a later follow-up found 13-month crawlers knew as many words as 13-month walkers — contested]**
- Family beats: Augusto, the smaller twin, bum-jumps for months, then "just stood up and kept on walking"; Nelson becomes "a little Speedy Gonzales"; Amelia drops the camera when Pascoe walks.

### Ep 7 "What Babies Know" (Part 2)
Netflix: "Disputing the notion of a blank slate, three studies explore how babies are born with expectations about everyday life."

- **Susan Hespos** (Northwestern Infant Cognition Lab; now Western Sydney University MARCS). Babies look about twice as long at a block hanging in midair as at one supported ("They know this stuff way earlier than we thought"); a toddler trying one round object on its head, then another, is grouping by sameness ("a really cool, uniquely human ability"). Lit: 5-month-olds expect liquids to pour and solids to tumble (Hespos, Ferry and Rips 2009, *Psychol Sci* 20:603); 7- and 9-month-olds abstract same/different from four exemplars (Ferry, Hespos and Gentner 2015, *Child Dev* 86:1386; N = 64; the single-exemplar replication failed, N = 26). Wikipedia lists her as "Part 2, Episode 1"; lab filmed fall 2019. https://news.northwestern.edu/stories/2020/02/netflix-babies-docu-series-features-northwestern-psychology-professor-susan-hespos **[solid]**
- **Kang Lee** (OISE, University of Toronto). Filmed in his lab, on campus and in Toronto neighborhoods on "how babies learn about faces" and own-race bias in 6- and 9-month-olds. Lit: 9- but not 6-month-olds pair own-race faces with happy music and other-race faces with sad music (Xiao et al. 2018, *Dev Sci* 21:e12537); 7-month-olds (N = 96) follow own-race adults' gaze more when it is only 50% reliable (Xiao et al. 2018, *Child Dev* 89:e229). HuffPost's Part 2 item "babies can tell monkeys apart but adults can't" (3-month-olds distinguish individual monkey faces; by 1 year the skill is pruned) matches the classic Pascalis, de Haan and Nelson 2002, *Science* 296:1321 (6-month-olds discriminate monkey faces; 9-month-olds and adults do not). https://www.oise.utoronto.ca/about/news/07-02-20-oise-professor-kang-lee-makes-cameo-appearance-netflix-series **[solid]**
- **Rebecca Saxe** (MIT). Put her own infant son Percy, awake, in an MRI scanner adapted to protect his hearing and showed him a movie of happy and sad faces with scenes as baseline: "The biggest surprise was the similarity between baby and grown-up brains"; face and scene regions were in the same places; babies' face areas also light up for people they love. Lit: Deen et al. 2017, *Nat Commun* 8:13995 — 17 infants scanned, 9 usable, aged 4–6 months; Kosakowski et al. 2022, *Curr Biol* 32:265 — custom 32-channel infant coil, 2–9-month-olds show face-, scene- and body-selective responses in adult locations. Saxe first scanned her elder son Arthur at ~1 month in 2015 (~2-hour sessions for ~10 minutes of usable data). https://cbmm.mit.edu/news-events/news/5-mind-blowing-things-we-learned-netflixs-babies-part-two-huffington-post ; https://www.quantamagazine.org/infant-brains-reveal-how-the-mind-gets-built-20170110 **[solid appearance in Part 2; placement in this episode is probable, not confirmed]**

### Ep 8 "Movement"
Netflix: "From curiosity to understanding: researchers unveil the surprising ways that infants learn how to anticipate and respond to the movements of others."

- Scientists: **not identifiable** from any accessible source (no university release, review or credit list names them; IMDb credits were blocked). **[unverified roster]**
- Likely underlying literature for "anticipating others' movements": 12-month-olds, like adults, shift gaze proactively to the goal of another person's reach, 6-month-olds do not (Falck-Ytter, Gredebäck and von Hofsten 2006, *Nat Neurosci* 9:878); "sticky mittens" — 3-month-olds given Velcro mittens that let them grab learn to read others' reaches as goal-directed (Sommerville, Woodward and Needham 2005, *Cognition* 96:B1); infants encode reaching as goal-directed by 6 months (Woodward 1998, *Cognition* 69:1). **[solid lit; episode attribution unknown]**

### Ep 9 "Senses"
Netflix: "Several experts make sense of how babies touch, taste and see as they embark on a sensory journey that will shape the rest of their lives."

- **Julie Mennella** (Monell Chemical Senses Center) is named in Part 2 coverage on food preferences; placement in "Senses" (taste) is inferred. Lit: 46 pregnant women drank 300 mL carrot juice four days a week for three weeks in late pregnancy or early lactation; at ~5.7 months their infants made fewer negative faces to carrot-flavored cereal (Mennella, Jagnow and Beauchamp 2001, *Pediatrics* 107:e88); garlic flavors milk within 2 hours and babies suck longer (Mennella and Beauchamp 1991, *Pediatrics* 88:737). https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1351272/ **[solid lit; episode placement inferred]**
- Vision: a reviewer was "surprised by how limited babies' vision is at birth" (newborn acuity ~20/400 or worse, fixed focus ~19 cm; see Senses theme). Touch and vision scientists: **unidentified**. Saxe's face-region work may sit here instead of Ep 7. **[unverified roster]**

### Ep 10 "Relationships"
Netflix: "A coy smile, a puppet show and a pointed finger lead to discoveries in how babies get along with others using humor, morality and shared experiences."

- Coy smile: **Vasudevi (Vasu) Reddy** (University of Portsmouth), named in Part 2 coverage: babies use teasing, laughter and smiling to connect with adults. Lit: coy smiles (gaze aversion with smiling, arm to face) in 2–3-month-olds meeting their mirror image or an adult (Reddy 2000, *Dev Sci* 3:186, "Coyness in early infancy"); "infant clowns" and teasing from ~8 months (Reddy 1991/2001); book *How Infants Know Minds* (2008). https://www.goodto.com/entertainment/netflix-babies-everything-to-know-529458 **[solid lit; episode placement inferred from "coy smile"]**
- Puppet show: **Kiley Hamlin** (UBC Centre for Infant Cognition). Infants watch a character helped or hindered and are then offered both; "babies seem to respond in ways that show greater preference for helpers over harmers." UBC: her "developmental research and her personal journey of discovery" feature in "Relationships." Lit: Hamlin, Wynn and Bloom 2007, *Nature* 450:557 (6- and 10-month-olds); 3-month-olds by looking time, 10 of 12 prefer the helper (Hamlin, Wynn and Bloom 2010, *Dev Sci* 13:923); 8-month-olds prefer a puppet that punishes the antisocial puppet (Hamlin et al. 2011, *PNAS* 108:19931); "Not like me = bad" — 200 nine- and 14-month-olds choose graham crackers or green beans, then favor puppets who harm the opposite-taste puppet (Hamlin et al. 2013, *Psychol Sci*). https://ubyssey.ca/science/netflix-babies **[solid appearance]**
- Replication status: the climber's "bounce" drives choices (Scarf et al. 2012, *PLoS ONE* 7:e42698); Salvadori et al. 2015 found 62.5% and 50% helper choice (chance); **ManyBabies 4** (Lucca et al. 2025, *Dev Sci* 28:e13581): 1,018 infants tested, 567 included, 37 labs — 49.3% chose the helper. The helper preference is now **[contested]**.
- Pointed finger: **Malinda Carpenter** (University of St Andrews, Baby and Child "ABC" Lab). Netflix "came to St Andrews to film the lab"; Wikipedia: "series 2 episode 4 Relationships... featured Carpenter's team's studies." Lit: joint attention, gaze following and pointing emerge 9–15 months in 24 infants followed longitudinally (Carpenter, Nagell and Tomasello 1998, *Monographs SRCD* 63(4)); 12-month-olds point declaratively to share attention and interest and stop when the adult ignores the referent (Liszkowski et al. 2004, *Dev Sci* 7:297). https://developmentlab.wp.st-andrews.ac.uk/netflix-babies/ **[solid]**

### Ep 11 "Nature and Nurture"
Netflix: "Resolving the debate of nature versus nurture, three scientists show how genetics and environment work together as infants discover who they are."

- **Maria (Masha) Gartstein** (Washington State University; co-author of the Infant Behavior Questionnaire-Revised). WSU release names the episode: babies show lower fear at 6 months when parents read and respond to their cues. Lit: Gartstein, Hancock and Iverson 2018, *Child Dev* 89:1519 — 148 mother–infant dyads; play observed at 4 months; fear measured at 6, 8, 10, 12 months; maternal responsiveness predicted lower observed fear. https://news.wsu.edu/news/2020/06/17/wsu-psychologist-appear-netflix-series-babies-friday/ **[solid]**
- **Philippe Rochat** (Emory Infant Lab) — self-awareness. His lab's media page lists this episode (the page itself would not load; attribution via search snippet). Likely content: mirror self-recognition emerges ~18–24 months (rouge test: ~65% pass at 20–24 months; Amsterdam 1972, *Dev Psychobiol* 5:297); Rochat's five levels of self-awareness (Rochat 2003, *Conscious Cogn* 12:717); 4- vs 9-month-olds telling self from other in a mirror (Rochat and Striano 2002, *Child Dev* 73:35). https://scholarblogs.emory.edu/rochatlab/media-coverage/ **[appearance probable; study unverified]**
- Third scientist: **unidentified**. **[unverified]**

### Ep 12 "Toddlers"
Netflix: "Researchers reveal the social skills that toddlers naturally possess, abilities that ultimately separate humans from all other species."

- **Felix Warneken** (University of Michigan; formerly MPI Leipzig and Harvard). On camera he "clumsily knocks a cup off a table" and a toddler walks over to hand it back. Lit: Warneken and Tomasello 2006, *Science* 311:1301 — 24 eighteen-month-olds helped in 10 situations (out-of-reach objects, cabinets, wrong results); MPG: "virtually all children helped at least once," and 84% of helps came within 10 seconds, before eye contact; three human-raised chimpanzees helped only with reaching. Rewards backfire: 20-month-olds given a toy for helping later helped less (Warneken and Tomasello 2008, *Dev Psychol* 44:1785). https://michigantoday.umich.edu/2020/07/23/children-show-altruism-at-a-young-age **[solid]**
- **Laura Schulz** (MIT Early Childhood Cognition Lab): CBMM confirms the series "features CBMM PI Laura Schulz and her research with toddlers." Most likely study: 15-month-olds who watch an adult struggle before succeeding try harder on a new toy (Leonard, Lee and Schulz 2017, *Science* 357:1290; N = 262 across three experiments). **[appearance solid; study inferred]**
- Reviews mention toddlers understanding "when they understand desires" and "other people's minds and needs" (cf. Repacholi and Gopnik 1997, *Dev Psychol* 33:12 — 18-month-olds hand over the broccoli an adult prefers; 14-month-olds hand over crackers). Third scientist: **unidentified**. **[unverified]**

---

## 2. Literature greatest hits, by theme

### Sleep
- Newborns (0–2 months) sleep a mean 14.6 h/day (range 9.3–20.0); 3 months 13.6; 6 months 12.9; 12 months 12.9. The textbook "16–18 h" is the upper tail. Galland et al. 2012, *Sleep Med Rev* (34 studies). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21784676/ **[solid]**
- Half of newborn sleep is REM (about a third of the whole day); REM falls to ~20–25% by age 3 and stays there; at 30 weeks' gestation REM is ~80%. Roffwarg, Muzio and Dement 1966, *Science* 152:604 (the "ontogenetic hypothesis": REM builds the brain). https://doi.org/10.1126/science.152.3722.604 **[solid]**
- Newborns fall asleep straight into REM: ~60% of sleep onsets at 3 weeks vs 20% at 6 months; adults enter via NREM. Sleep spindles appear at ~2 months (Jenni et al. 2004). https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9602694/ **[solid]**
- Circadian rhythms switch on after ~2 months (Rivkees 2003, *Pediatrics* 112:373). **[solid]**
- "Sleeping through the night" is a minority skill: at 6 months only 62.4% manage 6 unbroken hours and 43.0% manage 8; at 12 months 72.1% and 56.6%; no link to later development. Pennestri et al. 2018, *Pediatrics* 142:e20174330 (388 infants). **[solid]**
- Night wakings: 0–3.4 per night at 0–2 months, 0–2.5 at 1–2 years; longest stretch grows ~39 min per month in the first half-year. Galland 2012. **[solid]**
- Sleep regressions at 4/8/12 months: no peer-reviewed support for discrete universal regressions; app data from 841 children and 156,989 logged sessions show smooth consolidation (Mindell et al. 2016). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26987761/ **[pop]**
- Naps consolidate preschool memory ~10%; spindle density predicts the gain (r = 0.65); only habitual nappers benefit. Kurdziel, Duclos and Spencer 2013, *PNAS*. **[solid]**
- Sleep's job flips at ~2.4 years from neural reorganization (REM-heavy) to repair, in a model fit to 60+ datasets. Cao et al. 2020, *Sci Adv* 6:eaba0398. **[solid, model-based]**
- Sleep twitches are the brain pinging its own body map; Blumberg's informal estimate: limbs twitch "at least 40,000 times a day." https://now.uiowa.edu/node/37661 **[solid mechanism; 40,000 is an informal estimate]**
- Sleep training (graduated extinction, bedtime fading) in 43 infants: faster sleep onset, lower cortisol, no attachment or behavior differences at 12 months (Gradisar et al. 2016, *Pediatrics*); 5-year follow-up of 326 children: no harms, no benefits (Price et al. 2012, *Pediatrics*). **[solid, small samples]**

### Crying
- Crying rises to a peak around 6 weeks (Brazelton 1962, *Pediatrics* 29:579: ~2¼ h/day through week 7, 80 diaries); meta-analysis of 8,690 infants: 117–133 min/day in weeks 1–6, ~68 min at 10–12 weeks; individual range 30 min to over 5 hours. Wolke, Bilgin and Samara 2017, *J Pediatr* 185:55. **[solid]**
- Colic by country: Denmark 5.5% and Germany 6.7% at 3–4 weeks; UK 28% at 1–2 weeks, Canada 34.1% at 3–4 weeks, Italy 20.9% at 8–9 weeks. Wolke 2017. **[solid]**
- Wessel's rule of threes (1954, *Pediatrics* 14:421): crying more than 3 h/day, 3 days/week, for 3 weeks. Prevalence 1.5–40% depending on definition. **[definition solid; prevalence contested]**
- Shaken-baby hospitalizations track the crying curve, peaking at 10–13 weeks (273 California cases); basis of Barr's "Period of PURPLE Crying." Barr, Trent and Cross 2006, *Child Abuse Negl* 30:7. **[solid]**
- Newborns cry in their mother tongue: 30 French and 30 German 2–5-day-olds, 1,254 cries — French cries rise in pitch, German cries fall. Mampe et al. 2009, *Curr Biol* 19:1994. **[solid, single study]**
- Newborns do make tears: term newborns' basal tear production (Schirmer 9.2 mm) is adult-like (Isenberg et al. 1998, *Arch Ophthalmol* 116:773). The "no tears until 1–3 months" claim is folklore. **[pop claim contradicted by data]**
- Contagious crying: newborns cry to another newborn's cry but not to a recording of their own (Martin and Clark 1982, *Dev Psychol* 18:3). **[solid]**

### Feeding
- Day-one colostrum totals about 15 mL (three teaspoons): 1.5 ± 1.1 g per feed, 15 ± 11 g/day in 90 newborns. Santoro et al. 2010, *J Pediatr* 156:29. **[solid]**
- Newborn stomach capacity is ~20 mL, and the viral cherry/walnut/egg poster is unsourced folklore (Bergman 2013, *Acta Paediatr* 102:773; Spangler et al. 2008, *J Hum Lact* 24:199). **[contested / pop]**
- Milk has a clock: melatonin is undetectable by day and peaks near midnight (~47 pg/mL); cortisol peaks in the morning; fat peaks in the evening. Italianer et al. 2020, *Nutrients* 12:2328 (71 components). **[solid]**
- Milk leukocytes: 13–70% of cells in colostrum, 0–2% in mature milk, surging to as high as 94% when mother or baby is infected. Hassiotou et al. 2013. **[solid, small n]**
- Flavor learning starts in the womb: 300 mL carrot juice, 4 days/week for 3 weeks in pregnancy made babies like carrot cereal at ~5.7 months. Mennella, Jagnow and Beauchamp 2001, *Pediatrics* 107:e88. **[solid]**
- Taste at birth: sweet relaxes, sour purses, bitter gapes; salt is ignored until a preference switches on at ~4 months (Beauchamp, Cowart and Moran 1986, *Dev Psychobiol* 19:17). The "10,000 taste buds" figure is folklore; counts run ~2,000–8,000. **[solid / contested]**
- Birth seeds the microbiome: vaginally born babies carry Lactobacillus/Prevotella like their mother's vagina, C-section babies carry Staphylococcus/Corynebacterium like skin (Dominguez-Bello et al. 2010, *PNAS* 107:11971; 10 newborns). Baby Biome Study: Bacteroides abundant in 51% of vaginally born but low or absent in 99.6% of 282 C-section babies; differences largely gone by 1 year (Shao et al. 2019, *Nature* 574:117; 771 babies). **[solid]**
- Early peanut cuts allergy: 640 high-risk infants randomized at 4–11 months; peanut allergy at age 5 was 13.7% with avoidance vs 1.9% with consumption (86% relative reduction). Du Toit et al. 2015, *NEJM* 372:803 (LEAP). **[solid]**
- Breastfeeding and IQ: Brazilian cohort of 3,493 adults — ≥12 months of breastfeeding predicted +3.76 IQ points and higher income at 30 (Victora et al. 2015, *Lancet Glob Health*); but sibling comparisons erase 10 of 11 claimed benefits (Colen and Ramey 2014). **[contested]**
- Hinde's cows: 1.49 million Holsteins, ~445 kg more milk over two lactations after daughters. Hinde et al. 2014. **[solid]**

### Movement
- Learning to walk: 12–19-month-olds average 2,368 steps and 17 falls per hour of free play, 701 m per hour (7.7 football fields); extrapolated to ~14,000 steps, ~46 football fields and ~100 falls a day. Adolph et al. 2012, *Psychol Sci* 23:1387 (151 infants). https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3591461/ **[solid; daily totals are an extrapolation]**
- No transfer across postures: experienced 12-month crawlers never attempt a 90-cm cliff, but 63% of 12-month novice walkers go over it; learning to avoid the cliff starts over with each new posture. Kretch and Adolph 2013, *Child Dev* 84:226. **[solid]**
- WHO milestone windows (1st–99th percentile, months): sitting unsupported 3.8–9.2; hands-and-knees crawling 5.2–13.5; walking alone 8.2–17.6; 4.3% of children never crawl on hands and knees. WHO MGRS 2006, *Acta Paediatr* Suppl 450:86 (816 children, 5 countries). **[solid]**
- Newborn stepping disappears by ~2–3 months because legs get heavy, not because the brain forgets: weights on the legs abolish it, submerging the legs in water brings it back (Thelen, Fisher and Ridley-Johnson 1984, *Infant Behav Dev* 7:479); 7-month-olds step again on a treadmill (Thelen 1986, *Child Dev* 57:1498). **[solid]**
- Newborn locomotor primitives: two muscle-activation patterns at birth, four in toddlers, shared with rat, cat, macaque and guineafowl. Dominici et al. 2011, *Science* 334:997. **[solid]**
- Palmar grasp: Louis Robinson (1891) hung "upward of sixty" newborns from a stick; nearly all held ≥10 s, record 2 min 35 s by a 3-week-old. *Popular Science Monthly*, March 1892. **[solid as Victorian report]**
- Reflex exit dates (StatPearls): Moro gone by ~6 months; palmar grasp ~6 months; rooting fades after 1 month; stepping ~2–3 months; Babinski 12–24 months. **[solid ranges]**
- Back to Sleep did not delay walking: 351 infants; prone sleepers rolled, sat and crawled earlier, but walked at the same age (Davis et al. 1998, *Pediatrics* 102:1135); SIDS fell more than 50% from 1994 to 2016 (NICHD). **[solid]**
- Culture: 64 rural Kenyan (Kipsigis) babies sat and walked early because adults deliberately trained them (Super 1976, *Dev Med Child Neurol* 18:561); Hopi cradleboards did not delay walking (Dennis and Dennis 1940). **[solid]**
- Growth spurts follow sleep bursts within 48 hours (Lampl and Johnson 2011, *Sleep*). **[solid, small n]**
- Baby walkers: each 24 hours in a walker delays walking ~3 days and standing ~4 days (Garrett 2002, *BMJ*); Canada banned them in April 2004, the first country to do so. **[solid]**

### Language
- Perceptual narrowing: 11 of 12 English-learning 6–8-month-olds discriminated Hindi retroflex/dental contrasts; 8 of 12 at 8–10 months; 2 of 10 at 10–12 months; only 3 of 10 English adults. Werker and Tees 1984, *Infant Behav Dev* 7:49. **[solid]**
- Social gating: 9-month-old Americans learned Mandarin contrasts from live tutors in 12 sessions (~5 hours; 65.7% vs 56.7% for controls) but learned nothing from the same tutors on DVD or audio. Kuhl, Tsao and Liu 2003, *PNAS* 100:9096. **[solid]**
- Statistical learning: 8-month-olds segment words from 2 minutes of monotone nonsense (transitional probability 1.0 within words, 0.33 across). Saffran, Aslin and Newport 1996, *Science* 274:1926. **[solid]**
- Parentese pays: the 11–14-month-olds who heard the most one-on-one parentese had 433 words at 24 months vs 169 for those who heard least (Ramírez-Esparza, García-Sierra and Kuhl 2014, *Dev Sci* 17:880); coaching parents at 6, 10 and 14 months raised 18-month vocabulary (~100 vs ~60 words; Ferjan Ramírez, Lytle and Kuhl 2020, *PNAS* 117:3484). **[solid]**
- The 30-million-word gap: Hart and Risley 1995 recorded 42 families one hour a month; 2,153 vs 616 words per hour by SES, extrapolated to a 30M gap by age 3. Not reproduced when all caregivers and overheard speech are counted (Sperry, Sperry and Miller 2019, *Child Dev* 90:1303); LENA data on 329 families put the gap nearer 4 million words by age 4 (Gilkerson et al. 2017). **[contested]**
- Processing speed: by 24 months lower-SES toddlers were ~6 months behind in real-time word recognition (Fernald, Marchman and Weisleder 2013, *Dev Sci* 16:234). **[solid]**
- Baby videos: each daily hour of baby DVDs went with 6–8 fewer understood words at 8–16 months (Zimmerman, Christakis and Meltzoff 2007, *J Pediatr* 151:364); Disney refunded Baby Einstein DVDs ($15.99 each, up to four) in October 2009; a month of DVD watching taught 12–18-month-olds no new words (DeLoache et al. 2010, *Psychol Sci* 21:1570). **[solid; causality contested]**
- Milestones: canonical babbling ~6 months; first words ~8–14 months; ~50 words at 18 months and 200–300 at 24 months are standard CDI norms (Fenson et al. 1994). **[solid range; exact medians unverified here]**
- The "vocabulary spurt" is rare: only 5 of 38 children fit a spurt curve better than steady acceleration (Ganger and Brent 2004, *Dev Psychol*). **[contested as a universal stage]**
- Pointing emerges ~12 months and predicts later language (meta-analytic r = .52 concurrent; Colonnesi et al. 2010, *Dev Rev*); declarative pointing is the human-specific part (Tomasello, Carpenter and Liszkowski 2007, *Child Dev* 78:705). **[solid; effect size contested]**
- Baby sign language teaches signs, not language: 40 infants followed 8–20 months, no vocabulary advantage (Kirk et al. 2013, *Child Dev* 84:574). **[solid]**
- Fast mapping: preschoolers who heard "the chromium tray, not the red one" once remembered the color word a week later (Carey and Bartlett 1978). **[solid]**
- Vocal learners across vertebrates: three bird groups and five mammal groups (Jarvis 2019, *Science*); Mozart K.448 raised college students' spatial scores for ~10–15 minutes (Rauscher 1993, *Nature*, 36 students) and never was tested on babies; a 40-study meta-analysis found "little evidence left" (Pietschnig et al. 2010); Georgia's Governor Zell Miller still budgeted $105,000 in 1998 for a classical CD per newborn. **[Mozart effect: debunked]**

### Cognition
- Drawbridge: 3.5–4.5-month-olds look longer when a screen rotates "through" a hidden box (Baillargeon 1987, *Dev Psychol* 23:655; Baillargeon, Spelke and Wasserman 1985, *Cognition* 20:191) — object permanence long before Piaget's 8–12 months. **[solid]**
- Number: 6-month-olds tell 8 from 16 dots but not 8 from 12 (Xu and Spelke 2000, *Cognition* 74:B1); 9-month-olds manage 8 vs 12 (Lipton and Spelke 2003); newborns 49 hours old match 4 vs 12 syllables to 4 vs 12 objects, 15 of 16 of them (Izard et al. 2009, *PNAS* 106:10382). **[solid]**
- Infant arithmetic: 5-month-olds look longer when 1 + 1 doll yields 1 (Wynn 1992, *Nature* 358:749). **[solid]**
- Intuitive statistics: 8-month-olds look longer (9.9 s vs 7.5 s) when 4 red balls are drawn from a box of 70 white and 5 red — unless the balls come from the experimenter's pocket (Xu and Garcia 2008, *PNAS* 105:5012). 15-month-olds reason from samples: 80% vs 33% tried the other toy depending on which box the squeaky balls came from (Gweon, Tenenbaum and Schulz 2010, *PNAS* 107:9066). **[solid]**
- Goldilocks effect: 7–8-month-olds look away from events that are too predictable and from events that are too surprising, a U-shape over 72 infants (Kidd, Piantadosi and Aslin 2012, *PLoS ONE* 7:e36399). **[solid]**
- Little scientists: 110 eleven-month-olds who saw a ball pass through a wall then banged the ball; those who saw it float dropped it — surprise targets learning (Stahl and Feigenson 2015, *Science* 348:91). **[solid]**
- Awake infant fMRI: face and scene areas present in 4–6-month-olds (9 of 17 scanned usable; Deen et al. 2017, *Nat Commun*). **[solid]**
- Memory: 9-month-olds imitate after 24 hours, 14-month-olds after a week (Meltzoff 1988); adults' earliest memories date to ~3–3.5 years; 5–7-year-olds recall over 60% of events from age 3, 8–9-year-olds under 40% (Bauer and Larkina 2014, *Memory* 22:907). **[solid]**
- Mirror self-recognition: ~65% at 20–24 months (Amsterdam 1972); but only 2 of 82 Kenyan children and 0% in Fiji touched the mark (Broesch et al. 2011), and 7% of ni-Vanuatu vs 68% of Canadian toddlers (Cebioğlu and Broesch 2021) — the test measures culture too. **[solid; interpretation contested]**
- Neonatal imitation: Meltzoff and Moore 1977 (*Science*, 6 infants aged 12–21 days) vs 106 newborns tested at 1, 3, 6 and 9 weeks with 11 models who imitated nothing (Oostenbroek et al. 2016, *Curr Biol* 26:1334). **[contested, leaning debunked]**
- Persistence is contagious: 15-month-olds who watched an adult struggle tried harder (Leonard, Lee and Schulz 2017, *Science* 357:1290; N = 262). Marshmallow test effect halves in a 900-child replication (Watts, Duncan and Quan 2018). **[solid / contested]**
- Perceptual narrowing for faces: 6-month-olds discriminate individual monkey faces; 9-month-olds and adults cannot (Pascalis, de Haan and Nelson 2002, *Science* 296:1321). **[solid]**

### Social
- Oxytocin: 80 couples, fathers' levels equal to mothers' (Gordon et al. 2010); gay primary-caregiving fathers' amygdalae respond like mothers' (Abraham et al. 2014, *PNAS*, 89 parents). **[solid]**
- Fathers' testosterone drops: 624 Filipino men followed 4.5 years; new fathers' morning T fell 26% and evening T 34% (vs 12%/14% in non-fathers); ≥3 hours of daily childcare predicted the lowest levels (Gettler et al. 2011, *PNAS* 108:16194). **[solid]**
- Mothers' brains shrink to specialize: gray-matter loss in social-cognition regions lasting ≥2 years, enough to classify every woman as pregnant or not (25 mothers vs 20 controls; Hoekzema et al. 2017, *Nat Neurosci*). **[solid]**
- Still face: three phases (play, ~2 minutes of blank face, reunion); the popular "matched only ~30% of the time, mismatches repaired in seconds" is Tronick's own summary (Tronick and Cohn 1989, 54 dyads), not a figure in a primary abstract. **[solid paradigm; 30% contested]**
- Harlow's rhesus infants spent ~17–18 h/day on the cloth mother and under 1 hour on the wire mother that fed them (Harlow and Zimmermann 1959, *Science* 130:421). **[solid; hours via secondary]**
- Attachment: cross-cultural meta-analysis of 32 samples (~1,990 infants): 65% secure, 21% avoidant, 14% resistant; within-country variation exceeds between-country (van IJzendoorn and Kroonenberg 1988, *Child Dev*). Disorganized ~15% in low-risk samples, 48% in maltreated children (van IJzendoorn et al. 1999). Stranger and separation wariness appear ~8–9 months (CDC). **[solid]**
- Social referencing: at a 30-cm visual-cliff drop, 74% of 12-month-olds crossed when mother smiled and none when she looked afraid (Sorce, Emde, Campos and Klinnert 1985, *Dev Psychol* 21:195). **[solid]**
- Eye contact: 2–5-day-olds looked 106.8 s at direct-gaze faces vs 63.7 s at averted gaze (17 newborns; Farroni et al. 2002, *PNAS* 99:9602). **[solid]**
- Helping: 18-month-olds help within seconds, before eye contact (Warneken and Tomasello 2006); a toy reward makes 20-month-olds help less (2008). **[solid]**
- Fairness: 15-month-olds look longer at an unequal cracker split (10.57 s vs 8.07 s) and the ones who share their favorite toy are the ones most bothered by unfairness (Schmidt and Sommerville 2011, *PLoS ONE* 6:e23223); 19-month-olds expect two giraffes to get one toy each (Sloane, Baillargeon and Premack 2012). **[solid]**
- Helper/hinderer: 6- and 10-month-olds prefer the helper (Hamlin, Wynn and Bloom 2007, *Nature*) — but ManyBabies 4 (567 infants, 37 labs) found 49.3% helper choice. **[contested]**
- Bucharest Early Intervention Project: 136 institutionalized toddlers randomized to foster care or care-as-usual; foster-care children had higher cognitive scores at 42 and 54 months, most for those placed before ~24 months (Nelson et al. 2007, *Science* 318:1937); at age 8, IQ 81.5 vs 76.2. **[solid]**
- Kangaroo care: immediate skin-to-skin for 3,211 infants weighing 1.0–1.8 kg cut 28-day mortality from 15.7% to 12.0% (RR 0.75); trial stopped early (WHO iKMC 2021, *NEJM* 384:2028). **[solid]**
- Aka fathers (Central Africa) hold infants 22% of in-camp time and are within arm's reach 51.5% of the day — "at least five times more than fathers in any other human population" (Hewlett). **[solid]**
- Mothers of 11-month-old girls underestimated, mothers of boys overestimated, their babies' crawling on slopes; actual performance was identical (Mondschein, Adolph and Tamis-LeMonda 2000, *J Exp Child Psychol* 77:304). **[solid]**

### Senses
- Newborn vision: acuity ~1 cycle/degree, roughly 20/400–20/800; adult-like by ~3–5 years; fixed focal distance a median 19 cm (~7.5 inches) in alert newborns, adult accommodation by ~4 months (Haynes, White and Held 1965, *Science* 148:528). **[solid; Snellen conversion contested]**
- Not colorblind: over 75% of neonates orient to a large saturated red patch; blue is ignored; trichromacy by ~3 months (Adams et al. 1994; Teller 1998). "Babies see in gray" is false. **[solid]**
- Faces at 9 minutes old: 40 newborns (median age 9 min) tracked a face-like pattern farther than a scrambled one (Goren, Sarty and Wu 1975, *Pediatrics* 56:544; replicated by Johnson et al. 1991; failed by Easterbrook 1999). **[contested]**
- Hearing: first fetal response at 19 weeks (500 Hz); by 27 weeks 96% respond to 250–500 Hz but none to 1,000–3,000 Hz; all respond to 3,000 Hz by 35 weeks (Hepper and Shahidullah 1994, *Arch Dis Child* 71:F81). **[solid]**
- Mother's voice: 8 of 10 newborns under 3 days old changed their sucking to hear mother rather than a stranger (DeCasper and Fifer 1980, *Science* 208:1174); newborns prefer a passage read aloud daily in the last 6 weeks of pregnancy, even in a stranger's voice (DeCasper and Spence 1986). **[solid]**
- Vowels learned in utero: 80 newborns aged 7–75 hours in Sweden and the US sucked more for non-native vowels (Moon, Lagercrantz and Kuhl 2013, *Acta Paediatr* 102:156). **[solid]**
- Smell: 22 of 30 newborns chose the unwashed breast (Varendi, Porter and Winberg 1994, *Lancet* 344:989); a breast-odor pad 17 cm away makes prone newborns crawl toward it (Varendi and Porter 2001). **[solid]**
- Pain: newborns' brains activate 18 of 20 adult pain regions, and a 128-mN poke in a newborn looks like a 512-mN poke in an adult (10 infants, 10 adults; Goksan et al. 2015, *eLife* 4:e06356). Until the mid-1980s newborns were sometimes operated on with muscle relaxants only (Anand and Hickey 1987, *NEJM*). **[solid]**
- Infant synesthesia: shapes bias color preferences in 2–3-month-olds but not 8-month-olds or adults (Wagner and Dobkins 2011, *Psychol Sci* 22:1067). **[solid, single study]**
- Head and body: a newborn's head is about a quarter of body length vs ~one-eighth in adults (textbook); Barbu-Roth's on-air "one third of the weight" is not a standard figure. **[solid / pop]**

### Growth
- Saltatory growth: bursts of 0.5–2.5 cm separated by 2–63 days of nothing; 90–95% of infancy is growth-free (Lampl, Veldhuis and Johnson 1992, *Science*). **[solid, contested]**
- Weight doubles by ~4–6 months and triples by 12; length grows ~50% in year one (~50 to ~75 cm). **[solid textbook]**
- Brain volume +101% in year one and +15% in year two; gray matter +149%, cerebellum +240% in year one; ~83% of adult volume by age 2 (Knickmeyer et al. 2008, *J Neurosci* 28:12176; 98 children). **[solid]**
- "One million new neural connections per second" (Harvard Center on the Developing Child) is uncited; earlier versions said 700–1,000. Measured: synaptic density peaks ~3 months in auditory cortex and after 15 months in prefrontal cortex, 50–60% above adult density, then is pruned (Huttenlocher and Dabholkar 1997, *J Comp Neurol* 387:167). **[pop / solid]**
- "300 bones fuse to 206": repeated everywhere, no primary source; depends on how cartilaginous elements are counted. **[pop]**
- Fontanelles: six at birth; the anterior averages 2.1 cm and closes at a median 13.8 months; the posterior closes by ~2 months (Kiesler and Ricer 2003, *Am Fam Physician* 67:2547). **[solid]**
- Teething does not cause fever: 16 studies, 3,506 children; 70.5% show some sign (gum irritation 86.8%, irritability 68.2%, drooling 55.7%); temperature rises at most to 37.8 °C (Massignan et al. 2016, *Pediatrics* 137:e20153501). First tooth ~6 months. **[solid]**
- Temperament: ~20% of 4-month-olds are "high-reactive," four times more likely to be inhibited at 4 and about half anxious by 7 (Kagan and Snidman 2004); Thomas and Chess's NYLS: 40% easy, 10% difficult, 15% slow-to-warm; heritability of infant temperament ~.20–.60 (Saudino 2005). **[solid; Kagan % 20–25]**
- Dunedin: self-control at ages 3–11 in 1,037 children predicted adult outcomes at 32 — criminal conviction 13% vs 43%, income under NZ$20k 10% vs 32% for top vs bottom fifth (Moffitt et al. 2011, *PNAS* 108:2693). **[solid]**
- Birth order has essentially no effect on personality in 20,000 people (Rohrer, Egloff and Schmukle 2015, *PNAS*). **[solid]**

### Myths and busted claims (jokes live here)
- **Wonder Weeks**: ten "leaps" on a fixed calendar. Groningen PhD student Carolina de Weerth, with Paul van Geert, tested the schedule in 4 infants and found nothing; she said Plooij pressured her not to publish; the university let Plooij go in January 1998. Later studies: 28 infants, no pattern; 18 infants, fussiness only by maternal report. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wonder_Weeks **[pop / contested]**
- **Mozart effect**: see Language. **[debunked]**
- **Baby Einstein**: see Language; Disney dropped "educational" in 2006 after an FTC complaint, refunds in 2009. **[solid]**
- **30-million-word gap**: 42 families, hour-a-month samples, big extrapolation; not replicated as stated. **[contested]**
- **Neonatal imitation**: 106 newborns, 11 gestures, nothing. **[contested, leaning debunked]**
- **"Babies don't feel pain"**: surgery without anesthesia into the 1980s; fMRI says otherwise. **[debunked]**
- **Teething fever**: max 37.8 °C. **[debunked]**
- **Sleep regressions on a schedule**: no peer-reviewed support. **[pop]**
- **"Babies see upside down"**: every retina inverts the image at every age; a garbled Stratton 1896 goggles story. **[pop]**
- **Full-moon births**: 564,039 North Carolina births over 62 lunar cycles, no effect (Arliss, Kaplan and Galvin 2005, *AJOG* 192:1462). **[debunked]**
- **Vaccines–autism**: Wakefield 1998, 12 children, retracted 2010, struck off May 2010. **[debunked]**
- **Pacifiers and SIDS**: adjusted odds ratio 0.08 (185 cases, 312 controls; Li et al. 2006, *BMJ* 332:18) — press rounded to "90%"; association only. **[solid, correlational]**
- **Breast milk and IQ**: see Feeding. **[contested]**
- **Tummy time**: 16 studies, 4,237 infants, modest gross-motor and flat-head benefits, almost all questionnaire-based (Hewitt et al. 2020, *Pediatrics* 145:e20192168). **[solid, weak base]**

---

## 3. Hooks: 30 quotable, numeric, surprising lines (each with a source)

1. A toddler takes about 2,368 steps and falls 17 times an hour — roughly 14,000 steps and 100 falls a day. Adolph et al. 2012, *Psychol Sci*. **[solid; daily figures extrapolated]**
2. Babies grow up to 2.5 cm overnight, then nothing for up to 63 days; 90–95% of infancy is growth-free. Lampl 1992, *Science*. **[solid, contested]**
3. On air: 62.7 cm on Monday, 64.5 on Tuesday — Lampl assumed she was "really terrible at this." Netflix *Babies* Ep 3. **[solid]**
4. The growth-chart curve is connect-the-dots from 882 children measured monthly. Netflix *Babies* Ep 3. **[solid]**
5. Fathers' oxytocin matched mothers' in 80 couples; "Fatherhood is biological. It's as deep as motherhood." Gordon et al. 2010; Ep 1. **[solid]**
6. Gay dads raising babies from day one show mother-like amygdala activation; "It's a choice to be a parent to that infant." Abraham et al. 2014, *PNAS*; Ep 1. **[solid]**
7. Eight-month-olds learn made-up words like "pabeecoo" after two minutes of hearing them 45 times each. Saffran 1996, *Science*; Ep 4. **[solid]**
8. Only three bird groups and five mammal groups can learn new sounds: hummingbirds, parrots, songbirds, elephants, bats, dolphins, seals, humans. Jarvis 2019, *Science*; Ep 4. **[solid]**
9. Humans carry an extra copy of SRGAP2 that keeps neurons wired together longer, duplicated ~3.4, 2.4 and 1 million years ago. Dennis et al. 2012, *Cell*; Ep 4. **[solid]**
10. Six million logged events from ~1,000 babies: 1.5M sleeps, 2.5M feeds, 1.5M diapers — random at one month, a rhythm by one year. NYU Baby Sleep Study, Ep 5. **[unpublished]**
11. A sleeping baby's twitch is a sonar ping: one twitch fires "hundreds, probably thousands, of neurons" to map the body. Blumberg, Ep 5; Blumberg 2013, *Curr Biol*. **[solid]**
12. Babies cycle sleep and wake "hundreds" of times a day because the hypothalamic clock isn't wired to the brainstem yet; nothing fixes it before 3–4 months. Blumberg, Ep 5. **[solid]**
13. Half of a newborn's sleep is REM; adults get about 20%; at 30 weeks' gestation it's 80%. Roffwarg 1966, *Science*. **[solid]**
14. Only 43% of 6-month-olds sleep eight hours straight; at a year, 57%. Pennestri 2018, *Pediatrics*. **[solid]**
15. Newborn stepping uses two muscle patterns; toddlers add two more; rats, cats, macaques and guineafowl share them. Dominici 2011, *Science*; Ep 6. **[solid]**
16. Cowgill carried 130 pounds of gear through 15 museums to X-ray 600 children's bones, back to 250,000 years ago; Reviewer 2 said "Children's bones don't respond to exercise." Ep 6. **[solid]**
17. Toddler femurs are wide ovals, adult femurs upside-down teardrops, because toddlers waddle. Cowgill et al. 2010, *AJPA*. **[solid]**
18. Walkers have bigger vocabularies than same-age crawlers — and hear exactly the same number of words. Walle and Campos 2014; Ep 6. **[solid, parent-report]**
19. One five-minute block of a zoo day: 374 adult words. LENA recording, Ep 6. **[solid]**
20. French newborns cry with rising melody, German newborns with falling melody — 1,254 cries, 60 babies. Mampe 2009, *Curr Biol*. **[solid]**
21. Crying peaks around six weeks at ~2 hours a day; Danish babies cry least, British and Canadian most. Wolke 2017, *J Pediatr*. **[solid]**
22. Day-one colostrum: 1.5 grams a feed, 15 grams a day — three teaspoons. Santoro 2010, *J Pediatr*. **[solid]**
23. Milk leukocytes jump from 2% to 94% when the baby gets sick. Hassiotou 2013. **[solid, small n]**
24. Rhesus mothers give sons richer milk and daughters more of it; 1.49 million Holsteins give ~445 kg more milk after a daughter. Hinde 2009; Hinde 2014. **[solid]**
25. Six-month-olds can tell 8 dots from 16 but not 8 from 12; two-day-olds match 4 vs 12 syllables to 4 vs 12 objects. Xu and Spelke 2000; Izard 2009. **[solid]**
26. Eleven-month-olds who watch a ball pass through a wall then bang the ball on the table; if it floated, they drop it. Stahl and Feigenson 2015, *Science*. **[solid]**
27. Three-month-olds can tell monkey faces apart; by nine months, like adults, they can't. Pascalis 2002, *Science*; Kang Lee, Ep 7. **[solid]**
28. Saxe scanned her own awake infant son and found face and scene regions "in the same place" as adults'. Deen 2017, *Nat Commun*; Ep 7. **[solid]**
29. The helper-vs-hinderer puppet show that launched infant morality: 567 babies in 37 labs later chose the helper 49.3% of the time. Hamlin 2007, *Nature*; ManyBabies 4, 2025. **[contested]**
30. The Wonder Weeks' ten leaps were tested on four babies by a PhD student who found nothing; her supervisor's co-author lost his job. de Weerth and van Geert 1998. **[pop]**

Bonus: a newborn focuses at 19 cm, orients to red but not blue, and knows mother's smell well enough that 22 of 30 pick the unwashed breast (Haynes 1965; Adams 1994; Varendi 1994). Pointing starts at 12 months; mirror self-recognition at ~20–24 months in Western toddlers but 7% in Vanuatu (Amsterdam 1972; Cebioğlu and Broesch 2021). Newborns were operated on without anesthesia until the mid-1980s (Anand and Hickey 1987).

# Netflix *Babies* (2020) and the infant-science literature: fact sheet for a dense song

Compiled 2026-10-16. One fact per line, number and citation inline.
Reliability flags: **[solid]** = primary paper or primary source checked; **[contested]** = real evidence but disputed, under-replicated, or a secondary-source number; **[pop]** = pop-science-only, no primary support, or an on-air gloss that goes beyond the paper; **[unverified]** = could not confirm.
Part 1 episode content is taken from the actual episode audio (Netflix posted five full episodes on YouTube: Love, Crawling, First Words, Sleep, First Steps). "First Food" and all of Part 2 are reconstructed from press releases, lab pages and reviews; gaps are marked.

---

## 0. Series facts

- Produced by Nutopia for Netflix; executive producer Jane Root (Nutopia CEO); series producer / EP Sara Brailsford. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babies_(American_TV_series) **[solid]**
- 12 episodes of 46–54 minutes in two parts: Part 1 (Love, First Food, Crawling, First Words, Sleep, First Steps) released 21 Feb 2020; Part 2 (What Babies Know, Movement, Senses, Relationships, Nature and Nurture, Toddlers) released 19 Jun 2020. https://media.netflix.com/en/only-on-netflix/80117833 **[solid]**
- Opening title cards, verbatim: "15 BABIES / FILMED OVER ONE YEAR / 36 WORLD RENOWNED SCIENTISTS / REVEALING HOW WE ALL BEGIN." **[solid, transcript]**
- Nutopia: the project was "filmed over the course of three years"; Netflix's educational guide says the 36 scientists are "mostly from Europe, the U.S. and Israel." https://digbi.net/wp-content/uploads/Babies-Educational-Resource-Netflix.pdf **[solid]**
- Part 1 sat in Netflix's Top 10 for several weeks (Northwestern release); WSU says it "rose to number seven." https://news.northwestern.edu/stories/2020/02/netflix-babies-docu-series-features-northwestern-psychology-professor-susan-hespos **[solid]**
- Opening narration (Ep 1), Andre Fenton: "As a scientist and a new parent, I realize that babies are born totally helpless. But they're going to grow up to be masters of the universe. How does that happen?" **[solid, transcript]**
- Also in the Ep 1 open: "Isn't it amazing that the answer to what it means to be human lies in the smallest, youngest creatures?" and the series' recurring question, "One of the great puzzles is, what's it like to be a baby?" **[solid, transcript]**
- Netflix's guide poses one question per episode, e.g. "Why do human babies learn to speak much later than they learn language?" and "Why is it challenging for babies to sleep according to a pattern?" **[solid]**
- Families (names as spoken on air): Willow (London; parents Rachel and Adam; big sister Lily, who presses stop on the camera). **[solid, transcript]**
- Pascoe (London; Amelia and Rich; Rich is the stay-at-home "primary carer"; Pascoe never crawls and goes straight to cruising and walking). **[solid, transcript]**
- Dakota (US; Destiny, a realtor who does house showings while pumping, and Shawn, who tests the breast pump: "Excuse the six pack"). **[solid, transcript]**
- Eric (two dads, Isaac and Josh, via surrogate AJ in Canada, "where it is illegal to get paid for surrogacy"). **[solid, transcript]**
- Nelson (London; Morning-Star and Charlie); Mateo (Stacy); twins Kiki and Yaya (Belinda and Dayo), who "just refuse to sleep at night." **[solid, transcript]**
- Twins Eloisa and Augusto (Agustina; Spanish-speaking): Eloisa 2.7 kg at birth, Augusto "a little bit under two" kg and 10 days in an incubator. **[solid, transcript]**
- Bilingual family Natasha (English) and Adrien (French), one parent one language, with Lola and baby Hugo; Penelope (Merced, CA), who wears a LENA recorder to the zoo. **[solid, transcript]**
- Lead-list scientists who do NOT appear in any accessible source: Patricia Kuhl, Maria Gloria Dominguez-Bello, Charles Nelson, Elizabeth Spelke, Fei Xu, Michael Tomasello, Dan Fessler, Celeste Kidd. **[solid negative, within available sources]**
- The microbiome scientist is Susan Lynch (UCSF), not Dominguez-Bello; the language scientists are Hirsh-Pasek, Saffran and Jarvis, not Kuhl. **[solid]**
