## 1. Episode by episode

### Ep 1 "Love" (Part 1)
Netflix: "The biology of bonding unravels to reveal how caregiving, social interaction and stress can physically change both parent and newborn."

- **Ruth Feldman** (IDC Herzliya, now Reichman University, Israel; joint appointment Yale Child Study Center). Daughter Estie was placed on her chest when Feldman was 22: "at that moment I felt what it means to love." **[solid, transcript]**
- In 2001 she began "the first study to test the role of oxytocin in parent/infant bonding": "about 80 mothers," sampled through pregnancy and the first month; oxytocin rises in pregnancy, stays high, and touch raises it in mother and baby alike. **[solid]**
- Next: "80 couples," saliva sampled in the first months; "Mothers' and fathers' level of oxytocin were identical." Gordon, Zagoory-Sharon, Leckman and Feldman 2010, *Biological Psychiatry* 68:377 (160 first-time parents at 6 weeks and 6 months). https://sciencedaily.com/releases/2010/08/100820101207.htm **[solid]**
- Mothers' oxytocin tracks affectionate touch and gaze; fathers' tracks stimulatory play (same paper). **[solid]**
- On air: "The more you do with a baby... and really lift your sleeves, and take care of the child... the more your oxytocin system will activate. Fatherhood is biological. It's as deep as motherhood." **[solid, transcript]**
- On air: the birth oxytocin surge "activates a very primitive structure. The amygdala... once the mother's amygdala is open, it stays like this forever"; dad's response is "about a quarter of what you see in mom's." **[pop: on-air gloss]**
- "In 2010 we recruited... 48 gay couples" raising surrogacy-born babies from day one: primary-caregiving fathers "had amygdala activation just like mothers." **[solid, transcript]**
- The paper: Abraham et al. 2014, *PNAS* 111:9792 — 89 parents (20 primary-care mothers, 21 secondary-care fathers, 48 primary-care gay fathers); gay fathers showed mother-like amygdala plus father-like superior-temporal-sulcus activity; amygdala–STS connectivity scaled with hours of childcare (r = 0.33). https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4103311/ **[solid]**
- Closing line: "It doesn't really matter whether you are the biological parent or a committed caregiving parent. It's a choice. It's a choice to be a parent to that infant." **[solid, transcript]**
- **Ed Tronick** (UMass Boston; Harvard Medical School). Arrived at Harvard psychology in 1968, when "everyone was doing hard science"; a professor put an arm round him: "This emotion stuff is not what you want to be doing." **[solid, transcript]**
- Still-face: mothers of 3-, 4- and 5-month-olds "stop reacting to the baby for about two minutes"; babies smile, bid, eventually cry, then bring hands to mouth to self-comfort. Tronick, Als, Adamson, Wise and Brazelton 1978, *J Am Acad Child Psychiatry* 17:1 (PMID 632477). **[solid]**
- New version adds salivary cortisol: babies with "positive experience with their parents" are less stressed during the still face. Study specifics are not cited on air. **[solid paradigm; result described, not cited]**
- Tronick's parenting line: "Trust your baby to tell you what it is that he or she needs and trust your own instincts... That's how the two of you fall in love with one another." **[solid, transcript]**
- **Anne Rifkin-Graboi** (National Institute of Education, NTU Singapore), with imaging colleague **Anqi Qiu**: GUSTO cohort (Growing Up in Singapore Towards Healthy Outcomes). Babies scanned in the first weeks and again at 6 months; babies fed to sleep for the scan. **[solid, transcript]**
- "We had 20 babies" rated for maternal responsiveness with a card-sort at 6 months; less responsive caregiving went with "a bit bigger" hippocampi, read as babies "having to manage their own stress." "To my knowledge, that was the first time a group had seen this." **[contested: n = 20, counterintuitive direction, primary paper not located]**
- Family beats: Willow's birth opens the series; Josh at Eric's vaccination: "I don't know who is going to suffer more. Him or me." **[solid, transcript]**

### Ep 2 "First Food"
Netflix: "...how breast milk, iron and microbes can enrich, nourish and protect offspring." (Not on YouTube; roster from the Notre Dame episode summary, Netflix media page, UCSF Lynch Lab page and goodto.com.)

- **Katie Hinde** (Arizona State University; comparative lactation). Rhesus mothers of sons make richer, more energy-dense milk; daughters get more volume and a higher calcium:phosphorus ratio. Hinde 2009, *Am J Hum Biol* 21:512 (114 mothers; total milk energy equal); Hinde et al. 2013, *Am J Phys Anthropol* "Daughter dearest." https://doi.org/10.1002/ajhb.20917 **[solid]**
- Dairy cows favor daughters: 2.39 million lactation records from 1.49 million Holsteins show ~1.3% more milk (~445 kg over two lactations) after a heifer calf. Hinde, Carpenter, Clay and Bradford 2014, *PLoS ONE* 9:e86169. https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0086169 **[solid]**
- Milk cortisol predicts temperament: in 108 macaque pairs, higher milk glucocorticoids went with more nervous, less confident, faster-gaining infants. Hinde et al. 2015, *Behav Ecol*. **[solid]**
- Hinde on air (per summaries): "an individual mom makes individualized milk for an individual baby"; daily experiences leave "signatures" in milk; the best substitute is formula built from human-milk science, not cow's milk. https://sites.nd.edu/emily-clarke/2019/12/06/chapter-9/ **[solid, secondary]**
- Supporting lit for "milk responds to illness": leukocytes are 13–70% of cells in colostrum, 0–2% in mature milk, and surge to as high as 94% during infant or maternal infection (21 dyads). Hassiotou et al. 2013, *Clin Transl Immunol* 2:e3. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25505951/ **[solid, small n]**
- **Michael Georgieff** (University of Minnesota, Institute of Child Development; neonatologist). Many babies are short of iron, zinc and copper; babies are born with about four months of iron. **[solid, secondary]**
- Iron-sufficient newborns' ERPs distinguish mother's voice from a stranger's; iron-deficient newborns' do not. Siddappa et al. 2004, *Pediatr Res* 55:1034 (32 newborns of diabetic mothers; cord ferritin ≤34 µg/L = brain-iron deficient). **[solid]**
- Georgieff's on-air claim: eliminating micronutrient deficiencies worldwide would "shift the world's IQ positively by ten points." **[pop]**
- **Susan Lynch** (UCSF). "It's really important for babies to ingest microbes early in life"; babies living with dogs, and to a lesser extent cats, are better protected from asthma and allergy. https://lynchlab.ucsf.edu/node/701 **[solid]**
- Dust from dog-owning homes restructures the mouse gut microbiome (Lactobacillus johnsonii) and blunts allergic airway disease. Fujimura et al. 2014, *PNAS* 111:805. **[solid]**
- One-month-old stool microbiota fall into three states; the riskiest (NGM3: low Bifidobacterium, Akkermansia and Faecalibacterium, more Candida) predicts multisensitized atopy at 2 and asthma at 4. Fujimura et al. 2016, *Nat Med* 22:1187 (298 samples, median age 35 days). **[solid]**
- Also in the episode (goodto.com): a mother's pregnancy diet shapes the baby's food preferences because taste and memory form before birth (see Mennella, Senses theme). **[solid]**
- Mothers interviewed: a British mother who loves breastfeeding and a Connecticut mother of twins who stopped for low supply (Notre Dame summary). **[secondary]**

### Ep 3 "Crawling"
Netflix: "From how they grow to how they go, three scientists find out that crawling is much more than a transitional phase."

- **Marianne Barbu-Roth** (CNRS / Université de Paris). "I started to be a nuclear physicist... studying the movement of electrons and atoms"; a newborn is "an astronaut coming back from the moon... now stuck with the gravity." **[solid, transcript]**
- "Around one hour after birth, if the baby is skin to skin on the belly of the mother, you will see the newborn moving slowly to the breast"; put in water, newborns "literally crawl." **[solid, transcript]**
- Optic flow: moving black dots projected beneath a suspended newborn make it move "literally swimming. Swimming in the air." Barbu-Roth et al. 2009, *Child Development* 80:8 — 48 three-day-olds; more air-steps to forward-translating flow than to rotating or static patterns. **[solid]**
- On air: "this crawling pattern that a lot of people believe was just a reflex is not a reflex." A newborn's head is "one third of the weight of the body." **[solid finding; head figure is pop]**
- The "Crawlerskate" (published as the Crawliskate): a mini-skateboard under the trunk; first baby tested 2016 after "one or two years" of building; two-day-olds "even crossed the mattress once or two or three times." Forma et al. 2019, *Child Development*. **[solid]**
- "We believe now that we are born moving arms and legs as quadrupeds... We are still quadrupeds inside us, in the nervous system." Her metaphor: "the engine is sleeping and waiting for the bodywork to be ready." **[interpretation]**
- **Michelle Lampl** (Emory, Center for the Study of Human Health; MD-PhD anthropologist). 1979: her neighbor's baby measured 62.7 cm one day and 64.5 cm the next, then 64.5 for days: "I'm really terrible at this... that's way too much." **[solid, transcript]**
- Growth charts are "connect-the-dots" from 882 children measured at 1, 2, 4, 6 and 8 weeks and then monthly to one year. **[solid, transcript]**
- She set out to measure 30 babies; her notebook for 23 August 1981 shows 12 children measured, first house at 7:20 a.m., last left at 10 p.m. **[solid, transcript]**
- 19 February 1986: a baby measured 59.8, 59.8, 59.8 and then 60.8 cm — one centimetre in one day. **[solid, transcript]**
- On air: daily-measured babies grew "between one half of a centimeter and 1.65 centimeters in one 24-hour period," separated by "two to twenty-eight days of no growth." **[solid, transcript]**
- The paper: Lampl, Veldhuis and Johnson 1992, *Science* 258:801 — 31 infants (10 weekly, 18 semiweekly, 3 daily) over 21 months; saltations 0.5–2.5 cm; stasis 2–63 days; "90 to 95 percent of normal development during infancy is growth-free." https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1439787/ **[solid, but critics attribute part to measurement error (Science 1995 exchange) — contested]**
- Growth days coincided with "changes in their sleeping behavior, tantrums, and times of insatiable hunger." **[solid, transcript]**
- Follow-up: length spurts follow sleep bursts within 48 hours; odds of a spurt rise a median 43% per extra sleep episode and 20% per extra hour (23 infants, 5,798 diary days). Lampl and Johnson 2011, *Sleep* 34:641. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3079944 **[solid, small n]**
- Head circumference grows in jumps too, coupled to length (40 infants, 2,616 measurements). Lampl et al. 2011, *Early Hum Dev* 87:361. **[solid]**
- "The day that the paper was published it was on the front page of The New York Times, The Washington Post"; at a conference "a leading figure in the field stood up and started by mocking the data." **[solid, transcript]**
- Netflix filmed in May 2019 on the Emory campus and at a family's home. https://news.emory.edu/stories/2020/02/emory-professor-featured-new-netflix-documentary-series-babies **[solid]**
- **Karen Adolph** (NYU Infant Action Lab). Studied fine art in college; a professor handed her a plant: "Draw this... you need to really look at it and understand how it grows." **[solid, transcript]**
- Adjustable slope apparatus raised "six degrees at a time" to a 90-degree drop-off. Grant (about 10 months, crawling a month) "approached the 90-degree drop-off the same way as he was approaching the shallower slopes" and went over. **[solid, transcript]**
- Marlow stopped at the brink; Dax switched to a bear crawl on hands and feet. "The babies who make smart decisions... generate perceptual information by looking and by touching." **[solid, transcript]**
- "It takes about 20 weeks of crawling experience before a baby is showing adult-like accuracy when they're looking at an impossibly steep slope." (cf. Adolph 1997, *Monographs SRCD* 62(3).) **[solid]**
- Diary study of her daughter Lily: 19 March a combat crawl, never seen again; 28 March a belly crawl, not again until 1 April. "Skills are sort of appearing and disappearing and reappearing." **[solid, transcript]**
- "The whole idea of motor milestones, where there's a kind of orderly progression... in every pediatrician's office, in every parenting book or magazine. It just really doesn't happen like that." **[solid, transcript]**
- "Crawling isn't mandatory. It is not an obligatory stage... if your baby finds another solution for getting around, like bum-shuffling, great." **[solid, transcript]**
- Lit: of 28 infants followed from first crawling to walking, 15 belly-crawled first and 13 skipped straight to hands-and-knees; belly-crawlers became better hands-and-knees crawlers. Adolph, Vereijken and Denny 1998, *Child Dev* 69:1299. **[solid]**
- Babies "can wake up and find that they've grown another two centimeters," or be dressed in an outfit that changes how they move. **[solid, transcript]**
- Family beat: Grandma on Skype insists "It's terribly important for them to crawl"; Pascoe shakes his head and never does. **[solid, transcript]**

### Ep 4 "First Words"
Netflix: "Experts examine the rhythm and flow of language and explore how babies break down linguistic beats before they know how to speak."

- **Kathy Hirsh-Pasek** (Temple University Infant Language Lab; Brookings). Was "headed toward being a musician"; her aha moment was a three-year-old at a pool complaining about her brother's ballgame "with the sophisticated grammar and language skills of an adult." **[solid, transcript]**
- Experiment: a Cinderella passage ("...because she had this mean, mean, mean stepmother") with pauses placed either at clause boundaries or mid-clause, played from left and right speakers; babies "overwhelmingly looked more quickly" and stayed longer on the natural version. **[solid, transcript]**
- The paper: Hirsh-Pasek et al. 1987, *Cognition* 26:269, "Clauses are perceptual units for young infants" (7–10-month-olds). **[solid]**
- "40 years ago... there was nobody, literally no one in the world, who I could find who was doing music and language together." **[solid, transcript]**
- "At just two days of age, we already know that babies recognize classes of language... English and some of the other Germanic languages kinda sound alike." (Newborn rhythm-class discrimination: Nazzi, Bertoncini and Mehler 1998, *JEP:HPP* 24:756; Mehler et al. 1988, *Cognition* 29:143.) **[solid]**
- Fetuses hear language "against the backdrop of 'bu-bum, bu-bum,' for the heart, and the swishing sound of the amniotic fluid." **[solid, transcript]**
- "At the end of the first year, these babies have kinda cracked the code of what the units of language are. And the next big job is actually finding the words." **[solid, transcript]**
- **Jenny Saffran** (University of Wisconsin–Madison Infant Learning Lab). Origin story: her mother studied brain damage; a patient lost every noun except "shopping center" — "The shopping center went to the shopping center to buy the shopping center." **[solid, transcript]**
- Made-up language with words like "pabeecoo" and "golatoo," synthesized in a random stream; babies listen "for two minutes" and hear each word "a lot, like 45 times." **[solid, transcript]**
- Test: a side light blinks and plays either a word or a non-word for as long as the baby looks; babies "will actually be bored of the words... and they'll turn their heads to listen longer to the sounds that were not words." **[solid, transcript]**
- The paper: Saffran, Aslin and Newport 1996, *Science* 274:1926 — 8-month-olds segment words using transitional probabilities of 1.0 within words vs 0.33 across. https://psych.wisc.edu/news/saffrans-infant-learning-lab-featured-on-netflix-docuseries-babies/ **[solid]**
- Word meaning by elimination: "doggie" with a stick and a bone one day, a ball and a shoe the next; only the dog is constant. "That is statistical learning as well." **[solid, transcript]**
- "By the end of the first year of life they understand somewhere between 10 and 50 words on average... most of them are not saying any words yet." **[solid, transcript]**
- Bilingual babies must learn "the statistics not just of one language, but of two" and sort the "two soups" by "musical properties" — rhythm and pitch (cf. Byers-Heinlein, Burns and Werker 2010, *Psychol Sci* 21:343). "It's vastly easier for babies than for older children and adults." **[solid]**
- **Erich Jarvis** (Rockefeller University; HHMI). "I was once a dancer... I was dancing six hours a day"; at 18 in 1988, at his senior concert, he chose science "and I never turned back." **[solid, transcript]**
- Vocal learners across tens of thousands of vertebrate species: "Three birds and five mammals. That's the hummingbirds, the parrots, songbirds, elephants, bats, dolphins, seals, and human." Jarvis 2019, *Science* 366:50. **[solid]**
- Began in 1989 imaging singing birds' brains; "eight years later, an accidental discovery": hopping and wing-flapping lit up regions right next to the song nuclei. Feenders et al. 2008, *PLoS ONE* 3:e1768, the "motor theory of vocal learning origin." **[solid]**
- "Think of spoken language as learning how to coordinate your body... This is why understanding language occurs in children before the ability to speak. Just like learning how to walk, speech comes with a delay." **[solid, transcript]**
- "In 2012, an intriguing discovery was made that humans have an extra copy of one gene... SRGAP2," which "maintains extra connections in the entire human brain." Dennis et al. 2012, *Cell* 149:912 — duplicated ~3.4, ~2.4 and ~1 million years ago; SRGAP2C slows spine maturation, leaving denser connections (Charrier et al. 2012, *Cell* 149:923). **[solid gene story; language link is Jarvis's hypothesis]**
- Netflix guide quote: "We humans are along a spectrum with other animals when it comes to spoken language abilities. But we are at the far end of the spectrum." **[solid]**
- Family beats: Nelson's language goes from "non-existent" to counting to six and "Red means stop"; Willow says "Dada"; Pascoe says "no, no" "with a bit of sass"; Lola translates Papa's French for Mum. **[solid, transcript]**

### Ep 5 "Sleep"
Netflix: "Researchers delve into sleep, a dynamic time for babies, with studies on twitching, apps and naps."

- **Andre Fenton** (NYU Center for Neural Science). "Getting your baby to sleep through the night turns out to be a universal human challenge." Became a sleep researcher because "all hell broke loose" when he couldn't get his daughter to sleep. **[solid, transcript]**
- On air: "A newborn baby is spending 16 hours a day sleeping." (Meta-analytic mean is 14.6 h at 0–2 months; see Sleep theme.) **[contested figure]**
- Founded the NYU Baby Sleep Study with baby-tracking app developers; visiting families, he found parents log to the minute, error "no more than five minutes." **[solid, transcript]**
- **Pascal Wallisch** (NYU): "about 1,000 babies... six million events made up out of about 1.5 million sleep events, 2.5 million eating events, and 1.5 million diaper change events." **[solid, transcript]**
- At one month eating and sleeping are random; "within about four months an initial cycle is emerging"; at eight months "a hint of a pattern"; "within a year, this clear pattern emerges. Babies are sleeping through the night." **[unverified: "we are not comfortable to publish this yet"]**
- The year-one pattern: sleep after the morning feed, again about five hours later in early afternoon, and overnight. Fenton: the transition "is something that almost all babies do. And it'll take about a year." **[unverified, unpublished]**
- **Mark Blumberg** (University of Iowa). "I've always been a terrible sleeper... we study the things that we're bad at." Never uses an alarm clock. **[solid, transcript]**
- Baby rats and baby humans "cycle very rapidly between sleep and wake... over the course of 24 hours, they can have hundreds of cycles." **[solid, transcript]**
- Dye tracing in newborn rats: the hypothalamic master clock has no wiring to brainstem sleep-wake centers at birth; a week later it does. "It's also happening in humans." **[solid, transcript]**
- "Prior to three to four months, there's very little you can do to help babies sleeping throughout the night. It's just gonna have to wait for the circadian system to connect them." **[solid; cf. Rivkees 2003, *Pediatrics* 112:373]**
- Twitching: his dog Katie; "The historical view going back hundreds of years, if not millennia, is that twitching is just a byproduct of dreams." **[solid, transcript]**
- Lab: EEG caps on infants asleep in car seats — "the easiest experiment you can do"; "This baby's a champion twitcher." **[solid, transcript]**
- Submarine metaphor: a sleeping brain is quiet "and then when a baby is twitching, boom. That is a sonar test... 'What do my limbs do? How many muscles do I have?'" **[solid, transcript]**
- "A single twitch can produce activity in hundreds, probably thousands, of neurons... you're mapping your body." Blumberg, Marques and Iida 2013, *Curr Biol* 23:R532. **[solid]**
- Human follow-up: in 22 infants, twitches during quiet sleep rise sharply from ~3 months and are time-locked to sleep spindles. Sokoloff et al. 2021, *Curr Biol*. Lab page: "highlighted in Episode 5." https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/node/41 **[solid]**
- **Rebecca Spencer** (UMass Amherst SomneuroLab). "I'm a lark"; postdoc at UC Berkeley in the mid-2000s when sleep-and-memory work took off. **[solid, transcript]**
- Infant study: 9-month-olds shown eight toys, four demonstrated with unusual actions; half nap, half are kept awake; the napper reproduces "three target objects," the non-napper "didn't remember any." **[solid, transcript; unpublished at filming]**
- Sleep spindles "happen simultaneously across the brain," moving memories from hippocampus to cortex; "in an infant, their hippocampus is smaller. So babies need to sleep in multiple sleep bouts, two naps and that overnight sleep." **[solid, transcript]**
- Landmark paper: Kurdziel, Duclos and Spencer 2013, *PNAS* 110:17267 — 40 preschoolers, ~78-minute classroom naps, ~10% more recall after napping, spindle density r = 0.65 with memory gain; only habitual nappers benefit. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3808582 **[solid]**
- Filmed March 2019 at UMass. https://www.massachusetts.edu/ada-exception-archive/umass-amherst-researcher-rebecca-spencer-featured-netflix-series-and-pbs-nova **[solid]**
- Fenton's closer: "It's solving one of the hardest problems in the universe. We should let them sleep." **[solid, transcript]**
- Family beats: Adam bets Rachel £50 he can handle both kids alone and loses; Pascoe later sleeps "till, like, 6:30–7:00" and drops to two naps. **[solid, transcript]**

### Ep 6 "First Steps"
Netflix: "...walking changes a baby's world and unveil findings on neonatal reflexes, skeletal development and talking."

- **Nadia Dominici** (Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam; Italian physicist turned neurophysiologist). Newborn stepping: "You support about 70% of the baby's weight and you put its feet on a flat surface... one leg slowly in front of the other." **[solid, transcript]**
- 2010 study at the Santa Lucia Foundation, Rome, with Sant'Eugenio Hospital's neonatal unit: EMG and motion sensors on 46 newborns (2–3 days old) stepping on a treadmill, compared with 10 toddlers. **[solid, transcript]**
- Newborn stepping needs only two muscle-activation patterns (weight support and swing); toddlers keep both and add two more (touchdown and push-off). Dominici et al. 2011, *Science* 334:997. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22096202/ **[solid]**
- The same primitives appear in rat, cat, macaque and guineafowl — "a common ancestral neural network." Same paper. **[solid]**
- "The baby is born with an innate walking skill"; parents "can possibly stimulate this stepping in newborns." **[solid, transcript]**
- **Libby Cowgill** (University of Missouri; biological anthropologist, powerlifter and CrossFit instructor). "I come from a long line of people who like to pick up very heavy objects and put them down again"; her grandfather did a gymnastics routine on top of the Empire State Building. **[solid, transcript]**
- In 2006 she visited "about 15 different museums" in Europe, Africa, the US and the Middle East carrying "about 130 pounds of scientific equipment on my back." **[solid, transcript]**
- Bone cross-sections (molds plus X-rays, films developed by hand) from "over 600 infants and children," from the 20th century back to 250,000 years ago. **[solid, transcript]**
- Adult femur cross-section is "an upside-down teardrop"; toddlers' is "a wide oval" that normalizes "by the time they're around five or six." **[solid, transcript]**
- Reviewer 2 on her first paper: "Children's bones don't respond to exercise." **[solid, transcript]**
- With **Cara Ocobock** (Notre Dame), motion capture and force plates on living toddlers ("Mabel is our all-star walker"): toddlers' legs go straight down, a wide base, more side-to-side ground reaction force, so bone is laid down on the sides. **[solid, transcript]**
- The paper: Cowgill, Warrener, Pontzer and Ocobock 2010, *Am J Phys Anthropol* 143:52 — normalized mediolateral forces significantly higher in young walkers than adults. https://anthropology.missouri.edu/news/prof-libby-cowgill-featured-netflix-series-babies **[solid]**
- **Eric Walle** (UC Merced Interpersonal Development Lab). As a grad student he handed parents a language survey "primarily just to distract the parents"; the accidental result: "walking infants had significantly larger vocabulary sizes... regardless of their age." **[solid, transcript]**
- The paper: Walle and Campos 2014, *Dev Psychol* 50:336 — walking onset brought a jump in receptive and productive vocabulary; replicated in Chinese infants (He, Walle and Campos 2015, *Infancy* 20:283). https://doi.org/10.1037/a0033238 **[solid; parent-report measures]**
- LENA recorders in "about 20 families' homes"; one five-minute block: "at 10:50, the child heard 374 adult words." **[solid, transcript]**
- Walkers and crawlers hear the same number of words and vocalize equally; the difference is parents' responses ("Du..." "Duck." "Duck!"). **[solid, transcript]**
- "Walking is a complete game changer for the infant... The baby's life is forever changed, the parent's life is forever changed." https://idlab.ucmerced.edu/node/81 **[solid]**
- Caveat: a later Walle follow-up found 13-month crawlers knew as many words as 13-month walkers. **[contested]**
- Family beats: Augusto bum-jumps for months, then "just stood up and kept on walking, as if he had been doing that forever"; Nelson becomes "a little Speedy Gonzales"; Amelia drops the camera when Pascoe walks. **[solid, transcript]**

### Ep 7 "What Babies Know" (Part 2)
Netflix: "Disputing the notion of a blank slate, three studies explore how babies are born with expectations about everyday life."

- **Susan Hespos** (Northwestern Infant Cognition Lab; now Western Sydney University MARCS). Babies look about twice as long at a block hanging in midair as at a supported one: "They know this stuff way earlier than we thought." **[solid, HuffPost]**
- A toddler trying one round object on its head, then another, is grouping by sameness — "a really cool, uniquely human ability." **[solid, HuffPost]**
- Lit: 5-month-olds expect liquids to pour and solids to tumble from an upturned cup. Hespos, Ferry and Rips 2009, *Psychol Sci* 20:603. **[solid]**
- Lit: 7- and 9-month-olds abstract same/different from four exemplars (N = 64); the single-exemplar replication failed (N = 26). Ferry, Hespos and Gentner 2015, *Child Dev* 86:1386. **[solid]**
- Wikipedia lists her as "Part 2, Episode 1"; her lab was filmed in Evanston in fall 2019. https://news.northwestern.edu/stories/2020/02/netflix-babies-docu-series-features-northwestern-psychology-professor-susan-hespos **[solid]**
- **Kang Lee** (OISE, University of Toronto). Filmed in his lab, on campus and in Toronto neighborhoods on "how babies learn about faces about developing racial biases," with 6- and 9-month-olds. https://www.oise.utoronto.ca/about/news/07-02-20-oise-professor-kang-lee-makes-cameo-appearance-netflix-series **[solid]**
- Lit: 9- but not 6-month-olds pair own-race faces with happy music and other-race faces with sad music. Xiao et al. 2018, *Dev Sci* 21:e12537. **[solid]**
- Lit: 7-month-olds (N = 96) follow own-race adults' gaze more when it is only 50% reliable. Xiao et al. 2018, *Child Dev* 89:e229. **[solid]**
- HuffPost's Part 2 item "babies can tell monkeys apart but adults can't" (3-month-olds distinguish individual monkey faces; by one year it is pruned) matches the classic: 6-month-olds discriminate monkey faces, 9-month-olds and adults do not. Pascalis, de Haan and Nelson 2002, *Science* 296:1321. **[solid]**
- goodto.com quotes Lee on language: babies can learn several languages at once; by about one year unused pathways are pruned and only native sounds remain. **[solid, secondary]**
- **Rebecca Saxe** (MIT). Put her infant son Percy, awake, in an MRI scanner adapted to protect his hearing and showed him happy and sad faces with scenes as baseline. https://cbmm.mit.edu/news-events/news/5-mind-blowing-things-we-learned-netflixs-babies-part-two-huffington-post **[solid]**
- "The biggest surprise was the similarity between baby and grown-up brains"; face and scene regions were in the same places; babies' face areas also light up for people they love. **[solid, HuffPost]**
- Lit: Deen et al. 2017, *Nat Commun* 8:13995 — 17 infants scanned, 9 usable, aged 4–6 months; face- and scene-preferring regions already in adult locations. **[solid]**
- Lit: a custom 32-channel infant coil shows face-, scene- and body-selective responses in 2–9-month-olds. Kosakowski et al. 2022, *Curr Biol* 32:265. **[solid]**
- Saxe first scanned her elder son Arthur at about one month in 2015, lying beside him in the scanner; ~2-hour sessions yield ~10 minutes of usable data. https://www.quantamagazine.org/infant-brains-reveal-how-the-mind-gets-built-20170110 **[solid]**
- Placement of Saxe's segment in this episode is probable (three studies; "what babies know"), not confirmed. **[unverified placement]**

### Ep 8 "Movement"
Netflix: "From curiosity to understanding: researchers unveil the surprising ways that infants learn how to anticipate and respond to the movements of others."

- Scientists: **not identifiable** from any accessible source; no university release, review or credit list names them, and IMDb credits returned 403. **[unverified roster]**
- Likely underlying literature: 12-month-olds, like adults, shift gaze proactively to the goal of another person's reach; 6-month-olds do not. Falck-Ytter, Gredebäck and von Hofsten 2006, *Nat Neurosci* 9:878. **[solid lit; attribution unknown]**
- "Sticky mittens": 3-month-olds given Velcro mittens that let them grab learn to read others' reaches as goal-directed. Sommerville, Woodward and Needham 2005, *Cognition* 96:B1. **[solid lit; attribution unknown]**
- Infants encode a reach as goal-directed by 6 months (Woodward 1998, *Cognition* 69:1). **[solid lit; attribution unknown]**

### Ep 9 "Senses"
Netflix: "Several experts make sense of how babies touch, taste and see as they embark on a sensory journey that will shape the rest of their lives."

- **Julie Mennella** (Monell Chemical Senses Center) is named in Part 2 coverage on food preferences; placement in "Senses" (taste) is inferred. https://www.goodto.com/entertainment/netflix-babies-everything-to-know-529458 **[appearance solid; placement inferred]**
- Lit: 46 pregnant women drank 300 mL carrot juice four days a week for three weeks in late pregnancy or early lactation; at ~5.7 months their infants made fewer negative faces to carrot cereal. Mennella, Jagnow and Beauchamp 2001, *Pediatrics* 107:e88. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1351272/ **[solid]**
- Lit: garlic flavors breast milk within two hours and babies attach longer and suck more. Mennella and Beauchamp 1991, *Pediatrics* 88:737. **[solid]**
- A reviewer was "surprised by how limited babies' vision is at birth." https://stephanieanneauthor.ca/netflix-pick-of-the-week-babies-part-2/ **[solid, secondary]**
- Touch and vision scientists: **unidentified**; Saxe's face-region work may sit here instead of Ep 7. **[unverified roster]**

### Ep 10 "Relationships"
Netflix: "A coy smile, a puppet show and a pointed finger lead to discoveries in how babies get along with others using humor, morality and shared experiences."

- Coy smile: **Vasudevi (Vasu) Reddy** (University of Portsmouth), named in Part 2 coverage: babies use teasing, laughter and smiling to connect with adults. **[appearance solid; placement inferred from "coy smile"]**
- Lit: coy smiles (smiling with gaze aversion and an arm to the face) in 2–3-month-olds meeting an adult or their mirror image. Reddy 2000, *Dev Sci* 3:186, "Coyness in early infancy." **[solid]**
- Lit: "infant clowns" and teasing from ~8 months (Reddy 1991/2001); book *How Infants Know Minds* (2008). **[solid]**
- Puppet show: **Kiley Hamlin** (UBC Centre for Infant Cognition). Infants watch a character helped or hindered and are offered both; "babies seem to respond in ways that show greater preference for helpers over harmers." https://ubyssey.ca/science/netflix-babies **[solid]**
- UBC: her "developmental research and her personal journey of discovery" feature in "Relationships"; she is now following babies from hours after birth to see whether early preferences predict later moral judgment. https://psych.ubc.ca/?p=24682 **[solid]**
- Lit: Hamlin, Wynn and Bloom 2007, *Nature* 450:557 — 6- and 10-month-olds reach for the helper over the hinderer. https://doi.org/10.1038/nature06288 **[solid paper; exact counts unverified]**
- Lit: 3-month-olds by looking time — 10 of 12 preferred helper over hinderer, and 10 of 12 neutral over hinderer, a "negativity bias." Hamlin, Wynn and Bloom 2010, *Dev Sci* 13:923. **[solid, small n]**
- Lit: 8-month-olds prefer a puppet that acts badly toward an antisocial puppet; 21-month-olds hand treats to good puppets and take them from bad ones. Hamlin et al. 2011, *PNAS* 108:19931. **[solid]**
- Lit: "Not like me = bad" — 200 nine- and 14-month-olds choose graham crackers or green beans, then favor the puppet who harms the opposite-taste puppet. Hamlin et al. 2013, *Psychol Sci*. **[solid]**
- Replication status: the climber's "bounce" drives choices (Scarf et al. 2012, *PLoS ONE* 7:e42698); box-paradigm replications found 62.5% and 50% helper choice (Salvadori et al. 2015). **[contested]**
- ManyBabies 4: 1,018 infants tested, 567 included (5.5–10.5 months), 37 labs on five continents — 49.3% chose the helper. Lucca et al. 2025, *Dev Sci* 28:e13581. **[solid replication; original effect now contested]**
- Pointed finger: **Malinda Carpenter** (University of St Andrews, Baby and Child "ABC" Lab). Netflix "came to St Andrews to film the lab"; Wikipedia: "series 2 episode 4 Relationships... featured Carpenter's team's studies." https://developmentlab.wp.st-andrews.ac.uk/netflix-babies/ **[solid]**
- Lit: joint attention, gaze following and pointing emerge between 9 and 15 months in 24 infants followed monthly. Carpenter, Nagell and Tomasello 1998, *Monographs SRCD* 63(4). **[solid]**
- Lit: 12-month-olds point declaratively to share attention and interest, and stop pointing when the adult ignores the referent. Liszkowski et al. 2004, *Dev Sci* 7:297. **[solid]**

### Ep 11 "Nature and Nurture"
Netflix: "Resolving the debate of nature versus nurture, three scientists show how genetics and environment work together as infants discover who they are."

- **Maria (Masha) Gartstein** (Washington State University; co-author of the Infant Behavior Questionnaire-Revised). WSU release names the episode: babies show lower fear at 6 months when parents read and respond to their cues. https://news.wsu.edu/news/2020/06/17/wsu-psychologist-appear-netflix-series-babies-friday/ **[solid]**
- Lit: 148 mother–infant dyads; play observed at 4 months; fear and positive affect measured at 6, 8, 10 and 12 months; maternal responsiveness predicted lower observed fear. Gartstein, Hancock and Iverson 2018, *Child Dev* 89:1519. **[solid]**
- **Philippe Rochat** (Emory Infant Lab) — self-awareness. His lab's media page lists this episode (page would not load; attribution via search snippet). https://scholarblogs.emory.edu/rochatlab/media-coverage/ **[appearance probable]**
- Likely content: mirror self-recognition emerges ~18–24 months, ~65% passing the rouge test at 20–24 months (Amsterdam 1972, *Dev Psychobiol* 5:297). **[solid lit; segment unverified]**
- Rochat's "five levels of self-awareness" (Rochat 2003, *Conscious Cogn* 12:717); 4- vs 9-month-olds tell self from other in a mirror (Rochat and Striano 2002, *Child Dev* 73:35). **[solid lit; segment unverified]**
- Third scientist: **unidentified**. **[unverified]**

### Ep 12 "Toddlers"
Netflix: "Researchers reveal the social skills that toddlers naturally possess, abilities that ultimately separate humans from all other species."

- **Felix Warneken** (University of Michigan; formerly MPI Leipzig and Harvard). On camera he "clumsily knocks a cup off a table" and a toddler walks over to hand it back. https://michigantoday.umich.edu/2020/07/23/children-show-altruism-at-a-young-age **[solid]**
- Lit: Warneken and Tomasello 2006, *Science* 311:1301 — 24 eighteen-month-olds helped in 10 situations (out-of-reach objects, cabinets, wrong results); three human-raised chimpanzees helped only with reaching. **[solid; N via Michigan release]**
- MPG release: "virtually all children helped at least once," and 84% of helps came within 10 seconds, before eye contact. https://www.mpg.de/532681/pressRelease20060302 **[solid]**
- Rewards backfire: 20-month-olds given a toy for helping later helped less than those praised or given nothing. Warneken and Tomasello 2008, *Dev Psychol* 44:1785. **[solid]**
- **Laura Schulz** (MIT Early Childhood Cognition Lab): CBMM confirms the series "features CBMM PI Laura Schulz and her research with toddlers." **[appearance solid]**
- Most likely study: 15-month-olds who watch an adult struggle before succeeding try harder on a novel toy (N = 262 across three experiments). Leonard, Lee and Schulz 2017, *Science* 357:1290. **[study inferred]**
- Reviews mention toddlers understanding desires and "other people's minds and needs" (cf. Repacholi and Gopnik 1997, *Dev Psychol* 33:12 — 18-month-olds hand over the broccoli the adult prefers; 14-month-olds hand over crackers). **[solid lit; attribution unknown]**
- Third scientist: **unidentified**. **[unverified]**

---

## 2. Literature greatest hits, by theme

### Sleep
- Newborns (0–2 months) sleep a mean 14.6 h/day (range 9.3–20.0); 3 months 13.6; 6 months 12.9; 12 months 12.9; 1–2 years 12.6. Galland et al. 2012, *Sleep Med Rev* (34 studies). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21784676/ **[solid]**
- Infants in predominantly Asian countries sleep about an hour less across childhood. Galland 2012. **[solid]**
- Half of newborn sleep is REM (about a third of the whole day); REM falls to ~20–25% by age 3; at 30 weeks' gestation it is ~80%. Roffwarg, Muzio and Dement 1966, *Science* 152:604. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.152.3722.604 **[solid]**
- Newborns fall asleep straight into REM: ~60% of sleep onsets at 3 weeks vs 20% at 6 months; adults enter via NREM. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9602694/ **[solid]**
- Sleep spindles appear at ~2 months; K-complexes by ~6 months (Jenni et al. 2004). **[solid]**
- Circadian rhythms switch on after ~2 months (Rivkees 2003, *Pediatrics* 112:373). **[solid]**
- Night wakings: 0–3.4 per night at 0–2 months, 0–2.5 at 1–2 years; the longest sleep stretch grows ~39 minutes per month in the first half-year. Galland 2012. **[solid]**
- "Sleeping through" is a minority skill: at 6 months 62.4% manage 6 unbroken hours and 43.0% manage 8; at 12 months 72.1% and 56.6%; no link to later development. Pennestri et al. 2018, *Pediatrics* 142:e20174330 (388 infants). **[solid]**
- Sleep regressions at 4/8/12 months: no peer-reviewed support for discrete universal regressions; app data from 841 children and 156,989 logged sessions show smooth consolidation. Mindell et al. 2016. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26987761/ **[pop]**
- Naps consolidate preschool memory by ~10%; spindle density predicts the gain (r = 0.65). Kurdziel, Duclos and Spencer 2013, *PNAS*. **[solid]**
- Sleep's job flips at ~2.4 years from neural reorganization (REM-heavy) to repair, in a model fit to 60+ datasets. Cao et al. 2020, *Sci Adv* 6:eaba0398. **[solid, model-based]**
- Blumberg's informal estimate: an infant's limbs twitch "at least 40,000 times a day." https://now.uiowa.edu/node/37661 **[informal estimate]**
- Sleep training in 43 infants: faster sleep onset, lower cortisol, no attachment or behavior differences at 12 months (Gradisar et al. 2016, *Pediatrics*); 5-year follow-up of 326 children found no harms and no benefits (Price et al. 2012, *Pediatrics*). **[solid, small samples]**

### Crying
- Crying rises to a peak around 6 weeks: Brazelton 1962, *Pediatrics* 29:579 (80 diaries, ~2¼ h/day through week 7). **[solid]**
- Meta-analysis of 8,690 infants: 117–133 minutes/day of fuss/cry in weeks 1–6, ~68 minutes at 10–12 weeks; individual range 30 minutes to over 5 hours. Wolke, Bilgin and Samara 2017, *J Pediatr* 185:55. **[solid]**
- Colic by country: Denmark 5.5% and Germany 6.7% at 3–4 weeks; UK 28% at 1–2 weeks, Canada 34.1% at 3–4 weeks, Italy 20.9% at 8–9 weeks. Wolke 2017. **[solid]**
- Colic fades fast: 17–25% in weeks 1–6, 11% at 8–9 weeks, 0.6% at 10–12 weeks. Wolke 2017. **[solid]**
- Wessel's rule of threes (1954, *Pediatrics* 14:421): crying more than 3 hours a day, 3 days a week, for 3 weeks; prevalence 1.5–40% depending on definition. **[definition solid; prevalence contested]**
- Shaken-baby hospitalizations track the crying curve, peaking at 10–13 weeks (273 California cases); basis of Barr's "Period of PURPLE Crying." Barr, Trent and Cross 2006, *Child Abuse Negl* 30:7. **[solid]**
- Newborns cry in their mother tongue: 30 French and 30 German 2–5-day-olds, 1,254 cries — French cries rise in pitch, German cries fall. Mampe et al. 2009, *Curr Biol* 19:1994. **[solid, single study]**
- Newborns do make tears: term newborns' basal tear production (Schirmer 9.2 mm) is adult-like; preterm 3.5 mm. Isenberg et al. 1998, *Arch Ophthalmol* 116:773; Toker 2002. The "no tears until 1–3 months" line is folklore. **[pop claim contradicted]**
- Contagious crying: newborns cry to another newborn's cry but not to a recording of their own. Martin and Clark 1982, *Dev Psychol* 18:3. **[solid]**

### Feeding
- Day-one colostrum totals about 15 mL — three teaspoons: 1.5 ± 1.1 g per feed, 15 ± 11 g/day in 90 newborns. Santoro et al. 2010, *J Pediatr* 156:29. **[solid]**
- Newborn stomach capacity is ~20 mL; the viral cherry/walnut/egg poster traces to early-1900s data of undetermined accuracy. Bergman 2013, *Acta Paediatr* 102:773; Spangler et al. 2008, *J Hum Lact* 24:199. **[contested / pop]**
- Milk has a clock: melatonin undetectable by day, peaking near midnight (~47 pg/mL); cortisol peaks in the morning; fat peaks in the evening. Italianer et al. 2020, *Nutrients* 12:2328 (71 components). **[solid]**
- Milk from mothers of 31 febrile infants carried more leukocytes, macrophages and TNF-α than after recovery. Riskin et al. 2012, *Pediatr Res* 71:220. **[solid, small]**
- Taste at birth: sweet relaxes, sour purses, bitter gapes; salt is ignored until a preference switches on at ~4 months. Rosenstein and Oster 1988, *Child Dev* 59:1555; Beauchamp, Cowart and Moran 1986, *Dev Psychobiol* 19:17. **[solid]**
- "10,000 taste buds" is folklore; adult counts run ~2,000–8,000 (Miller and Reedy 1990, *Physiol Behav* 47:1213). **[contested]**
- Birth seeds the microbiome: vaginally born babies carry Lactobacillus and Prevotella like their mother's vagina; C-section babies carry Staphylococcus and Corynebacterium like skin. Dominguez-Bello et al. 2010, *PNAS* 107:11971 (10 newborns). **[solid, tiny n]**
- Vaginal seeding pilot: 18 infants, 4 swabbed with gauze incubated in the mother's vagina; partial restoration, no health outcomes. Dominguez-Bello et al. 2016, *Nat Med*. **[solid, pilot]**
- Baby Biome Study: Bacteroides abundant in 51% of vaginally born babies but low or absent in 99.6% of 282 C-section babies; C-section babies carried hospital Enterococcus and Klebsiella; differences largely gone by one year. Shao et al. 2019, *Nature* 574:117 (771 babies). **[solid]**
- Early peanut cuts allergy: 640 high-risk infants randomized at 4–11 months; allergy at 5 was 13.7% with avoidance vs 1.9% with consumption (86% relative reduction); 35.3% vs 10.6% in skin-test-positive infants. Du Toit et al. 2015, *NEJM* 372:803 (LEAP). **[solid]**
- Breastfeeding and IQ: 3,493 Brazilian adults — ≥12 months of breastfeeding predicted +3.76 IQ points and 341 reais/month more income at 30 (Victora et al. 2015, *Lancet Glob Health*); PROBIT's 17,046 Belarusian infants showed +7.5 verbal IQ at 6.5 years (Kramer 2008). **[contested]**
- Sibling comparisons erase 10 of 11 claimed breastfeeding benefits. Colen and Ramey 2014, *Soc Sci Med* 109:55. **[contested]**
- Skin-to-skin after birth makes breastfeeding at 1–4 months ~24% more likely (RR ≈ 1.24). Moore, Bergman, Anderson and Medley 2016, Cochrane CD003519. **[solid]**

### Movement
- Learning to walk: 12–19-month-olds average 2,368 steps and 17 falls per hour of free play and travel 701 m — "7.7 American football fields" — per hour. Adolph et al. 2012, *Psychol Sci* 23:1387 (151 infants). https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3591461/ **[solid]**
- The daily figures — ~14,000 steps, ~46 football fields, ~100 falls — are the authors' extrapolation (hourly rate × 6 waking hours). Same paper. **[solid as estimate]**
- Novice walkers travel farther and faster than expert crawlers with the same fall rate; efficiency without extra cost is why crawlers switch. Same paper. **[solid]**
- No transfer across postures: experienced 12-month crawlers never attempt a 90-cm cliff, but 10 of 16 (63%) 12-month novice walkers go over it. Kretch and Adolph 2013, *Child Dev* 84:226. **[solid]**
- Walkers attempt risky drop-offs 80% of the time at 3 cm beyond their ability; crawlers 10%. Same paper. **[solid]**
- WHO milestone windows (1st–99th percentile, months): sitting unsupported 3.8–9.2; hands-and-knees crawling 5.2–13.5; standing alone 6.9–16.9; walking alone 8.2–17.6. WHO MGRS 2006, *Acta Paediatr* Suppl 450:86 (816 children, 5 countries). **[solid]**
- 4.3% of children never crawl on hands and knees; ~90% hit five milestones in a common order. Same paper. **[solid]**
- Newborn stepping disappears by ~2–3 months because legs get heavy, not because the brain forgets: ankle weights abolish it, submerging the legs in water restores it. Thelen, Fisher and Ridley-Johnson 1984, *Infant Behav Dev* 7:479. **[solid]**
- 7-month-olds who show no stepping on the floor step on a moving treadmill. Thelen 1986, *Child Dev* 57:1498. **[solid]**
- Daily practice of the stepping reflex kept it going to 8 weeks and brought earlier walking. Zelazo, Zelazo and Kolb 1972, *Science* 176:314. **[solid; walking ages not retrieved]**
- Palmar grasp: Louis Robinson (1891) hung "upward of sixty" newborns from a stick; nearly all held ≥10 seconds, record 2 min 35 s by a 3-week-old. *Popular Science Monthly*, March 1892. **[solid as Victorian report]**
- Reflex exit dates (StatPearls): Moro by ~6 months; palmar grasp ~6 months; rooting fades after 1 month; stepping ~2–3 months; asymmetric tonic neck ~3–6 months; Babinski 12–24 months. **[solid ranges]**
- Pincer grasp: CDC lists "picks things up between thumb and pointer finger" at 12 months; clinical sources say 9–10 months. **[solid]**
- Back to Sleep did not delay walking: 351 infants; prone sleepers rolled, sat and crawled earlier but walked at the same age. Davis et al. 1998, *Pediatrics* 102:1135. **[solid]**
- SIDS fell more than 50% from 1994 to 2016 after Back to Sleep (NICHD). **[solid]**
- Culture: 64 rural Kenyan (Kipsigis) babies sat and walked early because adults deliberately trained them (Super 1976, *Dev Med Child Neurol* 18:561); Hopi cradleboards did not delay walking (Dennis and Dennis 1940). **[solid]**
- Baby walkers: each 24 hours in a walker delays walking ~3 days and standing ~4 days (Garrett 2002, *BMJ*); Canada banned them in April 2004, the first country to do so; US walker injuries fell from 21,000 (1990) to 3,200 (2003). **[solid]**
- Tummy time: 16 studies, 4,237 infants; modest gross-motor and flat-head benefits, almost all questionnaire-based. Hewitt et al. 2020, *Pediatrics* 145:e20192168. **[solid, weak base]**

### Language
- Perceptual narrowing: 11 of 12 English-learning 6–8-month-olds discriminated Hindi retroflex/dental contrasts; 8 of 12 at 8–10 months; 2 of 10 at 10–12 months; only 3 of 10 English adults. Werker and Tees 1984, *Infant Behav Dev* 7:49. **[solid]**
- Longitudinally, all 6 infants passed at 6–8 months and none of the 6 at 10–12 months. Same paper. **[solid]**
- Perceptual magnet by 6 months: 32 Swedish and 32 American infants each treated their own language's vowel prototype as a magnet. Kuhl et al. 1992, *Science* 255:606. **[solid]**
- Social gating: 9-month-old Americans learned Mandarin contrasts from live tutors in 12 sessions (~5 hours; 65.7% vs 56.7%) but learned nothing from the same tutors on DVD or audio. Kuhl, Tsao and Liu 2003, *PNAS* 100:9096. **[solid]**
- Statistical learning: 8-month-olds segment words from 2 minutes of monotone nonsense. Saffran, Aslin and Newport 1996, *Science* 274:1926. **[solid]**
- Parentese pays: 11–14-month-olds who heard the most one-on-one parentese had 433 words at 24 months vs 169 for those who heard least. Ramírez-Esparza, García-Sierra and Kuhl 2014, *Dev Sci* 17:880. **[solid]**
- Coaching parents in parentese at 6, 10 and 14 months raised 18-month vocabulary (~100 vs ~60 words; d = 0.53). Ferjan Ramírez, Lytle and Kuhl 2020, *PNAS* 117:3484. **[solid]**
- The 30-million-word gap: Hart and Risley 1995 recorded 42 families one hour a month for 2.5 years; 2,153 vs 616 words per hour by SES, extrapolated to 45M vs 13M words by age 4. https://www.aft.org/ae/spring2003/hart_risley **[solid as reported]**
- Not reproduced: counting all caregivers and overheard speech erases much of the gap (Sperry, Sperry and Miller 2019, *Child Dev* 90:1303); LENA recordings of 329 families (49,765 hours) put the gap nearer 4 million words by age 4 (Gilkerson et al. 2017). **[contested]**
- Processing speed: by 24 months lower-SES toddlers were ~6 months behind in real-time word recognition. Fernald, Marchman and Weisleder 2013, *Dev Sci* 16:234. **[solid]**
- Baby videos: each daily hour of baby DVDs went with 6–8 fewer understood words at 8–16 months. Zimmerman, Christakis and Meltzoff 2007, *J Pediatr* 151:364. **[solid, correlational]**
- A month of Baby Einstein taught 12–18-month-olds no new words; parents who liked the DVD overestimated learning. DeLoache et al. 2010, *Psychol Sci* 21:1570. **[solid]**
- Disney dropped "educational" in 2006 after an FTC complaint and in October 2009 refunded $15.99 per DVD, up to four per household. https://www.washington.edu/news/2009/10/29/disney-offers-refunds-for-baby-einstein-products/ **[solid]**
- Milestones: canonical babbling ~6 months; first words ~8–14 months; ~50 words at 18 months and 200–300 at 24 months are standard CDI norms (Fenson et al. 1994). **[solid range; medians not re-verified]**
- The "vocabulary spurt" is rare: only 5 of 38 children fit a spurt curve better than steady acceleration. Ganger and Brent 2004, *Dev Psychol*. **[contested as universal stage]**
- Pointing emerges ~12 months and predicts later language (meta-analytic r = .52 concurrent; Colonnesi et al. 2010, *Dev Rev*); declarative pointing is the human-specific part (Tomasello, Carpenter and Liszkowski 2007, *Child Dev* 78:705). **[solid; effect size contested]**
- Bilingual 7-month-olds switch anticipatory looks when a rule changes; monolinguals don't (Kovács and Mehler 2009, *PNAS*); a 2023 multi-lab replication questions robustness. **[contested]**
- Newborns of Tagalog–English mothers prefer both languages equally and still tell them apart. Byers-Heinlein, Burns and Werker 2010, *Psychol Sci* 21:343. **[solid]**
- Baby sign language teaches signs, not language: 40 infants followed 8–20 months, no vocabulary advantage. Kirk et al. 2013, *Child Dev* 84:574. **[solid]**
- Fast mapping: preschoolers who heard "the chromium tray, not the red one" once remembered the color word a week later. Carey and Bartlett 1978. **[solid]**
- Vowels learned in utero: 80 newborns aged 7–75 hours in Sweden and the US sucked more for non-native vowels. Moon, Lagercrantz and Kuhl 2013, *Acta Paediatr* 102:156. **[solid]**

### Cognition
- Drawbridge: 3.5–4.5-month-olds look longer when a screen rotates "through" a hidden box — object permanence long before Piaget's 8–12 months. Baillargeon 1987, *Dev Psychol* 23:655; Baillargeon, Spelke and Wasserman 1985, *Cognition* 20:191. **[solid]**
- Number: 6-month-olds tell 8 from 16 dots but not 8 from 12 (Xu and Spelke 2000, *Cognition* 74:B1); 9-month-olds manage 8 vs 12 (Lipton and Spelke 2003, *Psychol Sci*). **[solid]**
- Newborns 49 hours old match 4 vs 12 syllables to 4 vs 12 objects, 15 of 16 of them. Izard et al. 2009, *PNAS* 106:10382. **[solid]**
- Infant arithmetic: 5-month-olds look longer when 1 + 1 doll yields 1. Wynn 1992, *Nature* 358:749. **[solid]**
- Intuitive statistics: 8-month-olds look longer (9.9 s vs 7.5 s) when 4 red balls are drawn from a box of 70 white and 5 red — unless the balls come from the experimenter's pocket. Xu and Garcia 2008, *PNAS* 105:5012. **[solid]**
- 15-month-olds reason from samples: 80% vs 33% tried the other toy depending on which box the squeaky balls came from. Gweon, Tenenbaum and Schulz 2010, *PNAS* 107:9066. **[solid]**
- Goldilocks effect: 7–8-month-olds look away from events that are too predictable and from events that are too surprising, a U-shape in 72 infants. Kidd, Piantadosi and Aslin 2012, *PLoS ONE* 7:e36399. **[solid]**
- Little scientists: 110 eleven-month-olds who saw a ball pass through a wall then banged the ball; those who saw it float dropped it. Stahl and Feigenson 2015, *Science* 348:91. **[solid]**
- Fantz 1963: infants under 5 days old look longer at black-and-white patterns than plain color — the birth of the looking-time method. *Science* 140:296. **[solid]**
- Memory: 9-month-olds imitate after 24 hours, 14-month-olds after a week (Meltzoff 1988); adults' earliest memories date to ~3–3.5 years in a corpus of over 11,000 memories (Rubin 2000, *Memory* 8:265). **[solid]**
- 5–7-year-olds recall over 60% of events discussed at age 3; 8–9-year-olds under 40%. Bauer and Larkina 2014, *Memory* 22:907. **[solid]**
- Mirror self-recognition: ~65% at 20–24 months (Amsterdam 1972); but 2 of 82 Kenyan children, 0% in Fiji (Broesch et al. 2011) and 7% of ni-Vanuatu vs 68% of Canadian toddlers (Cebioğlu and Broesch 2021). **[solid; interpretation contested]**
- Neonatal imitation: Meltzoff and Moore 1977 (*Science*, 6 infants aged 12–21 days) vs 106 newborns tested at 1, 3, 6 and 9 weeks with 11 models who imitated nothing. Oostenbroek et al. 2016, *Curr Biol* 26:1334. **[contested, leaning debunked]**
- Cross-modal: 1-month-olds looked longer at the pacifier shape they had sucked unseen (Meltzoff and Borton 1979, *Nature* 282:403), later not replicated; shapes bias color preference in 2–3-month-olds but not 8-month-olds (Wagner and Dobkins 2011, *Psychol Sci* 22:1067). **[contested / solid]**
- Persistence is contagious: 15-month-olds who watched an adult struggle tried harder (Leonard, Lee and Schulz 2017, *Science*; N = 262). **[solid]**
- Marshmallow test: the effect on age-15 achievement is about half the original in a ~900-child replication, and most of it comes from waiting ≥20 seconds. Watts, Duncan and Quan 2018, *Psychol Sci*. **[contested original]**
- Perceptual narrowing for faces: 6-month-olds discriminate individual monkey faces; 9-month-olds and adults cannot. Pascalis, de Haan and Nelson 2002, *Science* 296:1321. **[solid]**
- Blicket detector: 2–4-year-olds categorize objects by a novel causal power. Gopnik and Sobel 2000, *Child Dev* 71:1205. **[solid]**

### Social
- Oxytocin: 80 couples, fathers' levels equal to mothers' (Gordon et al. 2010); gay primary-caregiving fathers' amygdalae respond like mothers' (Abraham et al. 2014, *PNAS*, 89 parents). **[solid]**
- Fathers' testosterone drops: 624 Filipino men followed 4.5 years; new fathers' morning T fell 26% and evening T 34% (vs 12%/14% in non-fathers); ≥3 hours of daily childcare predicted the lowest levels. Gettler et al. 2011, *PNAS* 108:16194. **[solid]**
- Mothers' brains remodel: gray-matter loss in social-cognition regions lasting ≥2 years, enough to classify every woman as pregnant or not (25 mothers vs 20 controls); no change in fathers. Hoekzema et al. 2017, *Nat Neurosci*. **[solid]**
- Still face: three phases (play, ~2 minutes of blank face, reunion); the YouTube demo has over 6 million views. **[solid]**
- The popular "matched only ~30% of the time, mismatches repaired in seconds" is Tronick's own summary of Tronick and Cohn 1989 (54 dyads), not a figure in a primary abstract. **[contested number]**
- Harlow's rhesus infants spent ~17–18 h/day on the cloth mother and under an hour on the wire mother that fed them. Harlow and Zimmermann 1959, *Science* 130:421. **[solid; hours via secondary]**
- Strange Situation: 8 episodes of ~3 minutes, ~21 minutes total, at 12–18 months. Ainsworth, Blehar, Waters and Wall 1978. **[solid]**
- Cross-cultural meta-analysis of 32 samples (~1,990 infants): 65% secure, 21% avoidant, 14% resistant; within-country variation exceeds between-country. van IJzendoorn and Kroonenberg 1988, *Child Dev*. **[solid]**
- Disorganized attachment ~15% in low-risk samples, 48% in maltreated children. van IJzendoorn, Schuengel and Bakermans-Kranenburg 1999. **[solid]**
- Social smile ~2 months, chuckle ~4 months, laugh ~6 months (CDC milestones); stranger and separation wariness ~8–9 months. **[solid]**
- Social referencing: at a 30-cm visual-cliff drop, 74% of 12-month-olds crossed when mother smiled and none when she looked afraid. Sorce, Emde, Campos and Klinnert 1985, *Dev Psychol* 21:195. **[solid]**
- Eye contact: 2–5-day-olds looked 106.8 s at direct-gaze faces vs 63.7 s at averted gaze (17 newborns). Farroni et al. 2002, *PNAS* 99:9602. **[solid]**
- Helping: 18-month-olds help within seconds, before eye contact (Warneken and Tomasello 2006); a toy reward makes 20-month-olds help less (2008). **[solid]**
- Fairness: 15-month-olds look longer at an unequal cracker split (10.57 s vs 8.07 s); the ones who share their favorite toy are the ones most bothered by unfairness. Schmidt and Sommerville 2011, *PLoS ONE* 6:e23223. **[solid]**
- 19-month-olds expect two giraffes to get one toy each; 21-month-olds expect reward to go only to the one who worked. Sloane, Baillargeon and Premack 2012, *Psychol Sci*. **[solid]**
- In-group taste: after choosing a food, 87.5% of infants chose the puppet who liked the same food (single-trial design). Mahajan and Wynn 2012, *Cognition* 124:227. **[contested]**
- Helper/hinderer: ManyBabies 4 (567 infants, 37 labs) found 49.3% helper choice. **[contested]**
- Bucharest Early Intervention Project: 136 institutionalized toddlers randomized to foster care or care-as-usual; foster-care children had higher cognitive scores at 42 and 54 months, most for those placed before ~24 months. Nelson et al. 2007, *Science* 318:1937. **[solid]**
- At age 8, BEIP IQ was 81.5 (foster) vs 76.2 (institution), p = .07; children placed before 26 months fared best. Fox et al. 2011, *JCPP*. **[solid]**
- Kangaroo care: immediate skin-to-skin for 3,211 infants weighing 1.0–1.8 kg cut 28-day mortality from 15.7% to 12.0% (RR 0.75); 16.9 vs 1.5 h/day of contact; trial stopped early. WHO iKMC 2021, *NEJM* 384:2028. **[solid]**
- Twenty years on, Colombian kangaroo-care babies had less school absence and aggression and a larger left caudate (264 analysed). Charpak et al. 2017, *Pediatrics* 139:e20162063. **[solid]**
- Aka fathers (Central Africa) hold infants 22% of in-camp time and are within arm's reach 51.5% of the day — "at least five times more than fathers in any other human population" (Hewlett). **[solid]**
- Co-sleeping: 25% of US families always or almost always bed-shared and 42% sometimes (NCHS 1991–99); 93% in an Indian sample (Bharti 2006). **[solid; global % contested]**
- Parallel play is typical from ~24–30 months (Parten 1932). **[solid textbook]**
- Mothers of 11-month-old girls underestimated, mothers of boy

Research baby-science facts for lyrics final report11:54 PM

The fact sheet is written: /Users/jalex/projects/clawdpop/songs/baby-facts/FACTS.md — 482 lines, ~11,900 words, 354 flagged facts, 30 hooks, 47 URLs. The only unflagged bullets are the closing "could not verify" list, by design.

Summary of the most lyrically useful findings

  1. The best source turned out to be the episodes themselves: Netflix posted five Part 1 episodes on YouTube, so Love, Crawling, First Words, Sleep and First Steps are documented from the actual audio — exact numbers, quotes and family names (Willow, Pascoe, Dakota, Eric, Nelson, Mateo, twins Kiki/Yaya and Eloisa/Augusto).
  2. Lampl (Ep 3) is the richest vein: a baby measured 62.7 cm, then 64.5 cm the next day ("I'm really terrible at this"); 59.8, 59.8, 59.8, 60.8 cm; growth charts are "connect-the-dots" from 882 children; the 1992 Science paper (31 infants: 0.5–2.5 cm bursts, 2–63 days of stasis, 90–95% of infancy growth-free) made the NYT front page and got her mocked at a conference.
  3. Feldman (Ep 1): 80 couples, fathers' oxytocin "identical" to mothers'; 48 gay couples, primary-caregiving dads' amygdalae light up like mothers'; dad's response otherwise "about a quarter"; "Fatherhood is biological. It's as deep as motherhood." Tronick's professor told him "This emotion stuff is not what you want to be doing."
  4. Saffran (Ep 4): "pabeecoo," 2 minutes, 45 repetitions; her mother's aphasic patient who kept only the noun "shopping center." Jarvis: ex-dancer, "three birds and five mammals" are vocal learners, SRGAP2 extra copy.
  5. Sleep (Ep 5): Wallisch's 6 million app events from ~1,000 babies (1.5M sleeps, 2.5M feeds, 1.5M diapers) — explicitly unpublished; Blumberg's twitch-as-sonar-ping and "we study the things we're bad at"; Spencer's "I'm a lark" and spindles.
  6. First Steps (Ep 6): Dominici's two newborn muscle patterns plus two toddler ones, shared with rat, cat, macaque and guineafowl; Cowgill's 130 lb of gear through 15 museums, 600 children's bones back to 250,000 years, and Reviewer 2's "Children's bones don't respond to exercise"; Walle's 374 words in five minutes and the finding that walkers and crawlers hear the same number of words.
  7. Adolph: 2,368 steps and 17 falls per hour (the 701 m / 7.7 football fields is per hour; ~14,000 steps and ~46 fields per day are extrapolations); "crawling isn't mandatory"; Grant crawled straight over the 90-degree drop.
  8. Killer replication jokes: ManyBabies 4 (567 infants, 37 labs) found 49.3% helper preference for Hamlin's puppet show; Wonder Weeks tested on 4 infants by a PhD student, Plooij lost his job in Jan 1998; Mozart effect was 36 college students for ~10 minutes while Georgia spent $105k on CDs; Baby Einstein refunds $15.99 per DVD; 106 newborns imitated nothing.
  9. Solid numeric gems: French cries rise, German cries fall (1,254 cries); only 43% of 6-month-olds sleep 8 hours; half of newborn sleep is REM; colostrum is three teaspoons on day one; milk leukocytes jump to 94% during infection; 1.49 million Holsteins favor heifers; 22 of 30 newborns pick the unwashed breast; newborn focus at 19 cm.
  10. Corrections to the brief: Feldman's 2014 paper had 48 gay fathers, not 48 couples (the show says couples); newborn sleep is a meta-analytic 14.6 h, not 16–18; teething tops out at 37.8 °C; "300 bones" and "1 million synapses/second" have no primary source.

What I could not verify

  • Part 2 rosters are incomplete: Ep 8 "Movement" has no identifiable scientists; Ep 9 "Senses" has only Mennella (inferred) and no touch/vision names; the third scientists in Eps 11 and 12 are unknown. IMDb credits were blocked (403) and no press release names them. Confirmed Part 2: Hespos, Kang Lee, Saxe (Ep 7, Saxe's placement probable); Reddy, Hamlin, Malinda Carpenter (Ep 10); Gartstein, Rochat (Ep 11); Warneken, Schulz (Ep 12).
  • Not in the series at all (per every accessible source): Kuhl, Dominguez-Bello, Charles Nelson, Spelke, Fei Xu, Tomasello, Fessler, Kidd.
  • On-air glosses to avoid singing as fact: Georgieff's "shift world IQ by ten points," Feldman's "forever" and "a quarter," Barbu-Roth's head as "one third of body weight."
  • The shared WebSearch budget ran out midway (three parallel researchers plus my own searches), so the back half relied on direct fetches of known URLs, NCBI E-utilities and YouTube subtitles; the Rifkin-Graboi, Fenton/Wallisch and Spencer-infant results are described on air but not matched to published papers.
